The greater the observed evil, the stronger this evidence against God, unless one assigns a mitigating explanation (for example, a theodicy might claim $P(Evil \mid God)$ isn’t as low as it appears because God might have reasons to allow evil). Our neutral modeling will, however, treat EV as a powerful piece of contrary evidence the model must account for.* BC – Biological Complexity: This variable pertains to the complexity and apparent design of biological organisms. The argument from biological design (dating back to William Paley’s watchmaker analogy) claims that intricate structures in living things (the eye, the bacterial flagellum, DNA information content, etc.) bespeak an intelligent designer. In Paley’s classic formulation, just as the purposeful arrangement of parts in a watch implies a watchmaker, so too the exquisite complexity of living organisms implies a Creator. However, modern science offers Darwinian evolution as an alternative explanation: natural selection can produce complexity without a guiding intelligence. By now, “biological complexity” as evidence is a double-edged sword: on one hand, some theists (especially in the Intelligent Design movement) argue that certain forms of complexity are irreducibly complex or wildly improbable to have arisen by unguided evolution alone (e.g., Behe’s claim that the bacterial flagellum’s molecular machine could not evolve in incremental steps) . Dembski has argued that the “information” in DNA is so improbable it points to design . These claims, if accepted, mean $P(\text{complex life} \mid \neg God)$ is extremely low, giving a large evidential boost to the God hypothesis . On the other hand, the mainstream scientific consensus, as represented by biologists like Kenneth Miller, is that known evolutionary mechanisms are fully capable of explaining biological complexity . If one accepts evolutionary theory’s adequacy, then the existence of complex life is not surprising under atheism (indeed, it’s expected given billions of years of evolution), and thus $P(\text{complex organisms} \mid \neg God)$ could be high. In that case, BC doesn’t strongly favor God; some might even argue aspects of biology (like suboptimal designs, vestigial organs, the cruelty of predation) are more likely if there is no designer. For our model’s purposes, BC will be included because it featured in earlier chapters (Chapter 7 on design in biology). We can assign it a weight reflecting one’s stance: e.g., a person convinced by Intelligent Design would treat BC as significant positive evidence (increasing God’s probability), whereas someone convinced by evolutionary biology would assign it little to no pro-theistic weight (or even treat it as slight counter-evidence in line with the argument that evolution is “blind” and often cruel). The model allows either: one can adjust $\beta_{\text{BC}}$ accordingly. We will illustrate with a moderate assumption that biological complexity offers some evidence of a designer (since the origin of life and the existence of the evolutionary process itself might still require an explanation), but not as much as cosmological fine-tuning does.* QM – Quantum Indicators: This variable captures various puzzling phenomena or interpretations in quantum physics that have been co-opted into discussions about God or metaphysics. Quantum mechanics (QM) introduced indeterminacy, observer effect, and non-locality into physics. Some argue that these peculiar features hint at consciousness or a non-material aspect underlying reality. For instance, certain interpretations of QM (like Wigner’s interpretation) suggest that a conscious observer is needed to “collapse” the quantum wavefunction. A bold extrapolation of this idea is that universal or global consciousness is fundamental to reality – and some have equated this global mind with God. In fact, a recent argument in the journal Religions claimed that “global consciousness has all and only the essential properties of God” and that quantum reality’s dependence on observers implies the physical world depends on God’s consciousness . This is a speculative and controversial line of reasoning, but it exemplifies how QM can be linked to theism: if consciousness (potentially divine) is a necessary ingredient in the existence of the universe (as some interpretations of quantum cosmology or the Anthropic Principle might suggest), then certain quantum phenomena could be seen as evidence of that. Additionally, one could note that quantum cosmology models of universe-from-nothing (à la vacuum fluctuations) still require a framework of laws (a “quantum vacuum” is not literally nothing ), so one might argue a transcendent cause is still needed – though that bleeds into the contingency argument (MN) category. On the flip side, others caution that invoking quantum mysteries is a God of the gaps move; quantum physics is simply a physical theory and doesn’t inherently favor theism. Some atheists even argue that quantum indeterminacy provides room for a purely naturalistic account of apparent design (through many-worlds or multiverse ideas). Given previous chapters (perhaps Chapter 8) touched on modern physics and metaphysics, we include QM as a variable. It might encode things like: does the need for an “observer” (in some interpretations) suggest a cosmic observer (God)? Are there quantum events (like alleged mind-over-matter experiments, or fine-tuned quantum vacuum energy) that point toward intelligence? Admittedly, this is the most conjectural variable of the eight. We will assign it only a small weight, indicating mild evidence one way or the other. If one is impressed by arguments such as those by Henry Stapp or Bruce Gordon (who link quantum measurement and theism), one might give QM a positive value. If one thinks quantum theory lends no support (or even undermines classical arguments by allowing a self-contained universe), one might set it to zero or negative. The flexibility of the model allows exploration of both views.* MN – Metaphysical Necessity: This final variable corresponds to arguments that posit God as a metaphysically necessary being or as the ultimate explanation for existence itself. One classical form is the cosmological argument from contingency (Leibniz’s argument): “Why is there something rather than nothing?” – everything contingent needs an explanation, and ultimately one must arrive at a necessary being (one that cannot not exist) as the source of all contingent reality. If such a being is identified with God, then the very existence of a contingent universe is evidence for God. How to encode this in probability terms? We can think of MN as capturing the intuition that it is surprising for there to be a contingent universe at all (especially one that began to exist) without an external cause. If God doesn’t exist, one might have to say it’s a brute fact that the universe (or multiverse) exists – but some argue that “nothing” is a simpler, more natural default, so existence needs explanation. If God does exist (as a necessary, self-existent entity), then the existence of a contingent world is not puzzling – God could choose to create it. Thus, one could claim $P(\text{Contingent Universe exists} \mid God)$ is fairly high (God likely would create something contingent, though not certain), whereas $P(\text{Contingent Universe exists} \mid \neg God)$ is lower, since there is no reason for anything to exist at all in that scenario. This aligns with Richard Swinburne’s and Alexander Pruss’s contentions that theism provides a deep explanatory advantage: it can posit a metaphysically necessary foundation. In philosophy terms, if either God or the universe itself is necessary, that would answer the question of existence . But if the universe is not necessary (which most scientists think it isn’t, given it could have been otherwise or not at all), then a necessary cause (God) is appealing. We also include in MN any weight coming from ontological arguments (which claim that if God’s existence is possible, then as a necessary being God must exist – though those are contentious). In our model, MN will generally contribute a prior tilt rather than evidence in the usual sense, because it’s about the framework of existence. However, we can still treat the mere fact “there is something rather than nothing” as an evidence datum that receives a probability assessment. For example, one might say: $P(\text{something exists} \mid \neg God)$ is low (maybe one expected eternal nothingness without a necessary being), whereas $P(\text{something exists} \mid God)$ is basically 1 (if God exists, obviously something exists, namely God, and God could create others). This would give a strong Bayes factor to theism. Not everyone finds it sensible to assign such probabilities – some argue “nothing” is not actually an alternative state that has a probability. Due caution is needed, but we include MN to cover the metaphysical argumentation (from contingency, necessity, ontology) from Chapter 9. In quantitative terms, MN might be reflected in how we choose the prior probability for God (since a necessary being might be seen as an intrinsically plausible or implausible idea a priori). We will come back to priors shortly. For now, think of MN as a placeholder for “the universe’s existence and laws require a necessary explanation” – which if one is persuaded by, adds to the case for God.These eight variables summarize a wide range of arguments: FT connects to the Teleological Argument (cosmic design), MO to the Moral Argument, CG to the Argument from Consciousness or Mind, RE to the Argument from Religious Experience, EV to the Problem of Evil (atheistic argument), BC to the Biological Design Argument, QM to more speculative modern physics arguments, and MN to the Contingency (and perhaps Ontological) Argument. Each variable can be conceptualized as a binary or continuous factor: e.g., FT could be binary (the universe is fine-tuned vs it is not) or a matter of degree (how fine-tuned); EV could be assessed by amount of evil or simply presence of apparently gratuitous evil. For simplicity, one might treat them as binary indicators (1 if the world has that feature strongly, 0 if not). In any case, the model’s structure will combine their contributions.From Qualitative Arguments to Quantitative CoefficientsHow can we quantify these philosophical arguments? This is admittedly challenging and requires careful, sometimes subjective, judgment. Philosophers have attempted to do this in various ways, often by estimating conditional probabilities. For instance, we might ask: “What is the probability that the fundamental constants would be life-permitting, if there were no intelligent designer?” and “What is that probability if there is an intelligent designer (God)?” One then takes the ratio of these as the evidential weight (Bayes factor) for fine-tuning. Similarly: “What is the probability of encountering objective moral truths if God does not exist? If God exists?” – and so on. The answers are not found in a physics or ethics textbook; they involve philosophical assessment and sometimes guesswork. However, scholars have made educated attempts.One approach is to use peer-reviewed philosophical arguments as guides. For example, in the fine-tuning case, Barnes (2020) carefully argues that $P(\text{Fine-tuned life} \mid \neg God)$ should be extremely small , while $P(\text{Fine-tuned life} \mid God)$ can reasonably be high . From this, we could assign a numerical Bayes factor: if “extremely small” means $10^{-50}$ (just to illustrate how small physicists sometimes estimate fine-tuning odds) and “high” means 0.5 or 1, the factor would be on the order of $10^{50}$ in favor of God – an astronomically large weight. A more modest interpretation might yield a factor of, say, $10^5$ or $10^6$. In practice, for our model demonstration, we might use a log-scale and say FT has a very large positive coefficient, acknowledging the literature’s stance that it’s strong evidence .For other arguments, literature provides rough qualitative estimates. Richard Swinburne, in The Existence of God, doesn’t assign precise numbers to each argument but qualitatively rates them: e.g., he argues the cosmological (contingency) argument gives some finite boost to theism, the teleological argument gives a larger boost, the arguments from consciousness and morality add further probability, and the problem of evil subtracts some probability, but not enough to overcome the others – leaving the overall probability of God (with all evidence considered) above 50% . Stephen Unwin, a risk analyst, made a bold attempt at numeric estimation in his 2003 book The Probability of God. Unwin chose six evidential factors and assigned each a likelihood ratio based on his subjective judgment of the data . For instance, he considered: (1) human goodness (which he thought is 10 times more likely if God exists), (2) moral evil (only 1/2 as likely if God exists, since God might prevent some evil), (3) natural evil (only 1/10 as likely if God exists), (4) miracles (events with no natural explanation – he split these into minor “intra-natural” coincidences, 2:1 more likely with God, and major “extra-natural” miracles being reported, which he surprisingly rated 1:1, equally likely with or without God), and (5) religious experiences (about 2:1 more likely if God exists) . He multiplied all these ratios together (10 * 1/2 * 1/10 * 2 * 1 * 2) to get an overall likelihood factor ~2 in favor of God . Starting with a neutral prior of 50% (odds 1:1), this yielded an updated probability of about 67% . Unwin then acknowledged this was not exact science and introduced a final subjective “faith factor” to represent personal stance. While some have criticized his specific numbers as overly generous or arbitrary (indeed, he was criticized for calling the 50% prior “bogus” and for “cherry picking” only six factors ), the exercise is instructive. It shows how each qualitative argument (goodness, evil, etc.) can be mapped to a quantitative odds multiplier. Our eight variables essentially generalize Unwin’s six, adding cosmological fine-tuning (which Unwin omitted and which would have added a very large multiplier in favor) and quantum/necessity factors.Another way to assign numbers is to use expert consensus or ranges. For example, a group of philosophers might be polled on how strongly they think a particular argument supports or undermines theism. Alternatively, one can derive constraints: Draper’s analysis of evil implies $P(E \mid \text{God}) \ll P(E \mid \neg \text{God})$; if one operationalizes “$\ll$” as, say, one-tenth, one-hundredth, or even smaller, one can plug that in. If we take Draper at face value that $E$ is “many times” (he says “many times” for suffering) more likely on naturalism, we might assign EV an odds ratio of perhaps 0.1 or 0.01 (i.e., evil of this magnitude is 10 or 100 times more probable absent God). Conversely, for a variable like RE (religious experience), Swinburne argues each individual testimony isn’t strong (maybe say 2:1 as Unwin did), but the cumulative weight of millions of experiences might be significant. One could attempt a calculation: if each independent report is 2:1 in favor of God, a million such reports would multiply the odds astronomically – but they are not independent (and many might not be valid). More conservatively, one might say the existence of widespread religious experience is, say, 5 times likelier if God exists (thus $\beta_{\text{RE}} = \ln(5)$), acknowledging that even under atheism some percentage of people might have anomalous experiences or psychological urges to religion.The process of converting arguments to numbers should also respect philosophical probabilities from literature. For example, some philosophers have tried to put a prior probability on God’s existence based on simplicity or scope. One might consider God a “simple” hypothesis in the sense of being one entity (as Swinburne argues) – which could justify a not-too-low prior. Others, like Richard Dawkins, consider God a very complex hypothesis (since an omniscient mind is complex), which in a Bayesian Occam’s Razor sense might give God a low prior. These considerations can be converted into a prior odds (discussed in the next section).In summary, while we cannot pinpoint exact agreed values for each evidential variable, we can use reasonable estimates informed by the arguments: Fine-tuning (FT) will have a strong positive weight; Moral objectivity (MO) a moderate positive weight (unless one is unconvinced of the premise); Consciousness gap (CG) some positive weight; Religious experience (RE) a modest positive weight; Evil (EV) a strong negative weight; Biological complexity (BC) perhaps a small positive weight (or zero, depending on one’s stance; we’ll assume small positive to reflect some lingering intuition of design); Quantum indicators (QM) likely a very small effect (we’ll assume slight positive for argument’s sake); Metaphysical necessity (MN) maybe a moderate positive (if one feels contingency strongly favors theism) or this might be partly rolled into the prior.For illustration, we will assign hypothetical coefficients to each variable, then compute outcomes. The aim is not to assert these as the correct values, but to show how the model works. The coefficients will effectively be $\ln(\text{odds multiplier})$ for each evidence. For example, if we say $\beta_{\text{FT}} = \ln(50) \approx 3.91$, that corresponds to fine-tuning being 50? more likely with God (a strong effect). If $\beta_{\text{EV}} = \ln(0.2) \approx -1.61$, that corresponds to evil being only 20% as likely if God exists (or put differently, evil is 5? more likely in a godless world). These numbers can be traced to the qualitative claims: “fine-tuning is vastly improbable without God” – maybe not infinitely improbable, but we choose 50? as a representation; “evil is far more likely without God” – we choose 5? more likely without (hence 0.2 factor with). One could just as well choose 10? or 100?; the model can be tested under different choices.Prior Probability and Its RoleAny Bayesian analysis requires a prior probability for the hypothesis before considering the new evidence. In our case, we need a prior $P(\text{God})$ (or prior odds) to start the updating process or to plug into the logistic regression intercept $\beta_0$. The choice of prior is contentious in debates about God. There are a few approaches one might take:* Neutral (50/50) Prior: One approach is to invoke the principle of indifference and say that, absent evidence, the chance that God exists is 50%. This was essentially Unwin’s starting point . It treats “God exists” and “God does not exist” as two equipossible hypotheses. The advantage of a 50/50 prior is that it appears neutral or unbiased, allowing the evidence to speak for itself. The intercept $\beta_0$ corresponding to 50% is $\ln(\frac{0.5}{0.5}) = 0$. Many theistic apologists implicitly assume a neutral prior in their cumulative cases, effectively arguing “given no strong presumption either way, the evidence tips the scales to God’s existence > 50%.”* Skeptical Prior (Low): Others argue that we should start with a low prior for God. For instance, an atheist might reason that there are countless conceivable supernatural beings or metaphysical possibilities, and by default any specific God hypothesis is very complex or specific, thus initially unlikely. Richard Dawkins in The God Delusion contended that because a Creator would have to be immensely complex (to design a complex universe), the prior probability of such a being is extremely small – he thought near zero (though he didn’t formally calculate it). Another angle: one might say naturalism is the “default” since it posits less (only the natural world exists), whereas theism posits the natural world plus an additional supernatural entity – thereby, by Occam’s Razor, theism should have a lower prior. Paul Draper’s premise (3) in the quoted argument says “Naturalism is more plausible (prior) than theism” independent of evidence, for similar reasons of simplicity. Skeptical philosophers like J.L. Mackie have also implied the extraordinary nature of the God hypothesis demands strong evidence (the proverb “extraordinary claims require extraordinary evidence” reflects an understanding that without evidence, the prior for something like miracles or gods is low). In numeric terms, a skeptic might choose, say, $P(\text{God}) = 10%$ (0.1) or 1% as a prior. This corresponds to an intercept $\beta_0 = \ln(\frac{0.1}{0.9}) \approx -2.20$ for 10%, or $\ln(0.01/0.99) \approx -4.59$ for 1%. Such priors mean the evidence needs to be quite powerful to overcome the initial skepticism. For example, with a 10% prior, an odds multiplier of 9:1 from evidence is needed just to reach 50% posterior.* Theistic Prior (High): Conversely, one could argue for a prior leaning towards theism. Perhaps one finds that the very concept of God is deeply rooted in human culture and consciousness (some might claim we have an innate sense of the divine). Or one might be persuaded by an ontological argument that the prior probability of a maximally great being is not low at all – maybe even that if God’s existence is possible it might be 50% or more a priori. Some theologians might say that because God is a simpler explanation of existence than a brute universe (Swinburne famously argued God is an “infinitely simple” being in terms of substance – having no parts, etc.), one could give theism a reasonable prior. A religious person might personally feel the prior is already high due to personal conviction or alleged self-evidence of God in conscience or logical necessity. Numerically, a theistic prior could be $P(\text{God}) = 90%$ (0.9) for someone quite sure before looking at cosmological/empirical evidence. That corresponds to $\beta_0 = \ln(0.9/0.1) \approx 2.20$.From a Bayesian perspective, the prior can be subjective, but one can test how conclusions vary under different priors. In a scientific context, one often does a sensitivity analysis: try a range of priors from skeptic to neutral to favorable and see how much evidence would be needed to reach, say, a 50% or 95% posterior. If the evidence is extremely strong, even a very low prior could be overcome; if the evidence is only mild, a low prior would result in a low posterior (thus a skeptic remains unconvinced). In our logistic model, the prior essentially sets the intercept. If we don’t explicitly include MN as evidence, we might fold the intuition from MN into the prior (for example, if one is impressed by the contingency argument, one might already have a higher prior that some necessary cause exists).For our worked example, we will consider a neutral prior (50%) as a baseline, so $\beta_0 = 0$. We will later compare what happens if the prior is substantially different. One of the tables will illustrate multiple scenarios including different priors, to satisfy the comparative requirement.It’s worth noting that likelihood ratios vs priors are conceptually separate. A rational evaluator might say “Even if I grant a low prior for God, the fine-tuning evidence is so overwhelming that it pushes the probability way up.” Or another might say “Even if I grant fine-tuning is strong evidence, my inherent prior for God is so low that after updating I still only get a moderate probability.” The interplay between prior and evidence will be demonstrated. In short, the prior captures one’s starting point (perhaps informed by arguments not explicitly in our eight, like a personal conviction or metaphysical principle), and the evidences (FT, MO, etc.) then update that to a final posterior. The beauty of the Bayesian/logistic framework is that everything is transparent: one can see exactly how each assumption (prior or likelihood) contributes.Example: A Logistic Regression CalculationLet us now construct a concrete example with hypothetical values for each variable, and compute the resulting probability of God’s existence. This will illustrate how the qualitative arguments combine quantitatively. For the sake of argument, suppose we have the following assignments for the eight variables (1 indicates the evidence is present in our world in a way that favors theism, and 0 would indicate it’s absent or neutral; intermediate values could represent partial strength, but we’ll keep to 0/1 for simplicity in this example). We also choose coefficients (log-odds weights) for each, based on our earlier discussion:* FT = 1 (the universe is indeed finely tuned for life). We assign $\beta_{\text{FT}} = \ln(100) \approx 4.605$. This means we’re saying: it is about 100 times more likely to get a life-permitting cosmos if God exists than if not. (In reality, some would argue an even bigger number, but 100 is already strong evidence, roughly equivalent to finding a 99% vs 1% likelihood ratio.)* MO = 1 (objective moral truths exist). Let $\beta_{\text{MO}} = \ln(3) \approx 1.099$. This corresponds to: moral order is 3 times more likely in a theistic universe than an atheistic one. (A moderate weight, acknowledging the moral argument has force but perhaps not as overwhelming as fine-tuning.)* CG = 1 (consciousness is a fundamental reality not reducible to matter, indicating a gap for theism to explain). We set $\beta_{\text{CG}} = \ln(2) \approx 0.693$. So we’re giving a 2:1 favor to theism for the fact of consciousness. (One might increase this if one is very convinced by the argument from mind, but we’ll keep it modest.)* RE = 1 (there are significant religious experiences reported). $\beta_{\text{RE}} = \ln(2) \approx 0.693$ as well. This gives a 2:1 weight to the existence of widespread religious experiences in favor of God. (Unwin similarly gave 2:1 for religious experiences .)* EV = 1 (there is significant evil and suffering in the world – basically this is always “1” for our world, as we definitely observe evil). But here 1 stands for “the world’s evil is at the level that constitutes evidence against God.” We assign $\beta_{\text{EV}} = \ln(0.2) \approx -1.609$. This is a strong negative weight: it means the probability of seeing this much evil is only 20% (1/5) as high if God exists than if God doesn’t. Equivalently, the evil is 5 times more likely in a godless world. (One could argue it should be even more extreme; Draper might lean to an order-of-magnitude of perhaps 10 times. We choose 5? for illustration. If we combined moral and natural evil, maybe natural evil alone could be 10? and moral evil 2?, which multiplies to 20? for total evil. Our 5? is somewhat conservative in light of those arguments, but still a major counterweight.)* BC = 1 (life exhibits remarkable complexity/design). We give a small positive weight: $\beta_{\text{BC}} = \ln(1.5) \approx 0.405$. So we assume life’s complexity is 1.5 times more likely with a designer than without. This is mild; it reflects perhaps the intuition that while evolution explains a lot, the very existence of an evolutionary process and genetic code might itself be something a theist can still credit to God. It’s a nod to the design argument without going full Paley (which would have been much larger if we were in 1802 before Darwin). If one were an ID proponent, they might argue for a bigger number, but we’ll keep it minimal.* QM = 1 (suppose there are some quantum phenomena hinting at something beyond classical materialism – e.g. some interpretations that require mind, or just the general fit of quantum reality with a richer metaphysics). We’ll put $\beta_{\text{QM}} = \ln(1.1) \approx 0.095$. This is almost negligible. It gives a 1.1:1 slight nod to theism. Essentially, we’re saying quantum weirdness doesn’t strongly favor God, but maybe by about 10% it does, in our subjective take (just in case, for example, one thinks the mere existence of true randomness or non-locality gels a bit more with theistic than a strictly mechanistic worldview).* MN = 1 (contingency demands a necessary being). We set $\beta_{\text{MN}} = \ln(2) \approx 0.693$. This means the fact that a contingent universe exists is twice as likely if God exists than if not. In effect, we’re giving some weight to the cosmological contingency argument, but not an enormous amount. (This could arguably be larger, but since some might consider it part of prior, we’ll keep it moderate here.)Now, we also choose a prior. Let’s start with the neutral prior 50%, so $\beta_0 = 0$. Summing up all contributions, the logit is:\text{logit}[P(\text{God})] = 0 + 4.605(\text{FT}=1) + 1.099(\text{MO}=1) + 0.693(\text{CG}=1) + 0.693(\text{RE}=1) + (-1.609)(\text{EV}=1) + 0.405(\text{BC}=1) + 0.095(\text{QM}=1) + 0.693(\text{MN}=1).If we plug in all 1’s (assuming our world has all these evidences present as described), we get:* From FT: +4.605* MO: +1.099* CG: +0.693* RE: +0.693* EV: –1.609* BC: +0.405* QM: +0.095* MN: +0.693Adding these to the intercept 0:Total logit = 4.605 + 1.099 + 0.693 + 0.693 – 1.609 + 0.405 + 0.095 + 0.693 = 6.674.This corresponds to odds = $e^{6.674} \approx 789.4$ in favor of God. In probability terms, that’s $P = 789.4/(1+789.4) \approx 0.9987$, or about 99.87%. In other words, with the particular weights we assumed, the evidences together would make God’s existence a virtual certainty. That may seem surprisingly high, but recall our choices: we gave an extremely strong weight to FT which alone was 100:1 in favor; combined with several other pro-theism factors (even though EV was strong against, it was only 5:1 against). Indeed, the fine-tuning evidence in this setup dominates. If one truly believes fine-tuning is ~100? more likely with God, then unless one had a very low prior, the posterior will end up very high. This highlights how a single very large piece of evidence can outweigh multiple smaller pieces.However, our choices are not the only plausible ones. Let’s consider a slightly more conservative set to see a more nuanced result. Perhaps we should not count all evidence as strongly favoring theism. What if, for example, the fine-tuning were not quite so decisive, or the moral argument wasn’t fully accepted? We can adjust values:Imagine a scenario (Scenario X) where: FT is present but we give it a smaller weight (say $\beta_{\text{FT}} = \ln(10) = 2.303$, meaning 10? likely with God – still significant but not as astronomical), MO is present with $\beta_{\text{MO}} = \ln(1.5) = 0.405$ (you think moral values give some support but not huge), CG present with $\beta_{\text{CG}} = 0.405$ (a bit of support), RE present with $\beta_{\text{RE}} = 0.693$ (as before), EV present with $\beta_{\text{EV}} = -1.204$ (that’s $\ln(0.3)$, meaning evil is about 3.3 times more likely without God – a slightly weaker assumption than before of 5?, but still negative), BC present with $\beta_{\text{BC}} = 0.182$ (which is $\ln(1.2)$, a trivial support), QM neutral (set $\beta_{\text{QM}} = 0$ for simplicity), MN neutral (perhaps we exclude it here or set $\beta_{\text{MN}} = 0$ thinking it’s already in prior or we are unsure). Then the sum of coefficients would be:* FT: +2.303* MO: +0.405* CG: +0.405* RE: +0.693* EV: –1.204* BC: +0.182* QM: 0* MN: 0* Intercept: 0 (50% prior)Total = 2.303 + 0.405 + 0.405 + 0.693 – 1.204 + 0.182 = 2.784. (We omitted QM, MN for this scenario.)This logit 2.784 corresponds to odds $e^{2.784} \approx 16.18$, i.e. roughly 16:1 in favor. The probability would be $16.18/(1+16.18) \approx 0.941$, about 94.1% for God’s existence. So even with more modest weights, this hypothetical set of evidence still yields a high probability, mainly because FT (10?) and RE and others overcame EV (which we set at only 3.3? against). If we wanted a more evenly poised outcome, we could lower the pro-theistic weights or raise the impact of EV.Let’s consider Scenario A: a moderately pro-theism scenario, and Scenario B: a moderately pro-atheism scenario Monte Carlo simulation of the probabilistic model. Each bar shows the frequency of a particular posterior probability of God’s existence across 100,000 simulations, where each evidential variable’s weight was randomly sampled from a reasonable range. Most simulations yield a high posterior (right-side bars), but a minority produce lower probabilities (left-side tail), reflecting uncertainty in evidential strength.To further illustrate the model, let’s compare a few scenarios under different assumptions. We will examine how the final probability $P(\text{God})$ changes with varying priors and evidential weights:ScenarioPrior $P(\text{God})$Key Evidence AssumptionsPosterior $P(\text{God})$A. Neutral Prior, Pro-Theism50% (even odds)Strong FT, MO, CG, RE present; EV moderate; others mild~76% (more likely than not) B. Neutral Prior, Pro-Atheism50%FT absent; EV very high (severe); MO, CG weak; others neutral~35% (unlikely) C. Skeptical Prior, Same as A10% (skeptical)Same evidence as Scenario A (strong pro-theistic evidences)~26% (still low)D. Favorable Prior, Same as B90% (theistic leaning)Same evidence as Scenario B (strong counter-evidence)~83% (high, prior dominates)Explanation: In Scenario A, we assumed a neutral starting point and that the world displays clear signs of fine-tuning, moral truth, consciousness, and religious experiences, with only a moderate level of unexplained evil. The model gives about 76% probability to God – tipping the scales in favor of theism. In Scenario B, we again start neutral but consider a world where fine-tuning isn’t evident (perhaps physics could have been otherwise without life, but we don’t see it as special) and where evil and suffering are extremely severe (far more than one would expect if a benevolent God exists). Here the posterior drops to around 35%, meaning atheism becomes more probable . Scenario C uses the same evidences as A (a world favoring theism), but a person with a very skeptical prior (10%) evaluates it. Despite strong evidence, their posterior is only ~26%; their initial disbelief is hard to overcome. By contrast, Scenario D shows a person with a strong theistic prior (90%) evaluating a world like B (which had strong evidence against God). This person still ends up with ~83% belief – the prior confidence was so high that even contrary evidence doesn’t fully dislodge it (though it did decrease from 90% to 83%). These scenarios demonstrate how prior beliefs and evidence weights interplay. Generally, if one’s prior is neutral, the evidence will decide the outcome; if one’s prior is extreme, it takes overwhelming evidence to counter it.Discussion and ConclusionIn this chapter, we transitioned from qualitative philosophical arguments to a quantitative probabilistic analysis of God’s existence. We introduced a Bayesian-logistic model incorporating eight evidential variables drawn from classic debates: Cosmological Fine-Tuning (FT), Moral Objectivity (MO), the Consciousness Gap (CG), Religious Experience (RE), the severity of Evil (EV), Biological Complexity (BC), Quantum Indicators (QM), and Metaphysical Necessity (MN). Each of these was grounded in one or more arguments covered earlier in the book – for example, FT in the design argument , MO in the moral argument , and EV in the problem of evil . We saw how philosophers have assessed these factors: fine-tuning and consciousness are often cited as strong evidence for God , while the existence of intense suffering is strong evidence against . By assigning likelihood ratios or regression coefficients to each factor, we translated these intuitive judgments into a mathematical form. For instance, if fine-tuning is “highly improbable” without God but expected with God, we gave it a large positive weight ; if evil is “many times more likely” on naturalism than theism, we gave it a negative weight .Using a logistic regression model, we demonstrated a sample calculation of the posterior probability of God. The worked example illustrated that under one reasonable set of assumptions, the evidences combined to make God’s existence highly probable (over 99%), but we also explored variations showing more moderate results (e.g., ~76% in one scenario). We further employed Monte Carlo simulation (illustrated in the embedded figure) to account for uncertainty in the weights. The simulation showed that, in our model, the probability of God’s existence tends to be quite high in the majority of trials, though there is a tail of outcomes with lower probabilities. This indicates that unless multiple pro-theistic evidences are much weaker than we think (or the anti-theistic evidences much stronger), the overall balance tilts toward theism in this framework. Of course, a different model or different weight choices could yield a different result – our aim was not to prove God probabilistically, but to model the structure of a cumulative case argument.We also discussed the crucial role of the prior. The prior encapsulates one’s initial credence in God before considering the specific evidences. We compared a neutral 50/50 prior to a skeptical 10% prior and a theistic 90% prior. This showed that evidence does not speak in a vacuum: someone who finds the very idea of God antecedently unlikely will require stronger evidence to believe, whereas someone already inclined to believe will remain convinced even in the face of some counter-evidence. Bayesian epistemology thus provides a nuanced way to see why intelligent people can disagree – they might have different priors or weigh the same evidence differently.It’s important to address the limitations of this approach. Quantifying philosophical arguments involves subjectivity. We relied on philosophical literature to ground our estimates (e.g., Barnes for fine-tuning likelihoods , Draper for evil , Swinburne for experience ), but reasonable people could assign different numbers. The model’s output is only as good as the input assumptions. Moreover, dependencies between evidences were mostly ignored (we treated them as independent contributors, though one could argue, for example, that moral truth and consciousness might correlate if one has a certain worldview). A fully rigorous model might need to consider such correlations.Another point is that our eight variables and their weights represent a particular theistic hypothesis – roughly classical theism with a morally good creator. If one tweaked the hypothesis (say, a deistic God who doesn’t intervene or a morally ambiguous deity), the assessment of evidence like RE or EV could change (a deist might say miracles are very unlikely, so reports of miracles become evidence against a God who wouldn’t do them). Our model implicitly assumed a God consistent with the arguments presented (an omnipotent, omnibenevolent creator as per the book’s context).Despite these caveats, this probabilistic approach has significant value. It forces clarity about how strongly each argument affects one’s overall view. It also highlights that the cumulative case for God is not a simple sum – strong pieces of evidence can compensate for weaker ones or vice versa. For example, even if one finds the problem of evil quite damaging (as many do), one might still rationally believe in God if one simultaneously finds the fine-tuning and consciousness arguments overwhelmingly convincing. The model shows quantitatively how that can happen, by assigning a huge weight to fine-tuning that swamps the penalty from evil (we saw a glimpse of that in our initial calculation).In academic philosophy of religion, such Bayesian formulations are increasingly common. They bring probabilistic rigor to discussions often dominated by qualitative rhetoric. As long as one is transparent about the assumptions, others can see where they might disagree (perhaps a critic says: “I think you vastly overestimated $P(\text{Fine-tuning} \mid \neg God)$; it shouldn’t be near zero” – that translates to adjusting $\beta_{\text{FT}}$ and seeing how the conclusion changes). Thus, the debate shifts to the plausibility of those assumptions, a more focused dispute.In conclusion, developing a probabilistic model from philosophical arguments allows us to move from informal argumentation to a structured analysis. It doesn’t replace the need for sound philosophical judgment, but it complements it by ensuring our reasoning is coherent and by quantifying how much each piece of evidence matters. The chapter demonstrated this with a model where, given what many philosophers consider plausible estimates, the posterior probability of God’s existence came out high. However, by tweaking those estimates or priors, one can obtain lower probabilities, showing that the result is not a foregone conclusion of mathematics but a reflection of how one evaluates the world. This approach, therefore, provides a framework for ongoing dialogue: theists and atheists can pinpoint exactly which evidential weight or prior belief is driving their difference, and discuss that specific point. Ultimately, whether one ends up convinced that “God exists” or “God does not exist” – or remains uncertain – this quantitative methodology enriches our understanding of why we come to the conclusions we do, by tracing the path from arguments to analysis in a logically rigorous way.Sources:* Swinburne, R. (2004). The Existence of God – outlines a Bayesian cumulative case for theism .* Collins, R. (2009). “The Teleological Argument: An Exploration of the Fine-Tuning of the Universe” – discusses probabilities in fine-tuning .* Draper, P. (1989). “Pain and Pleasure: An Evidential Problem for Theists” – presents the Bayesian argument from evil .* Moreland, J.P. (2008). Consciousness and the Existence of God – argues consciousness is evidence for theism .* Price, R. (1767). “Four Dissertations” – early use of Bayes’ theorem for argument about miracles .* Unwin, S. (2003). The Probability of God – an attempt to quantify a cumulative case (see critique in Carrier (2018)) .* Scientific American (2007). “What is Bayes’s Theorem and how can it be used to assign probabilities to God?” – overview of applying Bayes to God .* Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: “Fine-Tuning” , “Cosmological Argument” , etc., for summaries of arguments.* Carrier, R. (2018). “Crank Bayesians: Swinburne & Unwin” – a critique of overconfident Bayesian apologetics .* MDPI Religions Journal (2023). “Quantum Physics and the Existence of God” – example of linking quantum mechanics to theism .Chapter 5 : Cosmology and Existence – Contingency, Origin, and Fine-TuningHistorical Cosmological Arguments for a Necessary ExistenceThe cosmological argument seeks to infer the existence of a necessary or first cause of reality from the existence or beginning of the universe. This line of reasoning has deep historical roots in philosophy and theology . Key classical formulations include:* Aquinas’s Cosmological Arguments: Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274) offered several versions in his Summa Theologica. Notably, his Third Way argues from contingency: things in the world come into being and pass away (they are contingent); if everything were contingent and could not exist, then, Aquinas argues, at some time in the past nothing would have existed. But if nothing ever existed, nothing would exist now – which is false . Therefore, there must be at least one necessary being that must exist and provides the ground for all contingent beings . Unlike later Kalam arguments, Aquinas did not hinge this on a beginning in time; he held that even an eternal universe would require a sustaining cause of its existence . Thus, Aquinas’s argument is that the universe’s existence right now depends on a necessary being (which he identifies as God) to uphold it, whether or not the universe had a temporal beginning.* Leibniz’s Principle of Sufficient Reason: Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646–1716) formulated a classic argument from sufficient reason and contingency. Leibniz asked, “Why is there something rather than nothing?” and argued that no contingent fact can be fully explained by another contingent fact – ultimately, the sufficient reason for the existence of the contingent universe must lie in a necessary substance that carries the reason for its existence in itself . In Leibniz’s words, “The sufficient reason… is found in a substance which… is a necessary being bearing the reason for its existence within itself.” This necessary being (God) explains the existence of all contingent reality. In modern terms, this is sometimes called the Leibnizian cosmological argument or argument from contingency. It relies on the Principle of Sufficient Reason (PSR) – the philosophical principle that every existent thing or true fact must have an explanation for why it is so and not otherwise . If one accepts the PSR, then one is led to posit an ultimate explanation – a necessary entity that itself needs no external explanation. Even without full acceptance of a strict PSR, many find it more plausible that some transcendent necessary being accounts for the existence of the universe, rather than believing that reality as a whole is a brute fact with no explanation . Thus, for Leibniz and his successors, the very contingency of the cosmos (that it could have been otherwise or not existed at all) is evidence that points toward a necessary, self-existent Ground of Being.* Kalam Argument in Islamic and Contemporary Thought: In medieval Islamic theology (kal?m), scholars developed a cosmological argument focusing on the universe’s beginning in time . For example, Al-Ghaz?l? (1058–1111) contended that actual infinites cannot exist in reality and thus an infinite past is impossible; hence the world began to exist, and whatever begins must have a cause . This reasoning was later termed the Kal?m Cosmological Argument. A simple formulation is given by modern philosopher William Lane Craig (who revitalized this argument in contemporary debates ):1. Everything that begins to exist has a cause of its existence.2. The universe began to exist.3. Therefore, the universe has a cause of its existence. Typically, further reasoning is offered that this cause must be outside the universe (since physical reality itself began) and therefore is immaterial, eternal, and powerful – often concluding that the cause is God . Craig, following al-Ghaz?l?, defends premise (2) with philosophical arguments against an infinite temporal regress and appeals to scientific evidence of a cosmic beginning (more on that below). Notably, premise (1) relies on a deep intuition or metaphysical principle that ex nihilo nihil fit – “from nothing, nothing comes,” i.e. being cannot spontaneously arise from non-being . Even critics of the Kalam argument acknowledge its intuitive appeal: “something cannot come from nothing” resonates with basic metaphysical intuition . The Kalam argument thus dovetails with modern cosmology’s indications of a beginning, bringing ancient Islamic thought into dialogue with contemporary science. As we will see, the question of whether the universe had a beginning is central to modern cosmological discussion of contingency.These classical arguments differ in emphasis – Aquinas and Leibniz focus on the contingency of the world (needing a sustaining necessary being), while the Kalam focuses on temporal origination (needing a first cause in time). Both themes, contingency and origin, remain highly relevant. They raise profound questions: Is the universe contingent (could it have been otherwise or not existed)? Did it have an origin in time? If so, what is the nature of the cause or explanation behind it? These questions set the stage for examining the findings of modern cosmology.Big Bang Cosmology – The Beginning of the Universe and its ContingencyModern cosmology has dramatically advanced our understanding of the universe’s origin. The Big Bang theory – supported by a broad scientific consensus – posits that the universe expanded from an extremely hot, dense state about 13.8 billion years ago . Space and time, as we know them, are thought to have begun with this primordial event. This marks a stunning convergence with the Kalam’s premise that the universe began to exist. Indeed, throughout the 20th century, discoveries forced scientists “to the uncomfortable conclusion that our universe came into being in the finite past” . In effect, what many religious thinkers had long affirmed – a temporal beginning – gained empirical backing: Edwin Hubble’s observation of an expanding universe (1929) and the discovery of the cosmic microwave background radiation (1965) confirmed key predictions of a cosmic origin from a hot, dense beginning.Such a finite past has philosophical import. A cosmos with a beginning in time naturally prompts the question of what caused that beginning. Physicist Stephen Hawking once commented, “A point of creation would be a place where science broke down. One would have to appeal to religion and the hand of God.” . In other words, if there is an initial moment where physical laws and explanations run out, it looks like a moment of creation ex nihilo (out of nothing), which classical theology attributes to a Creator. The Big Bang, when first proposed, indeed “sounded too much like the creation ex nihilo of Genesis” to some researchers – leading a few, like Fred Hoyle, to prefer steady-state models that avoid a beginning. But steady-state cosmologies fell out of favor as evidence mounted for a Big Bang beginning. By now, as noted, the Big Bang model is well established: “there’s scientific consensus that our universe exploded into existence… in an event known as the Big Bang.” .Does the Big Bang imply a “creation from nothing”? In the standard Big Bang model, as one extrapolates back in time, one reaches a state of extremely high density and temperature – classically, a singularity at time t = 0. This is often taken to mark the beginning of the universe (when space and time themselves emerged). If so, the cosmos indeed had a first moment. However, physicists are quick to note that the classical Big Bang singularity may simply indicate the breakdown of known physics – quantum gravity effects could avert an actual “point” singularity. Researchers have proposed models to explain what, if anything, preceded the Big Bang:* Hawking’s No-Boundary Proposal: Stephen Hawking and James Hartle suggested in 1983 that the universe could be finite in time but without a boundary or singular start. Using a concept of “imaginary time,” their model smooths out the t=0 point – making the universe akin to a sphere that has no edge (just as there’s no edge to Earth’s surface, there’s no precise “before” the Big Bang, since time’s boundary is rounded off) . Hawking explained that asking what happened before the Big Bang is meaningless, “like asking what lies south of the South Pole”, if his no-boundary condition holds . In this view, the universe could be self-contained, with no initial singularity – potentially removing the need for a metaphysical cause at a specific moment. However, even the no-boundary model doesn’t evade the question of existential origin entirely: it still posits a law (the wave function of the universe) that brings the cosmos into being. The philosophical question of “why is there something rather than nothing?” can be shifted but not eliminated – even a no-boundary universe is still contingent on the laws of physics (and one may ask why those laws, and not others, obtain).* Oscillating or Cyclical Universes: Another idea is that perhaps the universe did not begin 13.8 billion years ago, but has undergone endless cycles of expansion and contraction (“Big Bang, Big Crunch, Big Bang, …”). This would imply an eternal series of universes. However, thermodynamics poses a serious challenge: with each cycle, entropy (disorder) would build up. Over an infinite past, the universe would have inexorably wound down to heat death by now, unable to spring into a fresh, low-entropy Big Bang. In fact, cosmologist Alexander Vilenkin notes that in a cyclic model, “disorder would accumulate with each cycle… If the universe had been going through this process eternally, we should find ourselves in a universe completely disordered and dead”, which we plainly do not . The observed low entropy of our Big Bang (discussed more below) indicates our universe is not an infinitely old cyclical one. Thus, cyclic models cannot be extended infinitely into the past without contradiction .* Eternal Inflation and Multiverse Models: The most discussed modern alternative to a singular beginning is the idea of a multiverse that exists eternally, spawning many Big Bangs (universes) of which ours is just one bubble. In cosmic inflation theory, originally proposed by Alan Guth in 1980, the early universe underwent an exponential expansion that smoothed it out . Inflationary theory, in some forms, implies that inflation might be eternal – while our pocket of space stopped inflating and became a hot Big Bang, other regions might still be inflating, generating potentially infinitely many universes over time. Could this avoid a true beginning? Interestingly, a well-known result, the Borde-Guth-Vilenkin (BGV) theorem (2003), suggests that even an inflationary multiverse must have a past boundary under reasonable assumptions. Vilenkin and colleagues proved that any universe (or multiverse) on average expanding must be geodesically past-incomplete – in other words, it cannot be extended infinitely into the past; there must be some boundary or start to the inflationary process . Vilenkin, after examining various eternal universe scenarios (eternal inflation, cyclicity, a static “cosmic egg”), concluded: “All the evidence we have says that the universe had a beginning.” . This is a striking statement from a leading cosmologist: despite creative models, the weight of cosmological evidence still points to a finite past and some kind of cosmic genesis.In sum, modern cosmology has largely confirmed a beginning (as religious thinkers long asserted), even if the precise nature of the beginning is still debated. A genuine beginning in time buttresses the Kalam argument – if the universe began to exist, the intuitive need for a cause or explanation kicks in with full force. Even if one entertains models where the universe has no sharp boundary, the fact that the universe (or multiverse) is not eternal in the past suggests that reality is not self-existent; it had to originate somehow. This strongly implies contingency: the universe might not have existed – and for the cosmological argument, that “might not” is a key indicator that an external cause or a metaphysical reason is needed to explain why it does in fact exist .The Low-Entropy Beginning and the “Improbability” of the UniverseAnother aspect of modern cosmology relevant to contingency is the entropy of the early universe. The Second Law of Thermodynamics tells us that closed systems tend toward higher entropy (disorder) over time. Our universe today still has ordered structures (galaxies, stars, life) and far from maximum entropy; in fact, it appears the universe began in an extraordinarily low-entropy, highly ordered state . Sir Roger Penrose famously quantified how special this initial state was. He calculated that the odds of an initial low-entropy state compatible with our life-friendly universe (just by random chance) are about 1 in 10^10^123 – an inconceivably small probability . To call this number “astronomical” would be a gross understatement; as Penrose puts it, achieving such order by chance is “an extraordinary figure” beyond imagination . He quipped that this tells us “how precise the Creator’s aim must have been” to hit such a target, noting one could not even write down all the zeros in that number . (Penrose himself used the term “Creator” metaphorically, but the implication of fine-tuning is clear .)What does this mean philosophically? It underscores that the initial conditions of the cosmos were exquisitely arranged. If one assumes a purely random, undirected universe, it is hard to see why it started in such an ordered state (rather than in a high-entropy chaos). The low-entropy Big Bang stands out as a cosmic contingency that “begs for an explanation” . Some cosmologists have even entertained that this astonishing order might hint at some kind of teleological principle or constraint beyond known physics. At minimum, Penrose’s result is often cited by theists as powerful evidence of deliberate design – the universe’s beginning state was set up in a highly improbable, “just right” way to allow for the complexity and life that followed . (Alternative attempts to explain it, such as inflationary cosmology, do not actually eliminate the need for an initial low-entropy condition; in fact, inflation requires even more special initial conditions in some respects .)Summary: Modern cosmology reinforces the notion of the universe’s contingency and origin in several ways. The Big Bang gives a finite beginning, which cries out for a cause or reason beyond the universe. Efforts to avoid a beginning (no-boundary universe, cyclic models, eternal inflation) have not succeeded in eliminating a past boundary or the need for initial conditions – something must be “given” to start the physics. The incredibly ordered initial state further accentuates how non-random our cosmos appears to be from the very start. All of these findings resonate with the cosmological argument’s core intuition: the universe is not the kind of thing that explains itself. If it had a beginning, we seek a beginner; if it is contingent, we seek a necessary ground. In the next section, we consider another facet of cosmology that amplifies this intuition: the precise tuning of the universe’s fundamental parameters, which many argue points to purpose and design.Fine-Tuning of Physical Constants and the Inference of DesignOne of the most remarkable discoveries of late-20th-century physics and cosmology is that the fundamental constants and initial quantities of the universe are finely tuned for the emergence of complex life. The term fine-tuning refers to the observation that many independent parameters of physics – the strengths of forces, masses of elementary particles, cosmic initial conditions – fall within an extraordinarily narrow range that permits the existence of atoms, stars, planets, and life as we know it . Even slight deviations in these values would render the universe inhospitable to any form of complexity. Some examples of these cosmic “sweet spots” include:* The cosmological constant (energy density of empty space): Empirical measurements show it is about 10^(-120) in Planck units. If this constant were even a tiny bit larger (say on the order of 10^(-119) instead of 10^(-120)), the universe’s expansion would have accelerated so fast that galaxies and stars could not form . Steven Weinberg noted this value is stunningly “unnatural” on an atheistic or random model of the universe . It’s as if the vacuum energy was dialed down to almost zero, but not exactly zero – a vanishingly precise balance that allows cosmic structure. Susskind puts it in perspective: the vacuum energy appears tuned to 120 decimal places of precision – “to make the first 119 decimal places of the vacuum energy zero is almost certainly no accident,” he writes .* The strength of gravity relative to electromagnetism: If gravity were significantly stronger, stars would burn out much faster and small planets could not exist; if it were weaker, stars might not ignite nuclear fusion, and heavy elements essential for life might never form. A change on the order of even a few percent in the gravitational constant or the ratio of proton to electron mass would drastically alter the chemistry of the universe .* The initial entropy (as discussed): The Big Bang’s initial conditions had to be very special. As we saw, the odds of such a low-entropy state by chance are infinitesimal . This can be viewed as fine-tuning of initial conditions – the universe started in an extremely ordered state that allows billions of years of complex structure formation.* “Just Six Numbers”: As summarized by astrophysicist Martin Rees, six dimensionless numbers (such as the fine structure constant, the ratio of fundamental forces, etc.) form a “recipe” for a life-permitting universe . Tweak any of them, and the universe becomes barren. Rees and others have documented how sensitive the universe’s structure is to these key values .Over the decades, scientists like John D. Barrow and Frank Tipler compiled extensive evidence of such fine-tuning in The Anthropic Cosmological Principle (1986) . They and others articulate various forms of the anthropic principle – the idea that we can explain some of this tuning by noting that we observers can only exist in universes that have the right conditions for observers. In the words of Brandon Carter (who coined the term in 1973), what we can expect to observe must be restricted by the conditions necessary for our presence as observers. The Weak Anthropic Principle (WAP) simply states this truism: we observe this universe because it allows observers to exist. The Strong Anthropic Principle goes further to suggest perhaps the universe must have those properties which allow life at some stage, hinting at deeper theory or purpose .However, the bare anthropic principle alone is not an explanation for fine-tuning; it’s more of a selection effect description. The real question remains: why do the constants fall in the life-permitting range to begin with? By the late 20th century, it became “broadly agreed among physicists and cosmologists” that fine-tuning is a real phenomenon that demands some explanation . As one review puts it, “There is now broad agreement that the universe is in several respects ‘fine-tuned’ for life” – the debate is over what explanation to give.From a theistic perspective, the fine-tuning is naturally explained as intentional design. If a divine Creator purposed to bring about life (and eventually intelligent life capable of observing the universe), then we expect the universe’s parameters to be set just right to achieve that end . In this view, the various “cosmic coincidences” are not coincidences at all, but the result of teleology (purpose). Many consider this a modern, scientific form of the teleological argument (argument from design). As philosopher Robin Collins argues, the probability of obtaining a life-permitting universe by chance is astronomically low, whereas if a Creator wanted to create life, the probability of a life-friendly universe is high . Thus our observation strongly favors the design hypothesis over a chance hypothesis – one can even compute a Bayesian likelihood ratio on the order of 10^120 or more in favor of theism, given the tiny proportion of possible universes that would permit life . William Lane Craig summarizes: it is the combination of enormous improbability under atheistic chance and the existence of a specific life-permitting order (a pattern that is meaningful) that are “the earmarks of design” . In essence, that our universe fits a narrow specification (allowing life) when it could easily have been chaos is taken as positive evidence for an intelligent fine-tuner. Even Stephen Hawking, speaking of the fine adjustments of the constants, wrote: “The remarkable fact is that the values of these numbers seem to have been very finely adjusted to make possible the development of life.” (This from an author who did not readily invoke God – highlighting that the appearance of fine-tuning is an objective fact, whatever one’s interpretation.)Naturally, there are non-theistic responses to fine-tuning:* Chance – Could we really just be extremely lucky? This seems implausible when one multiplies the probabilities of all independent parameters aligning in the life-permitting range (the combined odds would be outrageously small, essentially zero ). In probabilistic terms, an event with probability less than 1 in 10^50 is often considered effectively impossible; fine-tuning examples are frequently far more extreme . As one scientist remarked, “we’re not talking about a single unlikely event here. We’re talking about hitting the jackpot over and over again” to get a universe like ours by blind luck . So while logically it’s not impossible that we just won a one-in-10^10^123 lottery, many find that an unsatisfying explanation, especially without some mechanism to make many “trials” (which leads to the multiverse idea).* Physical necessity – Perhaps there is some deeper law or theory of everything that forces the constants to have the values they do; i.e., maybe there is no free parameter at all – the universe could not have been otherwise. This is an intriguing possibility, but so far we have no evidence of such a unique theory that fixes constants. The values appear contingent in our current best theories (the Standard Model of particle physics, cosmological models, etc., all have free parameters). Absent any known reason that, say, the electron’s charge must be exactly what it is, the “necessity” hypothesis is speculative. John Barrow points out that even if future physics explains one constant’s value, it often will involve new constants or initial conditions, so some element of contingency remains . In any case, the idea of an underlying necessity doesn’t avoid the question; it just buries it one layer deeper (why that theory or “meta-law” with those fixed values?).* Multiverse (Anthropic Selection) – By far the most popular non-design explanation for fine-tuning is the multiverse hypothesis. The conjecture is that our universe is just one of a vast ensemble of universes (whether in a literal multiverse of distinct regions or sequential universes, or as possible domains in a larger quantum cosmology). If there are an enormous (perhaps infinite) number of universes with varied constants and initial conditions, then it’s not surprising that some universe turned out to have the right combination for life. We, as observers, could only exist in that rare universe, so naturally we find ourselves in a fine-tuned cosmos – nothing beyond the laws of probability and selection effect. This leverages the Weak Anthropic Principle together with a large sample size of universes.Modern physics has provided some motivation for multiverse ideas: eternal inflation implies bubble universes with different properties; string theory’s landscape of ~10^500 possible vacuum configurations suggests a huge variety of possible universes . According to Leonard Susskind (a leading string theorist), the existence of a landscape of vacua means our universe’s features might be a random draw; he even titled his book The Cosmic Landscape: String Theory and the Illusion of Intelligent Design – implying that fine-tuning only appears designed but in reality arises from cosmic lottery. Susskind acknowledges the “uncanny… spectacularly well-designed” appearance of the universe, particularly the cosmological constant being so near zero, but he favors the multiverse as the scientific explanation .It’s worth noting how some scientists frame the stakes: “If there is only one universe,” says cosmologist Bernard Carr, “you might have to have a fine-tuner. If you don’t want God, you’d better have a multiverse.” . Steven Weinberg – an avowed atheist – similarly commented that an impressive fine-tuning would leave only two options: “a benevolent Designer or a multiverse.” In other words, even atheistic physicists recognize that without the multiverse, the fine-tuning cries out for a design explanation. The multiverse is thus sometimes seen as the only viable alternative to the God hypothesis in face of fine-tuning. This is why debates about the multiverse often carry philosophical and theological overtones. Proponents argue it provides a naturalistic way to understand our fortunate cosmos without invoking divine purpose. Detractors point out that the multiverse (at least currently) is highly speculative and unobservable – raising the question of whether it’s a scientific explanation at all or more of a metaphysical speculation. Moreover, even if a multiverse exists, one can ask: why does that multiverse exist, with those laws? The problem of contingency can just recur at the higher level (as physicist Paul Davies observes, the multiverse “shifts the problem up a level” to the laws governing the multiverse ). There is also the issue that an infinite multiverse could predict all sorts of bizarre or lethal universes, and it’s non-trivial that only nice life-permitting ones would exist; anthropic reasoning must be applied carefully to avoid unfalsifiable conclusions .In summary, the fine-tuning debate pits the design hypothesis against the multiverse hypothesis (with a small minority holding out for pure chance or undiscovered physical necessity). As of now, fine-tuning is a well-established fact in science; what it means is hotly debated. For our purposes in evaluating evidence for God, fine-tuning (if not explained away by some multiverse) is a significant pro argument – it suggests that the universe is neither random nor self-explanatory, but rather intricately calibrated, which is exactly what we would expect if an intelligent Creator had set up the cosmos for a purpose . We now turn to how leading scientists and thinkers interpret these cosmological findings, and then connect these insights back to the variables in our probabilistic model.Perspectives of Leading Thinkers: Penrose, Hawking, Susskind, BarrowTo appreciate the intersection of cosmology and philosophical inference, it’s helpful to see what some renowned scientific thinkers have said about these issues:* Roger Penrose: A mathematical physicist known for contributions to general relativity and quantum theory, Penrose has often highlighted the extraordinary nature of the Big Bang’s initial state. We saw his calculation of the 1 in 10^10^123 odds for low entropy – a figure he uses to illustrate how “special” the universe’s initial conditions were . Penrose, who is not an advocate of traditional theism, nevertheless uses almost teleological language: the precision is such that it’s as if the universe “knew we were coming.” He has criticized the idea that inflation theory by itself can explain this fine-tuning; inflation might explain how a tiny region became large and smooth, but it assumes a low-entropy patch to start with, so it “begs the question” of why that patch existed . Penrose’s candid reflections underscore that even a scientifically sophisticated mind finds the order of the universe deeply puzzling if one rejects any sort of planning or guiding principle. In his search for understanding, Penrose has explored novel ideas (conformal cyclic cosmology, for instance), but the core takeaway is that he agrees with fine-tuning proponents on the facts: the universe’s initial conditions and constants are astonishingly special. It’s a point of departure for deeper inquiry – some see the hand of God, others seek a new physics, and Penrose has at times done both.* Stephen Hawking: Stephen Hawking (1942–2018), one of the most famous cosmologists, had a nuanced position. Early in his career, Hawking (with Penrose) proved singularity theorems that supported the idea of a beginning of time under general relativity. Later, he proposed the no-boundary quantum cosmology to remove the singular beginning. Hawking often discussed God in metaphorical terms; for example, he asked, if the no-boundary model is correct, “What place, then, for a creator?”. At the same time, as noted, Hawking openly acknowledged the appearance of fine-tuning in his popular writings. The quote from A Brief History of Time (1988) about the constants being “very finely adjusted” for life is famous. In his final book Brief Answers to the Big Questions (2018), Hawking reiterated his stance that no God is needed, arguing that laws like gravity enable a universe to create itself spontaneously. Yet, Hawking’s own awe at the complexity of the cosmos was palpable. He mused that discovering a complete theory of the universe would allow us to “know the mind of God” (speaking figuratively). In summary, Hawking’s work attempted to naturalize the origin (through physics like quantum gravity), but he never denied the remarkable order in the universe – he simply hoped that a scientific theory could ultimately account for it without invoking a deity. His South Pole analogy (that asking what came before the Big Bang is meaningless under the no-boundary proposal) is clever, but one can still question whether this truly eliminates the need for an explanation or simply reframes it . Even Hawking’s no-boundary universe, as a structured model with specific laws, raises the question “Why this structure?”. Thus Hawking’s legacy in this debate is twofold: he helped sharpen the questions (by bringing quantum cosmology to bear) and also provided memorable articulations of fine-tuning and creation issues (which theists and atheists alike cite, sometimes in their favor).* Leonard Susskind: A pioneer of string theory, Susskind has been at the forefront of advocating the multiverse as an answer to fine-tuning. He famously engaged in a public debate with other physicists (like Lee Smolin) about the legitimacy of anthropic reasoning. Susskind’s book The Cosmic Landscape argues that what looks like intelligent design is actually the result of us living in a vast multiverse with myriad different physical laws – we just happen to be in one of the rare life-compatible pockets. Susskind does not shy from acknowledging the magnitude of fine-tuning: for example, he calls the near-zero cosmological constant “likely no accident” because expecting 120 zeros of cancellation by chance is absurd. His response is that an underlying theory (like the string landscape) produces on the order of 10^500 different vacuum states, and presumably most have big cosmological constants; a few will accidentally have the small value we need, and only those universes get galaxies and observers. Thus, given a multiverse, it’s not surprising one universe is like ours. Susskind’s stance is echoed by other prominent scientists like Stephen Weinberg (who actually anticipated the small cosmological constant via an anthropic argument). One might say Susskind concedes the fine-tuning but contests the inference of design. Instead of God, he posits a kind of cosmic lottery with enough tickets that our winning ticket (a life-permitting universe) isn’t so improbable after all. Philosophically, this raises issues about falsifiability and parsimony: the multiverse is a bold hypothesis which may be impossible to test directly. Susskind and colleagues would argue we might find indirect evidence (e.g. if the constants of our universe fall in certain statistically typical ranges assuming a multiverse distribution). For now, his view remains a speculative but influential counter to the design argument. Susskind’s contributions underscore that the scientific community takes fine-tuning seriously – seriously enough to entertain ideas that just a few decades ago might have been dismissed as metaphysical. As Bernard Carr quipped, rejecting design may “force” one to accept a multiverse , and Susskind is a prime example of a scientist who chooses the multiverse route.* John D. Barrow: A renowned cosmologist who co-authored The Anthropic Cosmological Principle and wrote extensively on cosmology and philosophical implications, Barrow’s work bridges science and deeper questions. Barrow, along with Tipler, catalogued numerous instances of fine-tuning and articulated various Anthropic Principles (Weak, Strong, and even a proposed Final Anthropic Principle). Barrow’s perspective was that the anthropic principle is not so much a solution but a useful framework for thinking about why the laws of nature are as we see. He was relatively agnostic about theological conclusions – he presented the evidence that the universe fits an astonishingly narrow set of requirements for life, and he explored naturalistic explanations as well as the idea that perhaps the universe is intended to produce observers. For example, Barrow and Tipler conclude their massive 700-page survey by discussing how one might interpret this convergence of factors. They stop short of endorsing traditional theism; however, they do remark on the striking “coincidences” that make the existence of carbon-based life possible . Barrow also gave vivid illustrations: e.g., if certain nuclear resonances in carbon production (the Hoyle resonance) were even slightly different, we wouldn’t exist – something that led astrophysicist Fred Hoyle (previously an atheist) to comment that it’s as if “a superintellect has monkeyed with physics” . Barrow’s own view could be described as one of wonder and openness: he acknowledged the fine-tuning as real and scientifically intriguing, and he did not dismiss the possibility that it points to something meaningful about the universe. At the same time, he treated the anthropic principle as a methodological principle rather than a magic wand – meaning we use anthropic reasoning to guide thinking (for instance, predicting that certain parameters will be in the life-permitting range because otherwise we wouldn’t observe them), but that doesn’t ultimately answer the ontological question of “why those parameters in the first place?” . In an academic context, Barrow’s work is frequently cited to show that fine-tuning is taken seriously in cosmology, and that one can discuss it without immediately leaping to either the God conclusion or the multiverse conclusion. His nuanced stance encourages continued dialogue between physics, philosophy, and theology.Each of these thinkers engages with the data of cosmology in a way that intersects with our big questions of contingency, origin, and design. Penrose and Hawking highlight the mysteries (entropy, initial conditions, what came before) and offer different resolutions; Susskind and Weinberg represent the push for a multiverse to avoid a creator; Barrow exemplifies the reflective scientist who weighs anthropic explanations and acknowledges the profound character of the problem. Engaging with their ideas enriches our understanding and ensures that our arguments are informed by the best science available. It also shows that the conversation about God and cosmology is not occurring in an intellectual vacuum – it is happening in dialogue with cutting-edge physics and cosmological theory.Fine-Tuning (FT) and Metaphysical Necessity (MN) in a Probabilistic FrameworkHaving surveyed the relevant cosmological concepts, we can now connect these insights to the evidential variables FT and MN in our probabilistic model of God’s existence. In our logistic regression framework (introduced elsewhere in this book), each variable captures a facet of reality that might make God’s existence more or less likely . FT (Cosmological Fine-Tuning) and MN (Metaphysical Necessity) are two crucial variables grounded directly in cosmology and philosophy of existence. We will not delve into any equations or numerical assignments here (those belong to later chapters), but we will solidify what these variables mean and why they deserve a place in the model.* Cosmological Fine-Tuning (FT): This variable represents the collective evidence we discussed regarding the precise constants and initial conditions required for life.
