Conceptually, FT captures the intuition that a universe finely calibrated for life is more likely if there is a purposeful Creator than if everything arose by unguided chance . In formal terms, one can think of FT as correlating with the likelihood ratio favoring theism over atheism given a life-permitting universe. If our universe is one out of an extremely large ensemble of possible parameter settings, and if only a vanishing fraction of those possibilities allow life, then observing this universe multiplies the odds of a designing intelligence behind it (unless an atheistic multiverse is postulated to counterbalance that improbability) . In grounding FT philosophically, we lean on the Teleological Argument updated for modern science: the “purposeful order” in nature (here at a cosmic fundamental level) is seen as evidence of an ordering Mind. We have cited how even skeptical scientists recognize the fine-tuning as something that “cries out for explanation” . The FT variable is justified by this widely acknowledged fact of nature, and by the theistic interpretation that offers a robust explanation for it . When we include FT in the logistic model, we are effectively encoding the argument, “Given that the cosmos appears deliberately set up for life, it is more likely that a deliberate Agent (God) is behind it.” Of course, we remain aware of the multiverse counter-argument; later chapters will address how to factor in the possibility of a multiverse when assessing the strength of FT. But conceptually, unless and until the multiverse is confirmed with explanatory power, the fine-tuning stands as a pointer to a metaphysical designer. Thus, FT rests on solid scientific observations and has a clear rationale as evidence: if God exists and wanted to create life, we expect fine-tuning; if no God, fine-tuning is unexpected, so the datum of fine-tuning favors the God hypothesis .* Metaphysical Necessity (MN): This variable corresponds to the cosmological argument from contingency – the idea that the existence of the universe (or any contingent thing) requires an explanation in something that exists by necessity (i.e., a being that cannot not exist) . MN essentially asks: Is there a metaphysically necessary foundation to reality? If yes, that strongly suggests God (as traditionally conceived, God is a metaphysically necessary being – one whose non-existence is impossible and who provides the explanatory ultimate for everything else). If no, one is saying reality might just be a brute fact, with no ultimate reason. The MN variable = 1 can be thought of as the stance, “There must be an ultimate reason or ground for existence (a necessary being),” whereas MN = 0 would be the stance “Reality might just exist without any explanation; no necessary being is needed.” . Our discussion of Aquinas, Leibniz, and others lays the groundwork for why MN = 1 is a rational stance. Through Leibniz’s PSR, for example, we saw that if one embraces the principle that every contingent fact needs a sufficient reason, one is logically led to posit a necessary entity as the terminus of explanation . Aquinas’s and Avicenna’s arguments similarly claim that contingent beings alone cannot account for their own existence – something non-contingent (necessary) must undergird all . In simpler terms, MN reflects the age-old intuition that nothing comes from nothing, and if the universe (all contingent being) exists, there must be something that has to exist to get things started or keep things going. We include MN in the model because this intuition remains a powerful piece of evidence in philosophical theology. It is less about empirical data (unlike FT) and more about the logical structure of explanation and existence. Yet it connects deeply with cosmology: the fact that the universe had a beginning and is contingent in its properties bolsters the sense that it is not the necessary being itself. Some philosophers (e.g. Spinoza or certain naturalists) have speculated that perhaps the universe or the laws of physics are “necessary” – but this is a controversial and minority view. Typically, the universe is regarded as contingent (it might not have existed, and it doesn’t seem to exist by its own nature), which points to something beyond it that exists of its own nature . In our model, a higher weight on MN means we give credence to the cosmological argument’s premise that contingent existence is evidence for God. We ground this in the consensus of centuries of philosophy: “The existence of any contingent thing at all is evidence that a necessary being exists.” .In the logistic regression approach, we treat FT and MN as independent variables, though in truth they are related strands of cosmological reasoning – one empirical, one metaphysical. We avoid double-counting by distinguishing that FT addresses the specific character of the universe (its life-friendly calibration), while MN addresses the sheer existence of the universe (contingent vs. necessary). Both together cover the breadth of the cosmological evidence: FT deals with how the universe is (orderly, “fine-tuned”), MN deals with that the universe is (contingent). These variables are conceptually justified by our discussions above: FT is backed by contemporary physics and design arguments, MN is backed by classical metaphysics and the principle of sufficient reason. They have strong philosophical grounding in the works of Aquinas, Leibniz, and many modern defenders (e.g., Richard Swinburne and Alexander Pruss continue to defend versions of the contingency argument in today’s literature ). By including them, our model acknowledges that the cosmological realm provides some of the most formidable evidence in favor of theism – evidence that is not just scriptural or subjective, but rooted in the existence and structure of the world itself.Before proceeding, we reiterate that in this chapter we have laid a conceptual foundation. We stopped short of numerical probabilistic modeling or weighing of these factors – that will come in later chapters. Here, our goal was to establish why FT and MN matter, and what content they encapsulate. In doing so, we traced the history of cosmological arguments, reviewed modern cosmological findings (Big Bang, inflation, entropy, multiverse) and the fine-tuning debate, and saw how leading scientists interpret these issues. All this provides a rich interdisciplinary context for our probabilistic analysis. When we later assign a coefficient to the Fine-Tuning variable, we will recall the enormity of the likelihood shift suggested by a 1 in 10^120 kind of coincidence . When we consider the Metaphysical Necessity variable, we will remember the robust philosophical argumentation that a contingent universe is incomplete without a necessary foundation . In a sense, FT and MN in the model quantify the intuitions that the world looks engineered and the world requires an ultimate cause. These intuitions, now buttressed by scientific and philosophical analysis, form a crucial part of the cumulative case for God that our logistic regression framework seeks to evaluate.Conclusion: Cosmology, perhaps more than any other field, has expanded the natural theology conversation in the last century. The universe has proven to be dynamic, contingent, and astonishingly structured. This has revitalized classical arguments (like Kalam and contingency) and given birth to new ones (like fine-tuning). In our study of the probability of God’s existence, the insights from cosmology serve as a pillar: they address existence at the largest scale and most fundamental level. The contingency of the universe’s origin and the fine-tuning of its order are two sides of a coin – both pointing beyond themselves. Whether one ultimately sees the hand of God in these or not, any honest inquiry must grapple with them. As we move forward, having established Cosmology and Existence as a key theme, we will be better prepared to integrate these insights with those from morality, consciousness, and other domains, building towards a comprehensive probabilistic model. Our foundation is set: the heavens indeed declare a story, and now we have the tools to begin quantifying how that story influences the odds of God.* Aquinas, T. (1265–1274). Both “life” and “mind” present challenges to reductionistic explanations. We will examine, in Part A, how current naturalistic hypotheses attempt to explain the origin of life and evaluate their strengths and limitations, especially in terms of the information-theoretic complexity of even “simple” living systems. In Part B, we turn to consciousness and the “hard problem” of subjective experience, surveying major philosophical perspectives (materialism, dualism, panpsychism) and considering whether the existence of mind points to a primordial Mind or informational field – a theistic interpretation in which God is the ultimate source or “broadcaster” of consciousness. Throughout, we maintain a scholarly, interdisciplinary approach, citing current scientific and philosophical literature to illuminate these questions.Part A. The Origin of Biological Life and the Problem of InformationThe origin of life remains an unsolved problem in science – how did inert matter self-organize into the first living cell, a system with astounding biochemical complexity and information content? Researchers have proposed several naturalistic hypotheses for abiogenesis (life’s arising from non-life), including the RNA World, Metabolism-First scenarios, and Panspermia. Each hypothesis offers insights but also faces significant challenges. We critically evaluate these models, then examine empirical complexity metrics suggesting the spontaneous formation of a functional cell is exceedingly improbable. We also highlight the information-theoretic paradox at life’s dawn: key biological molecules (DNA and proteins) are interdependent in a classic chicken-and-egg loop that is hard to explain by unguided processes . Finally, we consider whether life’s complexity might be better explained if information (and by extension, intelligent causation) was involved ab initio.Naturalistic Hypotheses for the Origin of LifeThe “RNA World” Hypothesis: The RNA World scenario posits that early life was based on RNA molecules which served dual roles as carriers of genetic information and as catalysts (ribozymes). This hypothesis is supported by the discovery that the ribosome – the molecular machine that builds proteins – is a ribozyme; the catalytic core of the ribosome is composed of RNA, not protein . Such findings suggest an ancient stage in which RNA alone could propagate and catalyze metabolic reactions. The strength of RNA World lies in solving a key conundrum: modern cells require proteins (enzymes) to replicate DNA and express genes, yet proteins are encoded by DNA – so which came first? RNA, capable of self-replication and basic catalysis, is seen as a plausible first biomolecule that could play both roles. Laboratory experiments have indeed evolved short RNAs that can catalyze bond formation (peptide bond formation in a primitive fashion) and even self–replicate to a limited extent .Despite its appeal, the RNA World hypothesis faces significant difficulties . RNA is a complex polymer, and it is chemically unstable in the primordial conditions – it easily hydrolyzes and breaks down . The spontaneous formation of long, information-rich RNA strands is hard to explain; the building blocks (ribose sugar, nucleobases) themselves are not trivially produced in realistic prebiotic environments. Moreover, while RNA can catalyze reactions, known ribozymes have relatively limited catalytic repertoires (mostly cleavage and ligation of phosphodiester bonds). It appears that only very specific (and rare) RNA sequences have significant catalytic activity, meaning that an early RNA-based life would require the random emergence of just the right sequences – an event of low probability. As one review summarizes, objections to RNA-first include: “(i) RNA is too complex a molecule to have arisen prebiotically; (ii) RNA is inherently unstable; (iii) catalysis is a relatively rare property of long RNA sequences; and (iv) the catalytic repertoire of RNA is too limited.” . Proponents counter that intermediate stages or catalytic aid from peptides and lipids could have helped, but as of now, the RNA World, while the leading origin-of-life model, is far from a complete or fully plausible narrative. Even its advocates often acknowledge it is the “least bad” theory among alternatives, rather than an easy explanation .Metabolism-First (Chemical Cycles) Hypotheses: Another class of origin-of-life theories proposes that life began not with information-carrying polymers like RNA, but with self-sustaining metabolic networks of chemical reactions. In “metabolism-first” scenarios, simple organic molecules, perhaps on mineral surfaces or in hydrothermal vents, formed autocatalytic cycles – sets of reactions that produce the catalysts needed for those same reactions, thus reproducing the network without genetic templates. Notable examples include Günter Wächtershäuser’s iron–sulfur world hypothesis (life emerging from sulfur and iron-catalyzed reactions on mineral surfaces) and Stuart Kauffman’s concept of autocatalytic sets. These models have the advantage of not requiring large biomolecules at the start; they seek a gradual path from geochemistry to biochemistry. Such chemical cycles could, in principle, form protocells – compartments with reaction networks – that later acquired genetic molecules. Some experiments have demonstrated that certain simple metabolic cycles can arise under prebiotic conditions, lending partial support to this idea.The metabolism-first approach, however, has its own drawbacks. One major criticism is the lack of heredity: without something like DNA or RNA, how do these chemical networks store and transmit information about their composition to offspring? Proponents introduced concepts like “compound genomes” or composomes (sets of molecules whose composition can be replicated to daughter pools) as a form of rudimentary inheritance . Yet studies have shown that such networks have limited capacity for open-ended evolution. A 2010 analysis demonstrated that when “compound genomes” (ensembles of mutually catalytic molecules) grow beyond a certain complexity, they lose the very properties needed for Darwinian evolution – they do not reliably produce similar offspring compositions, essentially stalling evolution . In other words, metabolism-first models struggle to account for the emergence of true replication and variability. Additionally, while metabolism-first theories can produce interesting chemistry, it remains unclear how they would transition into the highly ordered information-bearing systems of actual biology (how to “add” genetic instructions to a self-sustaining cycle). To date, no metabolism-first model has demonstrated a realistic path to a cell-like system that can evolve greater complexity. These considerations temper the initial appeal of metabolism without genes – in practice, it seems metabolism alone cannot get evolution off the ground .Panspermia (Life from Elsewhere): Panspermia is a fundamentally different kind of hypothesis: it proposes that life did not begin on Earth at all, but was seeded from elsewhere (another planet or space). Classic panspermia (championed by figures like Fred Hoyle and Chandra Wickramasinghe) suggests that microbial life or prebiotic building blocks traveled to Earth via comets, meteorites, or interstellar dust. A variant, “directed panspermia,” was even proposed by Francis Crick and Leslie Orgel, speculating that an advanced extraterrestrial civilization might have sent life to Earth intentionally. The panspermia idea highlights that Earth’s early environment might not have been the only or best place for life’s inception – for instance, if early Earth was too hostile, maybe life first arose on Mars (which had liquid water earlier) and then hitched a ride here on a meteorite.The strength of panspermia is that it defers the hardest steps of abiogenesis to a possibly more favorable location or an earlier time in the universe. We know that organic molecules (amino acids, nucleobases) exist in space and in meteorites. Indeed, the Murchison meteorite famously contained a variety of amino acids, sugars, and other organics. If a primitive cell or spore can survive the journey (or if life originated on a planet with easier conditions), panspermia could bypass some difficulties faced by strictly Earth-bound theories. Panspermia also gains a little scientific plausibility from discoveries of extremophiles and microbial spores that can remain dormant under extreme conditions, potentially even surviving space (for example, bacterial spores have survived years on spacecraft and still revived).However, panspermia does not actually solve the origin of life problem; it only relocates it. If life came from off Earth, we still must explain how it arose wherever it came from. At best, panspermia postpones abiogenesis to an earlier time or another planet – perhaps buying time or different chemistry, but eventually, a first life must be explained. Moreover, evidence in support of panspermia remains circumstantial. No confirmed extraterrestrial organisms have been found, and the hypothesis is unfalsifiable in a strict sense (one can always say “life started elsewhere”). Most scientists treat it as a remote possibility. Fred Hoyle, who advocated panspermia largely because he found the chance origin of life on Earth implausible, famously quipped that believing life arose by chance here was like believing “a tornado sweeping through a junkyard might assemble a Boeing 747” – and thus preferred that life be pre-existing and delivered to Earth . His argument underscores the improbability issue, which we discuss next, but shifting improbability to outer space doesn’t make it vanish (and in Hoyle’s case, he and Wickramasinghe ended up positing an eternal or steady-state universe with life always present to seed new worlds). In summary, panspermia addresses where life came from, but not how. It could be part of a larger solution (life’s building blocks widespread in cosmos), yet the fundamental biogenic leap still demands explanation.Complexity and Information: The Improbability of Spontaneous LifeOne reason the origin of life is often described as enigmatic or even “miraculous” is the sheer complexity and specificity of even the simplest known life. A living cell is not a random blob of chemicals; it is an information-driven molecular system. The cell’s key molecules – nucleic acids (DNA/RNA) and proteins – form a tightly integrated code system. Modern cells store genetic information in DNA (or RNA for some viruses), and use it to produce proteins; those proteins (enzymes) in turn catalyze the replication, repair, and expression of the DNA. This leads to a famous chicken-and-egg problem: DNA cannot do anything (cannot be replicated or translated) without proteins, but the cell cannot make proteins without the instructions in DNA. As the philosopher Karl Popper succinctly put it: “What makes the origin of life and the genetic code a disturbing riddle is this: the code cannot be translated except by using certain products of its translation.” In other words, the first cell would need a minimum toolkit of molecules that all depend on each other to function, a scenario which is extremely hard to assemble by chance alone. Popper called it a “baffling, vicious circle” for any origin-of-life model .Figure 6.1: The informational interdependence in the cell’s core machinery. DNA (and RNA) carry genetic instructions, but those instructions can only be executed with the help of protein enzymes; conversely, those proteins are encoded in the DNA. This cycle (DNA ? proteins ? DNA) epitomizes the chicken-and-egg dilemma in origin-of-life research . Any origin scenario must explain how this loop could start unguided.Researchers have tried to solve this conundrum by positing simpler precursors (e.g. the RNA World suggests an RNA-based system preceded the full DNA/protein system). Yet, even granting an RNA World, the step to a DNA/RNA + protein world is formidable. Modern translation involves dozens of specialized molecules: ribosomal RNAs, ~50 ribosomal proteins, ~20 aminoacyl-tRNA synthetase enzymes (one for each amino acid), tRNAs, initiation/elongation factors, etc. – a full suite of ~100 macromolecules working in concert. In the words of biochemist Frank Salisbury, “it’s nice to talk about RNA World, but it’s a long way from a self-replicating RNA molecule to the genetic code and protein synthesis apparatus we see today.” The interdependence means many parts had to arise together, or a different self-organizing principle had to exist that somehow bootstrapped molecular information in stages.Compounding the puzzle, probabilistic analyses have suggested that the chance assembly of even one functional biomolecule of modest length by random processes is vanishingly small. For example, a typical enzyme is a protein of a few hundred amino acids arranged in a very particular sequence (which folds into a precise 3D structure). How rare are such sequences among all possible amino acid chains? Experiments by Douglas Axe and others have provided estimates. Axe (2004) found that for a protein ~150 amino acids long, only about 1 in 10^74 random sequences would fold into a stable, functional structure . In line with this, our model’s analysis cited an estimate of ~1 in 10^77 chance for a 100-amino-acid protein forming by random combination . These numbers imply that if we were to randomly draw amino acid sequences, we’d have to sample astronomically many (10^74 or more) to have a good chance of stumbling on even one functional protein. For context, 10^74 is a hundred trillion trillion trillion trillion (etc.) – vastly more than the number of atoms in the observable universe (~10^80).Origin-of-life theorist Fred Hoyle dramatically illustrated this improbability. He calculated that the odds of obtaining all the proteins necessary for a simple cell by chance is about 1 in 10^40,000 – effectively zero . He famously said: “The chance of obtaining even a single functioning protein by chance combination of amino acids” is akin to a solar system full of blind men solving Rubik’s cubes simultaneously . While modern evolutionary biology counters that natural selection can greatly increase the odds once replication exists, abiogenesis is by definition a pre-Darwinian process – before true replication and selection, one essentially relies on “chance” and geochemical necessity. Hoyle’s huge numbers were meant to show that life’s emergence by a blind search in the chemical space is wildly implausible. (Hoyle himself, as noted, took this as evidence that life must have come from extraterrestrial sources or involved some intelligence.)Empirical complexity metrics aren’t limited to proteins. The genome of the simplest free-living bacterium (like Mycoplasma) is on the order of 500,000 DNA base pairs encoding hundreds of essential genes. Even a hypothetical minimal cell might need on the order of 250–300 genes (with total DNA length >100,000 bases). The combinatorial space of nucleotide sequences of that length is astronomically large (4^100,000 possibilities). While we don’t think life searched randomly through all possibilities, the point remains that a highly specific information-rich configuration had to arise against a vast backdrop of nonfunctional alternatives. Origin-of-life expert Eugene Koonin has argued that the probability of evolving a modest RNA replicator by chance on Earth might be so low that it’s effectively zero – leading him to suggest that perhaps a multiverse of countless universes is needed for one to hit upon a replicator by luck . Koonin’s tongue-in-cheek invocation of multiple universes underscores how desperate the probability issue can seem.Given these challenges, origin-of-life research has shifted from purely random models to scenarios involving self-organization, guided energy flow, and feedback. For instance, some hypothesize that life began in far-from-equilibrium environments (thermal vents) that fostered order, or that certain surfaces catalyzed sequential reactions (clays organizing nucleotides), etc. These ideas may indeed reduce the “search space” problem somewhat. But no consensus “easy pathway” has emerged in the literature . As a 2022 review candidly stated, despite many interesting partial results, we still lack a coherent theory for a reproducible origin of a cell from chemistry – the gap between complex chemistry and simplest biology remains enormous.It is important to note that the mainstream scientific community holds that life’s complexity can ultimately be explained by natural processes (since life evidently arose). Researchers continue to investigate novel pathways (e.g., hybrid RNA-peptide worlds, networks of simple replicators, etc.). Nonetheless, the informational leap at life’s origin justifies, even among secular scientists, phrases like “life appears to be almost a miracle” – a remark by Francis Crick (who, after decades of study, marveled at how baffling the jump from chemistry to biology is). The accumulating biochemical knowledge has, if anything, underscored the magnitude of the problem. We see in life not a simple molecule or two, but a whole integrated information-processing system.The argument from biological complexity often made by theistic philosophers and Intelligent Design proponents takes these points to suggest that unguided processes are insufficient. They argue that the specified complexity (a term used to denote patterns that are both complex and independently specified or functional) in DNA, proteins, and cellular machines is a hallmark of mind, not blind matter . For example, Stephen Meyer points out that DNA is essentially digital code; based on our experience, code or information invariably comes from an intelligent source (like software from programmers, language from minds) . On this view, the origin of the genetic code and cellular information is best explained by a designing intelligence (God) rather than by unguided chemistry. Similarly, biochemist Michael Behe has highlighted “irreducibly complex” molecular machines (like the bacterial flagellum or ATP synthase) which require multiple parts to be present to function, arguing they could not arise by stepwise Darwinian paths . While such claims are controversial and often criticized within biology (with opponents suggesting potential evolutionary routes or accusing ID of a god-of-the-gaps), the core intuition resonates with the findings above: the probabilistic resources of the universe seem insufficient to randomly assemble the required molecular toolkit for life, suggesting that chance plus law might not tell the whole story.In summary, Part A has shown that life’s origin is a deep puzzle. The leading hypotheses – RNA-first, metabolism-first, etc. – each explain certain aspects (e.g., RNA World addresses the information/catalysis duality, metabolism-first addresses how energy and networks might form), but none fully overcomes the information hurdle. Empirical estimates (Hoyle’s 1 in 10^40,000 for a cell, Axe’s 1 in 10^74–10^77 for a single protein) cast doubt on purely stochastic beginnings . And the very nature of the cell’s information flow, with its interdependent cycles (DNA ? protein), suggests a circular causality hard to unwind into a linear origin narrative . These realities have led some to entertain that something else might be at play – perhaps a principle of self-organization we’ve yet to discover, or, as theists argue, the guiding hand of a Creator implanting information into matter. In the context of a probabilistic model for God’s existence, biological complexity (BC) can be seen as positive evidence if one expects that a Creator’s involvement would dramatically raise the probability of life existing. Indeed, if life’s emergence by unguided means is near-impossible, then the actual existence of life (especially intelligent life capable of doing science) might indicate that it was intentionally arranged. As our logistic model earlier reflected, we assign BC a positive weight: the world of biology looks finely tuned or information-infused for life .Before drawing conclusions, however, we now turn to Part B – consciousness – which presents a different kind of complexity: not complexity of structure or information in physical arrangements, but the perplexing existence of subjective experience. The “gap” between mind and matter has long been noted, and here too we will find arguments that point to something beyond reductive materialism. We will explore whether consciousness might be another clue, perhaps even more intimately, of a primordial Mind behind reality.Part B. The Emergence and Nature of ConsciousnessConsciousness – the fact that we have first-person subjective experiences – is often called the most mysterious phenomenon in nature. In philosophy of mind, this is epitomized by the “hard problem of consciousness,” a term coined by David Chalmers. The hard problem asks: Why and how do physical processes in the brain give rise to the felt quality of experience (the qualia – the redness of red, the pain of a headache, the taste of chocolate)? In contrast, “easy” problems of consciousness (which are not actually easy) involve explaining cognitive functions or behaviors: how the brain discriminates stimuli, integrates information, produces reports, etc. Neuroscience has made progress on these cognitive aspects, identifying neural correlates of consciousness (NCCs) for various mental states. But even if we map every neuron and every electrical spike, there seems to remain an explanatory gap (a term by Joseph Levine) between the objective description of brain processes and the subjective feeling of being someone undergoing those processes .Why is this a gap? Because objective science, by design, deals with third-person observable data – it can tell me which brain region lights up when I see red, but it cannot access the first-person perspective of what it is like for me to experience red. As philosopher Thomas Nagel famously illustrated in his essay “What is it like to be a bat?” (1974), an organism has conscious mental states if and only if there is something it is like to be that organism. No matter how much we know about a bat’s sonar system and brain, Nagel argued, we cannot know what it is like for the bat to experience the world, because that is inherently subjective. This line of reasoning suggests consciousness might not be fully capturable by physical description alone.The Hard Problem and the “Consciousness Gap”The “Consciousness Gap” refers to the difficulty of explaining subjective awareness in purely physical terms . Chalmers highlighted that even if we had a “brain theory of everything” detailing how every function is performed, we could still ask: why do those functions have an inner life? We could imagine a world of “philosophical zombies” – beings that behave exactly like humans and have identical brains, yet have no conscious experience inside. Nothing in the standard physical story seems to rule that out, which implies that our theories are missing an essential component – the component that turns on the lights of consciousness, so to speak. This motivates views that consciousness might be a fundamental property of reality (a view Chalmers entertains, sometimes called naturalistic dualism or a form of panpsychism).From a theistic perspective, the existence of consciousness is often seen as evidence of a reality beyond matter. Many philosophers (including dualists like Richard Swinburne and J.P. Moreland) argue: If God is a mind, it’s not surprising that minds (conscious beings) exist; but if the universe began as solely physical with no mind, the rise of conscious persons is utterly astonishing . Swinburne, for example, has posited that conscious souls are a kind of “halos” of brain bodies, something not deducible from physics but expected if a divine Mind breathes spirit into the world. Moreland (2008) explicitly contends that finite consciousness is best explained by the existence of a necessarily existent ultimate Consciousness (God) – arguing that if you start with an eternal Mind, the appearance of other minds makes sense, whereas starting with only matter and energy, the emergence of consciousness is inexplicable .Empirically, what do we know? We know that certain brain states reliably correlate with conscious states (for instance, specific oscillatory patterns in the thalamocortical circuits correlate with wakeful consciousness, whereas their disruption correlates with unconsciousness). We know that anesthetics, brain injuries, etc., can remove or alter consciousness, indicating that the brain is in some way the seat of mind. These findings strongly support that there is a dependence of consciousness on the brain’s physical processes. However, dependence is not the same as ontological identity or explanatory reduction. The hard problem reminds us that correlation is not explanation: identifying a neural correlate of the feeling of pain doesn’t tell us why that neural firing should be felt as pain. Thus, many philosophers and even some scientists hold that there is a “gap” in our scientific worldview concerning consciousness . This gap has prompted a variety of responses and theories, which we now survey.Philosophical Views of Consciousness: Materialism, Dualism, and PanpsychismMaterialism / Physicalism: The default assumption in much of science is physicalism – the doctrine that only physical things exist fundamentally, so consciousness must ultimately be a state or function of the physical brain. Under materialism, mind is typically seen as an emergent property of complex information processing. Many cognitive scientists adopt this view, seeking to explain consciousness in terms of neural networks, computations, or information integration. Some, like philosopher Daniel Dennett, even argue that consciousness is not something over and above those functions – instead, it just is the myriad brain processes, or even an “illusion” created by them (Dennett provocatively titled his 1991 book Consciousness Explained, but critics say it attempts to explain it away). The materialist approach has certainly been fruitful for neuroscience: it treats subjective experiences as arising from neural correlates that can be studied. For example, studies show that stimulating certain brain regions can induce experiences (e.g., electrical stimulation in the temporal lobe can trigger déjà vu or even “out-of-body” sensations), and conversely, damage to specific areas can erase certain aspects of consciousness (e.g., prosopagnosia – the loss of ability to recognize faces – results from injury to the fusiform face area). Such findings are often cited as evidence that the brain produces consciousness, much like a generator produces electricity.However, despite tremendous progress mapping brain activity, materialism faces criticism for not actually solving the hard problem. The explanatory gap remains: a detailed neural story still begs the question of why it is accompanied by experience. Materialists often reply that maybe our intuitions mislead us – perhaps if we understood the brain fully, the intuition of a gap would fade. Or they might invoke emergence: consciousness emerges when systems hit a threshold of complexity (similar to how wetness emerges from H?O molecules, although that analogy is imperfect because we can see how wetness is just collective behavior of molecules, whereas we cannot yet see how subjective feeling is just collective neuron behavior). Another rejoinder is illusionism – the claim that our inner feeling of an explanatory gap is itself a cognitive illusion and that when we say “there is something it is like,” that is not a scientifically meaningful fact, just a quirk of our introspection. Such positions are debated fiercely. Materialists also face the challenge of intentionality (how brain states can be about things, have meaning or reference), and reason (the argument by e.g. Alvin Plantinga that if minds are just products of evolution for survival, there is no reason they should track truth reliably – a point some use to argue that pure naturalism is self-defeating ). Despite these issues, materialism remains the predominant stance in neuroscience, largely because it is pragmatically useful and avoids invoking unverifiable entities. Yet, as Nagel (an atheist himself) pointed out in Mind and Cosmos (2012), the materialist neo-Darwinian framework may be fundamentally incomplete because it cannot account for the reality of mind and consciousness . Nagel suggested that new principles (beyond physical ones as currently understood) might be needed – a bold statement that drew considerable attention .Dualism: Dualism in philosophy of mind is the view that mind and matter are two fundamentally different kinds of substances or properties. The classic form is Cartesian dualism (after Descartes), where the mind or soul is a non-physical substance that interacts with the physical brain. In the 20th century, dualist views were refined and defended by thinkers like Karl Popper and Sir John Eccles, and by philosophical theologians such as Richard Swinburne and J.P. Moreland. Popper and Eccles co-authored The Self and Its Brain (1977), where Popper outlined a tripartite ontology (material world, subjective world, and world of objective knowledge) and Eccles, a neuroscientist, proposed a mechanism for mind-brain interaction. Eccles suggested that mental events influence brain activity via subtle effects on synapses, even speculating a particle he called the “psychon” that could transmit mind’s influence to neurons . This was an attempt to make dualism scientifically respectable by proposing an interaction channel. While the psychon is hypothetical, Eccles’ efforts underscore that dualists acknowledge the interaction problem (how something non-material could affect the material).The arguments for dualism often appeal to the irreducibility of subjective experience and the unity of consciousness. One classical argument is that one can conceive of one’s mind existing without one’s body (e.g., in an afterlife, or like Descartes’ evil demon scenario), but one cannot conceive of a body (brain) without any mind and yet claim to be oneself – suggesting mind has a logically independent existence. Another point is that mental states have properties (like “being about something”, or having a subjective perspective) that no physical state can have, ergo they belong to different categories. Thomas Nagel and others also note that an objective, third-person description will always leave out the first-person perspective – implying a gap in principle. Dualists also argue that free will or rationality would be impossible if we are entirely physical, since then our thoughts would be determined by biochemical laws rather than reasons. Even some physicists (like Sir Roger Penrose) who are not dualists per se have flirted with non-standard models where consciousness is not just an emergent computation, suggesting e.g. quantum processes could be involved (Penrose’s OR theory with Stuart Hameroff, though speculative, tries to find a physicalistic but non-computational basis for consciousness).Critics of dualism point out its chief weakness: the interaction issue – if mind and matter are so different, how do they causally interact without violating physical conservation laws? If every physical event has a sufficient physical cause (the closure of physics), there’s no “gap” for a non-physical mind to push atoms around. This was a point raised by many scientists: dualism, they say, either conflicts with physics or makes the mind causally inert (epiphenomenal). Popper and Eccles tried to circumvent this by suggesting perhaps some quantum indeterminacies in synapses could be biased by mind (so not violating energy conservation but picking outcomes). This remains highly speculative and not empirically demonstrated. Another criticism is that dualism can appear to “multiply mysteries” – we end up having two unexplained things (physical and mental substances) and a problematic interface, whereas an ideal theory might reduce mysteries by explaining one in terms of the other. Nonetheless, dualism remains a respectable position in philosophy of mind, especially property dualism which suggests that while there is only one kind of substance (physical), it has two kinds of properties: physical properties and mental properties. Chalmers himself sometimes leans this way, calling consciousness a fundamental property of the world (like space, time, mass, etc. – adding consciousness to the list of basics). This is a form of non-reductive physicalism or dual aspect theory.Panpsychism and Idealist Approaches: A once-fringe idea gaining renewed interest is panpsychism – the view that consciousness (or protoconsciousness) is a fundamental and ubiquitous feature of the physical world. In panpsychism, even elementary particles or fields have extremely simple forms of experience or “mind-like” quality. These rudimentary minds combine in larger systems, and under the right organizational structure (like a brain), yield the complex consciousness we know. Chalmers, after being convinced the hard problem is real, has entertained panpsychism as a possible solution: if consciousness is fundamental, we don’t need to “derive” it from non-conscious matter; instead, we only need to explain how simple conscious units combine into complex ones – known as the combination problem (for instance, if electrons have experiences, how do many tiny experiences combine to form the unitary experience of a human? This is a nontrivial puzzle). Nonetheless, panpsychism has the attractive feature of not treating consciousness as an anomaly, but as something built into the fabric of reality (making it more natural that minds exist). Some philosophers like Galen Strawson and physicist Arthur Eddington have advocated forms of panpsychism (Eddington in 1920s suggested that since physics only tells us about structure of matter, the intrinsic nature of matter could well be mental). Modern theories like Integrated Information Theory (IIT) by Giulio Tononi, though not explicitly panpsychist, imply a continuum of consciousness (a system that integrates information will have a non-zero measure of consciousness ?, even a bit in a photodiode has an extremely tiny ?). IIT has predicted, for example, that even simple systems have flickers of experience – an idea quite aligned with panpsychism.Another perspective, often associated with some interpretations of quantum mechanics and Eastern philosophies, is a form of idealism or neutral monism – the idea that consciousness (or an underlying field that is neither material nor mental in our usual sense) is primary, and the physical world might in some sense emerge from it. Bernardo Kastrup and others have recently argued for an idealist ontology where the apparent physical world is like a “dashboard” representation of an underlying mental reality. While these views are outside mainstream empirical science, they are receiving serious philosophical consideration because materialist models haven’t solved the core issues. Even physicist Sir Roger Penrose mused that consciousness might be “missing” from our scientific picture in a way analogous to how electromagnetism was missing before we discovered it – suggesting maybe new physics could reveal a “mental aspect” of nature.Thomas Nagel in Mind and Cosmos stops short of endorsing panpsychism but suggests a form of teleological naturalism, where nature has a propensity towards the development of minds (a built-in bias, so to speak, to produce consciousness and cognition) . He considers that current materialism cannot account for the reality of subjective and objective mental capacities, so something fundamental is missing – if not a deity, perhaps a fundamental principle or proto-mental aspect of nature.In sum, there is a spectrum of views: at one end strict materialism (mind is brain activity, full stop), in the middle property dualism or emergentism (mind arises from matter but in a way that introduces fundamentally new properties), further along dualism (mind and matter distinct), and at the far end panpsychism/idealism (mind is pervasively fundamental, or even the ground of being). Each view has arguments for and against, and the debate is ongoing. What is clear is that phenomenal consciousness (sentience) is not straightforwardly reducible to known physical concepts, which is why the topic is sometimes dubbed the “hard problem” in contrast to other scientific problems.Consciousness as Evidence of a Primordial MindIs consciousness a clue that the ultimate nature of reality is mental or informational? Many thinkers across disciplines have answered yes – positing that individual minds might be manifestations of a deeper, unitary Mind. In a theistic framework, this aligns with the idea that human minds are made in the “image” of a divine Mind, or that our consciousness is a spark of the divine consciousness. Here, we interpret consciousness as a plausible indicator of a primordial mind (God) in line with an informational model of reality.One way to conceptualize this is the “brain as receiver” model of consciousness. This model, associated with figures like William James, Henri Bergson, and more recently promoted by Eccles and others, suggests that the brain does not produce consciousness the way a factory produces a product, but rather transmits or filters an underlying consciousness field, much as a radio receiver tunes into broadcast signals . William James in 1890 proposed that consciousness might be to the brain as light is to a prism or radio waves to a receiver – the brain filters and limits a fundamental consciousness, shaping it into our individual mind, but the source of consciousness is non-local . In this transmissive model, physical brain damage or anesthesia doesn’t annihilate consciousness per se, but it disrupts the receiver, so the manifestation of consciousness (in that body) fades. This is consistent with, for example, near-death experience reports where consciousness is claimed to continue despite minimal brain activity – though such reports are controversial, they are intriguing in this context.Neuroscientist John Eccles’ “psychon” hypothesis can be seen as an attempt to give the receiver model some scientific bite: he imagined a field of mental units (“psychons”) that interact with brain neurons . If one were to put this in modern physics terms, one might speculate about unknown forces or quantum states interacting with the brain. The MDPI article by Rouleau & Cimino (2022) we reviewed highlights that brains do emit and absorb electromagnetic signals and that these could play a role in a transmissive interaction . While there is no consensus on any of this, the theoretical possibility remains open that consciousness might be an inherent part of the fabric of reality that brains access, rather than manufacture.From a theological perspective, one could say that God, as the ultimate Mind, “broadcasts” a sort of consciousness or rationality into the cosmos, and brains (when suitably organized) pick up this broadcast and channel it into individual self-awareness. In this analogy, God is like a transmitter and each mind is a receiver tuned to that signal. The content of consciousness (our memories, personality) is partly “local” (dependent on our brain structures) but the basic light of awareness could be “non-local” (stemming from the divine source). This view resonates with notions in philosophies of panentheism (God in all, and all in God) or the idea of logos (divine mind/order) permeating nature.(MISSING FIGURE 6.2)Figure 6.2: Conceptual model of consciousness as a received signal. In this depiction, an all-pervading Primordial Mind or global consciousness (analogous to God as the source) transmits an “informational signal” of consciousness, which is picked up or filtered by the human brain/mind (the receiver). This model is in line with transmissive theories where the brain limits and shapes a fundamental consciousness field into individual subjective experiences . Such a view interprets individual conscious minds as derivations from or participations in a universal Mind.Philosophers David Chalmers and Thomas Nagel, while not theists, have edged toward seeing consciousness as fundamental. Chalmers mused about a form of panprotopsychism: perhaps information has two aspects, physical and experiential, and whenever information is processed (even in simple systems) there is a glimmer of experience. This is sometimes called a dual-aspect theory of information. Interestingly, if one takes the informational model seriously, one could say that God is the ultimate information or logos underlying reality, and our consciousness is a tuned-in pattern of that information. The chapter epigraph in the Gospel of John, “In the beginning was the Word (Logos)… and the Word was God,” can be read through this lens: reality’s foundation is informational (a Mind), and all things (including life and mind) came to be through this Logos.Contemporary philosopher Stephen Priest (2024) goes so far as to argue from quantum mechanics to a form of idealism: he suggests that to avoid the paradoxes of quantum observation, one must posit a “global consciousness” that has all the essential properties of God . According to Priest, consciousness did not evolve from matter; rather matter and quantum events presuppose a cosmic consciousness in which they’re embedded . This is a bold thesis, but it shows the direction of some interdisciplinary thinking: physics and consciousness studies may be converging to a picture where mind is fundamental. If such a view holds, then individual minds (like ours) are evidence of – or at least consistent with – an underlying universal Mind. In a probabilistic argument for God’s existence, the consciousness variable (CG) thus carries weight: the existence of conscious, reasoning beings is more expected if a supreme Mind intended and produced minds, and less expected if the universe is wholly mindless at bottom .Even setting aside speculative models, we can make a modest inference: if one finds the hard problem truly hard (i.e. if one doubts materialism will ever fully explain qualia), and if one notes that conscious minds fit nicely in a worldview where Mind is primary, then consciousness provides indirect evidence for such a worldview. Philosopher C.S. Lewis and Alvin Plantinga have argued the “Argument from Reason” along these lines: that our capability for rational insight is best explained if a rational Creator made our minds, rather than them being accidental by-products of mindless nature . While not everyone is convinced by these arguments, they are part of the cumulative case that consciousness raises profound questions about the nature of reality.ConclusionLife and Mind – the phenomena of biological complexity and consciousness – pose significant explanatory challenges for naturalistic frameworks. In Part A, we saw that the origin of life requires vast amounts of functionally specified information to arise in a short window of time, a scenario that current scientific theories struggle to account for without invoking extremely improbable chance events or unknown principles. In Part B, we saw that consciousness confronts us with a reality (subjectivity, qualia, intentionality) that does not straightforwardly follow from a purely material picture of the world. Across both domains, an informational model that includes a primordial intelligent source (God) offers a unifying perspective: life’s information-rich systems and our conscious minds are not anomalies, but expected outcomes if the foundation of reality is informational and mental.This does not mean science has failed; rather, it highlights that science may be pointing beyond itself. As we incorporate these insights into our probabilistic model for God’s existence, Biological Complexity (BC) and the Consciousness Gap (CG) emerge as two evidential factors favoring the God hypothesis. A finely-tuned, information-loaded first cell is more likely if a Designer intended life . The existence of conscious persons capable of understanding that information is more likely if a supreme Consciousness set the stage . While much remains unknown, the trajectory of research in both origin-of-life studies and consciousness studies keeps these age-old questions at the forefront. In the spirit of interdisciplinary rigor, we remain open to multiple hypotheses, but we also acknowledge that theism provides a coherent and perhaps compelling explanatory context for why the universe contains both intricate molecular codes and conscious code-breakers. Life and mind, on this view, are reflections of the creative mind of God, manifested in physical form.In the next chapters, we will continue to examine other domains (from cosmological fine-tuning to moral truths and quantum mysteries) to see how they further inform this probabilistic model of divine existence. Each piece, including the ones discussed here, contributes to an overarching inference to the best explanation: a cosmos with life and consciousness may be far more than a cosmic accident – it may be the intentional work of a Logos that has woven order and mind into the very fabric of reality.References (Harvard Style)(Selected sources as cited above)Bernhardt, H.
