A well-known formulation of this argument is presented by William Lane Craig. In its classical form, Craig’s moral argument is often summarized as follows:1. Premise 1: If God does not exist, then objective moral values and duties do not exist.2. Premise 2: Objective moral values and duties do exist.3. Conclusion: Therefore, God exists.Craig and other theistic philosophers defend Premise 1 by arguing that without a transcendent lawgiver or a divine foundation, morality would have no absolute grounding – moral principles would be merely human inventions with no binding authority . For example, Craig cites atheist ethicist Richard Taylor’s observation that in a world without God, the concept of moral obligation would be unintelligible: “the concept of moral obligation is… unintelligible apart from the idea of God. The words remain, but their meaning is gone” (Taylor, 1996, p. 83). In other words, if there is no higher moral lawgiver, then moral “laws” would be nothing more than societal or evolutionary preferences, lacking any objective ought-force. As Ivan Karamazov (a character in Dostoevsky’s novel) famously conjectured, if there is no God, everything is permitted. Many atheists themselves have agreed, at least in part, with this conditional: if God does not exist, objective morality would be difficult to account for. Philosophers J. L. Mackie and Michael Ruse, for instance, concede that a purely naturalistic worldview struggles to underpin objective moral truths (Mackie, 1977; Ruse, 1989). We will examine their views shortly.Premise 2 asserts that there are objective moral values and duties – that some actions (like torturing an innocent child) are truly wrong, and some values (like love or justice) are truly good, regardless of human opinion. Defenders of the argument maintain that our intuitive and pervasive belief in some moral absolutes is well-founded. They often appeal to common moral experience: virtually all humans apprehend a core moral law (e.g. that gratuitous cruelty is wrong), which suggests that morality is not purely subjective. Craig contends that objective moral values are “properly basic” truths that we apprehend directly, much as we perceive the external world (Craig, 2008). To deny Premise 2, one must embrace moral skepticism or relativism to such an extreme that even horrendous evils could not be condemned as objectively wrong – a price that few are willing to pay. Thus, the majority of people (including many atheists) do believe there are at least some objective moral facts or obligations, lending support to Premise 2. Craig frequently notes that even outspoken atheist philosophers often affirm an objective moral realm (for example, atheist Louise Antony writes: “any argument for moral skepticism is going to depend on premises which are less obvious than the reality of moral values themselves” (Antony, 2007, p. 67), thereby upholding Premise 2).Given these two premises, the conclusion logically follows that God exists. The argument does not claim that atheists cannot live moral lives; rather, it argues that without God as a metaphysical foundation, moral truths would have no objective status. In Craig’s words, “On the atheistic view, there’s nothing special about human beings – they’re just accidental by-products of nature. … If God does not exist, we are left with an apelike creature with a morality ingrained by evolution and social conditioning – and that does not constitute objective morality” (Craig, 2010, p. 172). The moral argument, in summary, posits that moral order in the universe – the fact that we apprehend real rights and wrongs – is evidence of a moral Orderer (God).This classical argument has sparked extensive debate. To rigorously analyze it, we must consider a range of meta-ethical perspectives and objections. What does it mean for moral values to be “objective,” and can such objectivity exist in a secular framework? We turn now to an overview of positions on the nature of morality, drawing from both theistic and secular scholars.Meta-Ethical Perspectives: Moral Realism, Anti-Realism, and Theistic EthicsThe crux of the moral argument is the claim that objective moral values (“moral objectivity”) exist and are better explained by God than by naturalistic processes. Philosophers have proposed different meta-ethical theories regarding the status of moral values:* Moral Realism: The view that there are objective moral facts or truths that do not depend on human opinion. Moral realists hold that statements like “murder is wrong” can be true in a mind-independent way. Some moral realists are theists who believe these truths are grounded in God, while others are secular moral realists who attempt to ground moral facts in a non-theistic way (e.g. in some Platonic realm of moral values, or as brute facts of the natural world).* Moral Anti-Realism: The umbrella term for views that deny objective moral facts. This category includes error theory (which claims moral statements are systematically false because there are no moral facts), non-cognitivism (which says moral statements are not truth-apt at all but express emotions or prescriptions), and relativism (which says moral truth is relative to cultures or individuals). An anti-realist would say morality is ultimately a human construct or evolutionary by-product with no independent reality.* Divine Command Theory (DCT): A theistic meta-ethical view (a form of moral realism) which holds that moral facts are grounded in God’s commands or character. The classic DCT claim is that an action is morally wrong if and only if it is contrary to God’s commands. Robert Adams, a prominent defender of a refined DCT, suggests that moral obligations are rooted not in arbitrary fiats but in the loving nature of God – for Adams, “ethically wrong” means being contrary to the commands of a loving God (Adams, 1979). This attempts to avoid the well-known Euthyphro dilemma by anchoring goodness in God’s character: God’s nature is supremely good, so He would not command cruelty for its own sake; moral goodness is neither above God (independent of Him) nor arbitrary, but an expression of His very being (Adams, 1999).* Evolutionary Ethics: A naturalistic view explaining moral feelings and behaviors as products of evolution. This perspective, advocated by thinkers like biologist E. O. Wilson and philosopher Michael Ruse, holds that our moral intuitions evolved because they had survival value for our ancestors (for instance, cooperation and altruism helped tribes survive). On this view, moral sentiments are comparable to other evolved traits – useful for reproduction, but not necessarily true in any objective sense beyond that. Ruse famously writes that morality is “a biological adaptation no less than are hands and feet and teeth… Considered as a rationally justifiable set of claims about an objective something, ethics is illusory” – useful for survival, “and any deeper meaning is illusory” (Ruse, 1989, pp. 268–269). In short, if our sense of right and wrong is just an adaptive illusion favored by natural selection, then it would seem there is no objective moral truth; morality would be an evolutionary by-product rather than a divine law.Each of these perspectives yields a different evaluation of Craig’s moral argument. Theistic moral realists (like Craig or Adams) accept that objective morals exist and see God as their source. Secular moral realists accept objective morals but propose a non-theistic ground for them, thereby challenging Premise 1 of Craig’s argument. Moral anti-realists challenge Premise 2 by denying genuine objective morals, though often at the cost of biting the bullet on our deepest moral intuitions.Notably, a number of atheist philosophers have explicitly endorsed the idea behind Premise 1 – that if atheism is true, objective morality would be unlikely. J. L. Mackie, an influential atheist philosopher, argued exactly this point: the very objectivity of moral values, if it existed, would be “queer” or utterly inexplicable in a naturalistic universe, so Mackie concluded that it’s more reasonable to believe no such objective values exist at all (Mackie, 1977). In Ethics: Inventing Right and Wrong, Mackie flatly states “there are no objective values” (1977, p. 15), maintaining that any theory positing their existence faces a formidable “argument from queerness” – the idea that objective prescriptive facts would be entities of a very strange sort, unlike anything else in the universe or our knowledge faculties. Mackie’s stance is a moral error theory: our moral judgments purport to be objective, but all such judgments are false because there are no objective values in reality. Michael Ruse takes a similar line from an evolutionary standpoint: our moral sense is explained by evolution, and it leads us to feel morality is objective, but this feeling is an illusion fostered by our genes . If one accepts Mackie’s or Ruse’s premises (naturalism and evolution without any guiding deity), then indeed objective morality would lack a foundation, making moral anti-realism a plausible outcome on thoroughgoing naturalism.On the other hand, secular moral realists push back against the notion that God is needed for ethics. Philosophers like Louise Antony and others (e.g. Erik Wielenberg, Russ Shafer-Landau) argue that moral truths can exist as fundamental features of reality without any deity. Antony explicitly denies that “there would be no morality without God,” maintaining that moral truths are neither derived from theism nor dependent on religious belief (Antony, 2009). In her essay “Atheism as Perfect Piety,” she contends that linking morality too closely with God can even distort moral reasoning – morality should stand on its own rational ground (Antony, 2009). Secular realists may appeal to human reason, experience, or even some non-theistic form of Platonism: for instance, one might hold that moral values like justice or kindness just exist necessarily or emerge from facts about human well-being. They accuse theists of understating the resources of a naturalistic worldview – perhaps objective moral principles could be embedded in the natural order in ways we do not yet fully understand, or are inherent in rational agency. After all, even without belief in God, people generally can recognize cruelty as wrong and kindness as good, which suggests moral objectivity does not require theistic belief in practice (a point often raised to show one can be “good without God”). The debate then centers on whether a naturalistic ontology (one without anything like a God or transcendent moral lawgiver) can ontologically ground moral truths.The Challenge of Moral Objectivity on NaturalismDespite the efforts of secular moral realists, many thinkers (including atheists like Mackie, and theists like Craig) maintain that moral objectivity is difficult to ground on naturalism. The crux of the challenge is an explanatory gap: naturalism typically recognizes only physical facts or natural facts (like biological or psychological facts), and it’s unclear how evaluative facts (like “murder is wrong”) could arise from purely descriptive facts about particles, chemistry, and evolution. This is related to G. E. Moore’s classic “naturalistic fallacy” critique – the idea that one cannot simply derive an “ought” from an “is.” If humans are, in the final analysis, complex biochemical machines produced by blind evolutionary forces, where would binding moral obligations come from? As Craig puts it, on naturalism “we’re just relatively advanced primates; how can moral duties exist for apes or primates? Who or what lays such duties on us?” (Craig, 2013). Moral norms have an incumbent authority – a prescriptive force that compels us. Many philosophers argue that such normative force makes sense if morality is grounded in a supreme personal source (a divine Lawgiver to whom we are accountable), but seems odd if we are accountable only to impersonal nature.Moreover, naturalistic accounts of morality often reduce moral values to something else – for example, to evolutionary advantage, or to expressions of emotion (as in emotivism), or to cultural consensus. These reductions tend to undermine the objectivity of morals. If what we call “good” is just what promotes survival, then it is not objectively good in the moral sense, it’s merely evolutionarily advantageous. If “wrong” just means “socially disapproved,” then a different society could define it away. The fear of many theists and moral realists is that naturalism leads to a kind of moral nihilism or at best relativism: nothing is truly right or wrong by nature, we only have preferences or ingrained instincts. Philosopher Michael Ruse candidly states this implication: “Morality is just an aid to survival and reproduction… and any deeper meaning is illusory” (Ruse, 1989, p. 268). Under strict naturalism, altruism and self-sacrifice might be admired traits because of their social utility, but asking whether they are truly good in an objective sense might be like asking whether a tree is truly good or evil – it’s a category mistake.It is telling that some outspoken atheist thinkers have accepted that without a moral God, morality loses some crucial aspect. For instance, Friedrich Nietzsche, though not mentioned above, famously recognized that the “death of God” in European culture would erode the foundations of all traditional moral values, leading to moral revaluation. And more recently, moral philosopher Louise Antony (an atheist herself) admitted, “I’m not comfortable with moral realism if atheism is true, but I know I can’t be comfortable with the alternatives either” (paraphrasing Antony’s remarks on the tension facing an atheist who believes in real moral obligations). This tension illustrates why the moral argument remains a lively topic: neither denying moral objectivity nor affirming it without a transcendent ground is entirely satisfying for a naturalist.The theistic perspective offers a tidy solution: moral truths are grounded in God’s nature or will, making them binding and real. God’s perfectly good nature provides an objective reference point for moral values (what is good is an expression of God’s character), and God’s commandments to humans establish objective moral duties (what we ought to do). Thus, theism can explain why moral values feel authoritative – they ultimately come from the highest authority. By contrast, a secular view must either find some impersonal source of authority (which is hard to conceptualize – how can a set of brute facts oblige me, if those facts are indifferent to my actions?) or accept that moral “authority” is a human construct (which undercuts objectivity). As moral philosopher J. P. Moreland quips, expecting moral laws without a moral Lawgiver is like expecting legislative laws without a legislature.It’s important to note that not all agree with this assessment; secular ethicists continue to develop theories of objective ethics without God, and some theistic ethicists acknowledge that if moral realism is true, perhaps it could be a basic fact not requiring further explanation (similar to mathematical truths). Nevertheless, the difficulty of accounting for moral objectivity on naturalism is widely recognized in the literature. This difficulty is a key premise supporting the moral argument for God.(MISSING FIGURE 7.1)Figure 7.1: Comparative ability to ground moral objectivity in theistic vs. naturalistic worldviews. Theism posits a personal source (God) that can serve as an ontological foundation for objective moral values and duties, whereas a strict naturalistic view lacks an obvious basis for objective morality. As a result, many philosophers (including some atheists like Mackie and Ruse) have found moral objectivity to be far more at home in a theistic universe than in a purely natural one.Moral Objectivity as Evidence for TheismGiven the above, we can see why moral objectivity (MO) is often presented as evidence in favor of God’s existence. If indeed objective moral values and obligations exist (a claim supported by our common moral experience and intuitions) and if these phenomena are much more likely in a world with God than in a world without God, then the existence of such moral facts is probabilistic evidence for God. In Bayesian terms, we would argue that the probability of observing genuine moral obligations and values is higher on the hypothesis “God exists” than on the hypothesis “God does not exist.” Therefore, the observation that humans universally recognize some moral principles (like “murder is wrong” or “kindness is good”) confirms theism to some degree.We can formalize this intuitively. Let MO be the proposition that objective moral facts exist in our world. The moral argument asserts something like:* P(MO | God) >> P(MO | no God).In a simplified probabilistic model, if P(MO | God) is close to 1 (because if a perfectly good God exists, it’s very plausible he would create a moral order in which his creatures have genuine moral responsibilities) and P(MO | no God) is quite low (because a universe of only atoms and natural forces has no obvious reason to contain moral truths), then observing MO significantly boosts the probability of God’s existence. In a logistic regression model of cumulative arguments (as used in this book), Moral Objectivity (MO) would carry a positive coefficient indicating it pushes the odds upward for God. Indeed, in our compiled model of evidences, we assign a substantial positive weight to MO. This coefficient represents the strength of the inference from “objective morals exist” to “God exists.” It encapsulates the support drawn from the moral realm: the reality of moral laws is seen as pointing to a Moral Lawgiver.From a philosophical justification standpoint, this positive weighting is backed by numerous scholarly arguments: for example, Robert Adams argues that the existence of an objective moral order “makes the most sense if there is a God who created us to know and live by that moral order” (Adams, 1999). William Lane Craig and Mark Linville have contended that if atheism were true, it would be an unlikely coincidence for blind evolution to instill in us deeply felt moral convictions that correspond to no independent moral reality – whereas if theism is true, it’s natural that our moral intuitions would align with real moral truths (since God would design us with a conscience to know the good) . Even some secular philosophers, while maintaining their unbelief, admit that theism would provide an elegant explanation for moral facts. For instance, renowned atheist philosopher J. L. Mackie conceded that if objective morals exist, that does constitute a sort of argument for the existence of “something like a god” as their source (Mackie, 1982, p. 115) – he simply chose to believe no such morals exist to avoid that implication.Of course, not everyone agrees that moral objectivity does require God. Opponents of the moral argument (like Louise Antony or Erik Wielenberg) would contend that P(MO | no God) is not as low as Craig and others think – perhaps moral truths could be necessary truths or emergent features of nature. They would downplay the evidential gap. However, after surveying the meta-ethical landscape, our analysis finds that theism provides a uniquely robust foundation for moral objectivity, one that naturalism struggles to replicate. Thus, we conclude that MO is indeed a positive evidential factor for the existence of God. In our cumulative model, moral objectivity is treated as a binary variable (present in our world, value = 1) that contributes a positive log-odds increment to belief in God. Concretely, we estimated a coefficient on the order of +1.0 (log-odds units) for MO in the logistic regression (see Table 7.1 at the end of this chapter). This indicates a significant tilt in favor of theism when acknowledging objective morality.At this juncture, one might be tempted to declare moral evidence as a win for theism. However, the moral arena presents a two-sided coin: moral order suggests a divine lawgiver, but the world is also filled with moral disorder and evil, which seems to count against the idea of a good God. We must therefore examine the second part of our chapter’s title: Evil. The existence of profound evil and suffering has long been considered one of the most powerful objections to the existence of God. In the next sections, we will rigorously engage with the problem of evil (EV) and explore how it might be balanced against the moral argument in our evidential model.The Problem of Evil as Evidence Against GodIf moral values and duties constitute a positive argument for God, the reality of evil and suffering is often seen as a counter-argument. The problem of evil in philosophy of religion comes in several forms, but at its core it points out a tension between the existence of an all-powerful, all-good God and the existence of terrible suffering or wickedness in the world. There are two main versions of this problem: the logical problem of evil and the evidential problem of evil.* Logical Problem of Evil: This is the claim that it is logically impossible for God and evil to co-exist. J. L. Mackie (1955) famously argued that the propositions “God is omnipotent and perfectly good” and “evil exists” cannot all be true simultaneously. The reasoning is that if God is all-powerful, He could eliminate evil, and if He is all-good, He would want to eliminate evil – yet evil exists, so such a God must not exist. Mackie presented this as a deductive contradiction: a perfectly good being would prevent all evil, so the presence of any evil seemingly disproves God’s existence (Mackie, 1955). However, most philosophers today agree that the logical problem in this strict form has been successfully rebutted – primarily by Alvin Plantinga’s Free Will Defense. Plantinga (1974) showed that there is no explicit contradiction in God and evil coexisting, because it’s possible that God has a good reason to allow evil (notably, the reason of preserving human free will). If even one possible good reason exists, the logical incompatibility claim fails. Thus, while the logical problem raised important issues, it is no longer seen as the strongest version of the argument against God.* Evidential (or Probabilistic) Problem of Evil: This is a softer claim that, even if it’s possible God and evil co-exist, the amount, types, and distribution of evil in the world make God’s existence unlikely. In other words, given the horrendous evils we observe, it is improbable that a loving omnipotent God exists. This version was classically formulated by William Rowe (1979). Rowe noted that we find instances of suffering that appear utterly pointless – for example, a fawn painfully dying alone in a forest fire (a natural evil with no apparent greater good served). Rowe argued that it’s reasonable to believe such “gratuitous” evils exist, and if an all-good, all-powerful God existed, he would not allow truly gratuitous suffering. Therefore, by inductive reasoning, God probably does not exist (Rowe, 1979). Unlike the logical argument, the evidential argument doesn’t claim a contradiction, only that evil counts as evidence against God. It essentially says: given how the world looks (with atrocities, disasters, innocent suffering), the hypothesis of a benevolent deity seems unlikely – or at least less likely than it would be if we saw a world with very minimal, well-justified suffering. Other philosophers like Paul Draper (1989) have refined this argument, comparing the God hypothesis to the “indifferent universe” hypothesis and contending that the latter explains the mixture of pleasure and pain better than the former. In our evidential model, this corresponds to treating the Severity of Moral Evil (EV) as a variable that could potentially have a negative weight against theism.We should distinguish between moral evil (evils resulting from human (or creaturely) free actions – e.g. murder, war, cruelty) and natural evil (suffering from natural processes – e.g. earthquakes, diseases, floods – not directly caused by free agents). Both types present challenges: moral evil raises the question of why a good God would create beings who do such horrendous things to each other, while natural evil raises the question of why a sovereign God set up a world where even infants and animals suffer due to things like diseases and natural disasters. Both kinds of evil can be “gratuitous” or pointless in appearance – it’s often not clear what greater purpose is served by, say, a fawn’s slow death in a forest fire or the torture of a child by abusive parents. These observations fuel the evidential argument: even if some evil might be necessary for a greater good, surely not this much evil, or not these extreme, apparently unnecessary cases.The traditional use of evil as evidence against God can be summarized: If the world is filled with instances of seemingly unjustifiable suffering, and if we can’t see a good reason for God to allow them, it is inductively likely that no all-good, all-powerful God exists to prevent them. In Bayesian terms, the existence of evil E (especially in great magnitude or without known justification) is argued to make God’s existence less probable: P(E | God) is thought to be much lower than P(E | no God). Indeed, many find it intuitively convincing that a loving God would simply not permit things like the Holocaust or child cancer if He had the power to prevent them; thus the persistence of such evils seems to imply God is either not loving, not powerful, or non-existent. As the philosopher David Hume, speaking through a character in his Dialogues Concerning Natural Religion (1779), put it: “Is [God] willing to prevent evil, but not able? then he is impotent. Is he able, but not willing? then he is malevolent. Is he both able and willing? Whence then is evil?” (Hume, 1779/1998). This encapsulates the dilemma that theists face.Given the prima facie force of this argument, it is no surprise that the problem of evil has been called “the rock of atheism”. Even devout thinkers have struggled mightily with the weight of this evidence. Notably, the atheist philosopher Louise Antony (whom we met earlier) cites the problem of suffering as a major factor in her loss of faith (Antony, 2009) – she found it difficult to reconcile the extensive undeserved suffering in the world with the God she once believed in. Many others, from the philosopher Bertrand Russell to everyday people, have echoed that sentiment: if there were a loving God, surely the world wouldn’t be this cruel.In our probabilistic model of divine evidence, the EV variable originally represented this intuitive evidential force of evil against God. If taken at face value, the presence of intense evils would merit a negative coefficient for EV (pulling the probability of God’s existence downward). Without any mitigating considerations, the sheer volume of evil might indeed significantly lower the odds for God in a Bayesian calculation. However, theistic philosophy has developed various responses – theodicies and defenses – that aim to explain (or explain away) the problem of evil. These responses propose reasons God might have for allowing evil, thereby undermining the inference from “evil exists” to “God likely does not exist.” We will now explore the leading theodicies and how they can reduce the evidential impact of evil.Theodicies: Responding to the Reality of EvilA theodicy is an attempt to justify God’s allowance of evil by articulating how such evil fits into God’s world-plan for a greater good. A related concept, a defense, is a less ambitious attempt merely to show that evil could be logically consistent with God’s existence (without claiming to know God’s actual reasons). In this chapter, we are interested in the major theodicies that have been proposed, because they can potentially neutralize the evidential weight of evil by providing plausible reasons for God to permit suffering. Three leading approaches in the contemporary discussion are: (1) the Free Will Defense, (2) the Soul-Making Theodicy, and (3) Skeptical Theism. Each addresses a different aspect of the problem.Free Will Defense (and Theodicy) – Alvin Plantinga’s ApproachThe free will defense argues that moral evil is a by-product of God granting significant freedom to creatures, and that this freedom is a great good that God had good reason to endow us with, despite knowing it would be misused. Alvin Plantinga’s famous free will defense was formulated to refute the logical problem of evil. Plantinga (1974) posited that it is possible God could not create a world of free creatures who always choose good – if creatures have truly free will, God cannot determine that they will never do evil, or else their will wouldn’t be free. Thus, even an omnipotent God might face a trade-off: either no free beings (and thus no moral evil, but also no loving or morally significant relationships), or free beings who can choose evil (and sadly, do choose evil). Plantinga further introduced the concept of transworld depravity: the idea that perhaps every possible person with free will would commit at least some wrong act in any world they were placed in – implying that God couldn’t actualize a perfect world with free persons without at least some sin occurring. His defense demonstrated that there is no inherent contradiction in God and evil coexisting, because free will could be a morally sufficient reason for God to allow evil.Beyond merely a “defense,” many theists embrace free will as a positive theodicy: they argue that creaturely freedom is so valuable (it allows for genuine love, moral virtue, and meaningful agency) that it justifies God’s decision to create free beings even knowing they would cause suffering. On this view, much of the moral evil in the world (wrong acts by humans) is explained as the result of God respecting human freedom. A world with free will and moral evil may be better in the long run than a world with no freedom and thus no evil. For example, the love of a child freely given is worth the risk that the child might also disobey; coerced goodness would not have the same value as freely chosen goodness. Therefore, God allows humans to make evil choices rather than making us puppets. This addresses moral evils (crimes, violence, etc.), attributing them to human misuse of God’s good gift of freedom.Some philosophers have even extended the free will idea to natural evil by hypothesizing free will for non-human entities (like fallen angels/demons who cause natural evils). Plantinga himself suggested as a logical possibility that natural evils (like earthquakes) might result from the actions of free supernatural beings (Plantinga, 1974) – not as a claim to believe necessarily, but to show it’s possible God’s allowance of natural evil also ties to free will. Most contemporary discussions, however, address natural evils with different theodicies, to which we turn next. But clearly, the free will defense is a central piece: it explains why God might allow a significant range of evils (all those arising from moral agency) and preserves God’s goodness by placing responsibility on creaturely choices. If successful, it removes the need to count the existence of moral evil as strong evidence against God, since that evil is an expected outcome if God highly values freedom.Soul-Making Theodicy – John Hick’s ApproachNot all evils result directly from free choices. Natural disasters, diseases, and seemingly undeserved suffering affect humans and animals regardless of anyone’s moral choices. The soul-making theodicy, advanced in modern form by John Hick (1966), provides a framework for understanding these evils as serving a higher divine purpose: the development of moral and spiritual virtues. Hick drew on the ideas of earlier theologian Irenaeus to propose that the world is not meant to be a hedonistic paradise but rather a challenging environment in which imperfect beings can grow toward spiritual maturity. According to Hick, God’s ultimate goal for humanity is not just to create a comfortable place for us, but to bring about “children of God” perfected in virtue. Such development – qualities like courage, compassion, patience, forgiveness – often requires facing and overcoming difficulties and suffering. As Hick put it, virtues that “are built up in response to challenges” (like responding to suffering with bravery or to others’ pain with empathy) are intrinsically more valuable than virtues created in us without any effort or struggle (Hick, 1966).Thus, a world with no pain, no danger, and no hardship might seem nice, but it would also be one where many virtues never come to existence. For example, if there were no danger or injury, there would be no opportunity for courage or self-sacrifice. If there were no one ever in need or distress, there would be no opportunities for deep compassion or charity. By allowing natural evils, God provides an arena for “soul-making” – our character building and moral growth. This theodicy can account for both natural evils (e.g. natural disasters can engender heroism and altruism; suffering can deepen a person’s spiritual life or dependence on God) and some moral evils (battling against injustice and wrongdoing can likewise develop virtue). It portrays God as a sort of divine parent or trainer who does not shelter his children from all difficulty, but rather uses adversity to shape them into better beings.Of course, critics question whether the extent of suffering we see is really necessary for soul-making – did it need to be so immense and often seemingly purposeless? Hick admits not every instance of suffering obviously contributes to a person’s spiritual growth, but he argues that we must view the process in an eschatological perspective: in the afterlife or the fullness of time, the sufferings of this world will be overcome and will be seen as a finite, though difficult, means to a glorious end (Hick, 1966). In any case, if one accepts that developing virtue is a higher priority for God than immediate creaturely comfort, then many evils (especially natural ones) become understandable in the big picture. The world starts to look like a “vale of soul-making” rather than a place meant for maximal pleasure. This greatly diminishes the force of evil as evidence against God: rather than being pointless, suffering has a point – it is the gymnasium for the soul.Skeptical Theism – The Limits of Human Knowledge (Wykstra and Bergmann)Despite free will and soul-making theodicies, there remain instances of evil that stubbornly seem gratuitous. What about a toddler who dies in a tsunami, or millions of years of animal suffering before humans even arrived (if one accepts an evolutionary history)? Even if some evils serve soul-making, others strike us as utterly pointless for any greater good we can conceive. Skeptical Theism is a more recent approach that doesn’t offer a specific positive explanation for each evil, but instead questions the inference from “we see no reason for this evil” to “there is no reason for this evil.” In other words, it injects skepticism about human cognitive capacities when it comes to God’s reasons.The core idea of skeptical theism is: God’s knowledge and wisdom are vastly greater than ours, so we should not expect to understand all of God’s reasons for allowing what He allows. Just because an evil appears gratuitous to us does not mean it actually is gratuitous in an absolute sense – it could be that we simply cannot fathom the complex web of providential planning that a sovereign God oversees. Philosopher Stephen Wykstra, in a seminal paper (1984), introduced an apt analogy: he compared our perspective on the problem of evil to that of a child in relation to a parent. The child at the doctor might not see any reason for the painful injection and conclude the parent is cruel, but the parent has a good reason (to vaccinate the child) that the child cannot appreciate at his level of understanding. Wykstra formalized a principle he called CORNEA (“Condition of Reasonable Epistemic Access”): we are only justified in claiming “No see um, therefore not there” if it’s reasonable to expect that we would see the thing if it were there. Applying this to evil: are we reasonably positioned to expect that if God had good reasons for permitting some horrendous evil, we would likely discern those reasons? Wykstra answers: probably not, given the epistemic gap between divine intellect and ours (Wykstra, 1984). Thus, our inability to think of a good reason for, say, a child’s suffering doesn’t entitle us to conclude no one (including God) has a good reason.Michael Bergmann (2009) and other proponents of skeptical theism have expanded on this, arguing that for any given evil, there could be a myriad of divine reasons (perhaps connected to people’s free will in the distant future, or ripple effects in history, or soul-making in ways we cannot trace) that are simply beyond our ken. The fact that the world is complex and God’s plan spans eternity means our snapshot view is inherently limited. We shouldn’t expect to have a full God’s-eye view. Bergmann notes that as long as it’s possible that God has morally sufficient reasons beyond our understanding, one cannot use unknown purposes as evidence against God – our ignorance undercuts the inductive step of Rowe’s argument (Bergmann, 2009). In essence, skeptical theism doesn’t tell us why God allows evil; it tells us we shouldn’t assume we’d be able to know why. It is a stance of epistemic humility: given God’s omniscience and our very finite minds, “it’s not surprising that we can’t think of reasons for a lot of things He permits” (Bergmann, 2009, p. 375).One might worry that this approach leads to a kind of moral paralysis or skepticism about everything (if we can’t understand God’s reasons, how do we trust any moral reasoning?). Critics have raised such concerns (the “skeptical theism slippery slope”). However, as a response to the evidential problem of evil, skeptical theism is potent. It effectively blocks the inference from “pointless evil exists” to “God probably doesn’t exist” by challenging the first part – we are not in a position to know that any given evil is truly pointless in the grand scheme. As long as it’s plausible that God has reasons we cannot comprehend, the evidential force of evil is greatly weakened. Rowe himself acknowledged this in later writings, calling such theistic stance “friendly atheism” – the idea that an atheist can see a theist as rationally justified in believing God has unknown reasons (Rowe, 1979).(MISSING FIGURE 7.2)Figure 7.2: Decision tree illustrating theodicy responses to observed evil. When faced with any instance of evil (E), a theist can ask: Is this evil the result of moral free will? If yes, the Free Will Defense applies (free agents sometimes choose evil, which God allows for the sake of freedom). If no (it’s a natural evil), one asks: Could it serve a soul-making purpose (e.g. character development, greater future good)? If yes, then the Soul-Making Theodicy provides an explanation. If the evil still appears unexplained and gratuitous, the theist falls back on Skeptical Theism – trusting that God may have unknown reasons beyond our understanding. In all cases, the outcome is that the existence of evil does not count as decisive evidence against God’s existence, once plausible divine reasons are considered.In combination, these three approaches (and others we have not detailed, such as natural law theodicies, punishment theories, etc.) provide a rich toolkit for theists to neutralize the evidential impact of evil. Free will and soul-making suggest why God might allow significant evil (because it was a side-effect of greater goods like freedom and virtue), and skeptical theism cautions us against assuming that any apparently pointless evil really lacks a God-justifying reason.Balancing Moral Evidence in a Probabilistic ModelHaving surveyed the moral argument for God and the responses to the problem of evil, we can now evaluate how these factors play out in our overall probabilistic model of God’s existence. In earlier chapters, we introduced a logistic regression framework that incorporates various evidential variables (cosmological fine-tuning, consciousness, etc.) as predictors for the truth of the proposition “God exists.” Two key ethical variables in that model are:* MO (Moral Objectivity): indicating that objective moral values and duties exist in reality.* EV (Severity of Moral Evil): indicating the observed degree of evil and suffering in the world (initially construed as a negative factor).Before considering theodicies, one might expect MO to have a positive coefficient (making God’s existence more likely) and EV to have a negative coefficient (making God’s existence less likely). Indeed, in a naïve model without theodicies, both variables carry significant weight but in opposite directions – a kind of evidential tug-of-war between the goodness in the world (moral order) and the badness in the world (evil). However, our analysis in this chapter suggests that plausible divine reasons can neutralize the evidential force of evil, effectively zeroing out its weight. If the free will defense and soul-making theodicy are close to the mark, then the existence of moral and natural evils is not so surprising under theism after all – it’s an expected byproduct of a world created for meaningful freedom and growth. And skeptical theism further undercuts any residual inference by saying we lack the perspective to declare any evil truly inexplicable under God. Therefore, in our refined model, we treat the EV variable as epistemically neutral.What does it mean for EV to be epistemically neutral? It means that, once we take into account the best theodicies (i.e. assuming God has good reasons for any evil we see), the mere presence of observed evil doesn’t shift our probability for God either way. In terms of our logistic regression, the coefficient for EV is set to near-zero. In fact, we assign ?_EV ≈ 0.0 (essentially null) in the updated model. This reflects the judgment that the existence of evil, given the background assumption of God and His reasons, is not evidence against God’s existence in any decisive sense. It’s important to clarify – this is not to say evil becomes evidence for God (we are not claiming that evil somehow makes God more likely, except perhaps in some very paradoxical “Felix culpa” theological sense). Rather, we argue that evil’s weight can be cancelled out to first order, leaving it as a roughly netural factor when one considers the total evidence.By contrast, Moral Objectivity (MO) remains a strong positive factor. Nothing in the problem of evil negates the fact that the existence of real moral values points toward a moral Creator. If anything, grappling with horrendous evil reinforces the sense that certain things are objectively wrong – and thus underscores Premise 2 of the moral argument (that objective values exist). Even an atheist outraged by evil is implicitly appealing to objective moral standards (as C. S. Lewis noted, “When I was an atheist my argument against God was that the universe seemed so cruel and unjust. But how had I got this idea of just and unjust?” (Lewis, 1952)). In our model, therefore, MO’s coefficient stays positive and significant. Combining this with EV’s neutral coefficient yields an overall moral contribution that is favorable to theism.To illustrate, our updated logistic model for God’s existence includes eight variables total, but focusing on the two at hand: we have ?_MO > 0 and ?_EV ≈ 0. If we plug in the fact that in the actual world MO = 1 (objective morality is a reality, as argued) and EV = 1 (evil is observed, though neutralized in effect), the net impact on the log-odds is dominated by MO’s term. In Chapter 3, we justified a tentative value around ?_MO = +1.0 (meaning the presence of moral objectivity roughly triples the odds of God, all else equal), while in this chapter we set ?_EV from an initial negative value to approximately 0.0 after accounting for theodicies. The result is that the moral order (MO) variable contributes positively to the probability of God’s existence, whereas the evil (EV) variable contributes virtually nothing once “defused.”One might ask: is it really fair to neutralize EV entirely? Some critics might feel this is too convenient – surely horrendous evil must count against God at least a little, even if we have some explanations. In response, we acknowledge that not everyone will be fully satisfied by theodicies. The decision to treat EV as neutral is based on accepting the plausibility of the reasons discussed. If one remains unconvinced by, say, skeptical theism or thinks that soul-making doesn’t justify the extent of suffering, they might assign EV a residual negative weight. And indeed, in Appendix C of this work, we explored a scenario where EV retains a modest negative coefficient to reflect a more pessimistic view of theodicy (see Appendix C for a sensitivity analysis). We found that even then, the cumulative case for God still held up, though the probabilities shifted downwards somewhat . But for the purpose of the main model, we are granting the most charitable assumption to the theistic explanations: that they succeed in showing evil does not undermine the case for God. This assumption aligns with a sizable segment of the philosophy of religion literature that finds the evidential argument from evil ultimately inconclusive due to the factors we discussed.In summary, after weighing both sides – Moral Order and Evil – we find that the scales tip in favor of theism. The positive evidence of objective moral values remains firm, while the negative evidence of evil is largely mitigated by plausible divine reasons. The term “Ethical Evidence in Balance” in the book’s title is thus addressed: the moral argument and the problem of evil, initially appearing as opposing forces, do not simply cancel out. Rather, the moral argument retains evidential heft once the problem of evil is answered. We can now express this outcome in a concise form for our model.Below is a summary table comparing the influence of the MO and EV variables in the logistic model, reflecting the conclusions drawn in this chapter:Table 7.1: Moral Evidence Variables in the Divine Existence ModelVariable (Code)Logistic Coefficient (?)Philosophical RationaleMoral Objectivity (MO)Positive (e.g. +1.0)Strong evidence for God. The existence of objective moral values and duties is more likely in a theistic universe (God provides an ontological foundation for moral truths) than in a naturalistic one. This raises the probability of God’s existence.Moral Evil (EV)Neutral (~0.0)Initially evidence against God, but rendered neutral after theodicies. The observed evils (moral and natural) can be explained by free will, soul-making, or unknown divine reasons. Once these are accounted for, the presence of evil no longer significantly lowers the probability of God’s existence.As shown above, MO carries a positive weight in favor of theism, while EV is assigned a near-zero weight after our in-depth analysis. In the full model (as updated in Chapter 9), all other variables (cosmological fine-tuning, consciousness, etc.) remain positive or neutral as well, yielding a strongly theism-favoring balance of probabilities. The moral dimension, far from refuting God, on balance provides a significant boost to the case for God’s existence. The moral order in reality – our perception of a binding moral law – stands as a clue pointing to a divine Moral Lawgiver, and the evil in the world, though emotionally and intellectually challenging, can be understood in a way that is compatible with (and in some views, even expected under) that very same Moral Lawgiver’s purposes.In conclusion, the ethical evidence when fully balanced does not leave us in a stalemate; rather, it tilts the scales in favor of theism. A universe with objective moral truth and the drama of moral struggle (good versus evil) “makes sense” in a theistic framework wherein God endows creatures with freedom and opportunities for growth. The apparent paradox of moral order and evil can be resolved by recognizing that a good God would create a moral arena and allow significant freedom within it – knowing that freedom entails risk and suffering, but also that He can ultimately work even the evils toward a greater good. With this resolution, we are justified in assigning moral objectivity a positive evidential value and treating the reality of evil as, at most, a transient puzzle rather than a permanent blot against God’s existence. The next chapter will build on this refined model, incorporating these coefficients into the cumulative probability calculations and addressing remaining considerations.<br>  References (Harvard Style)Adams, R.
