These experiences range from dramatic mystical visions to subtle feelings of presence during prayer, and they have been reported across virtually all cultures and eras . In the philosophy of religion, RE is considered a philosophical and empirical evidential category – meaning it is both a subjective phenomenon and a potential data point about reality. The core question is whether and how such experiences might serve as evidence for the existence of God (or a transcendent reality). Traditionally, arguments from religious experience propose that if many people independently report encounters with the divine, this increases the probability that something real is behind those reports . If a personal God exists, it is not surprising that some people would experience contact with the divine; whereas if no God exists, it is harder to explain why so many have experiences seemingly of “something” beyond the material . In this chapter, we analyze RE as evidence: examining its prima facie credibility, its prevalence in society, its transformative impact on individuals, its cross-cultural manifestations, and insights from research and neuroscience. We will see that religious experiences are far from fringe phenomena – they are widespread and often life-changing – yet their evidential force must be carefully assessed with both openness and skepticism.The Principle of Credulity: Trusting Experience Unless DefeatedA central argument in favor of taking religious experiences seriously comes from philosopher Richard Swinburne’s Principle of Credulity. Swinburne suggests that we should trust our experiences unless we have a specific reason not to . In his formulation: if it seems to someone that they perceive X (e.g. God), then in the absence of defeaters one is justified in believing X is present . In plain terms, things are probably as they seem to be – prima facie (at first glance) evidence – unless we have evidence that the experience was delusive. We apply this principle in daily life to sense-experience: if you see a tree, you assume the tree is real unless you learn you were hallucinating or tricked. Swinburne argues the same should hold for religious experience: if someone genuinely seems to experience God, that should count as evidence of God’s reality, unless there are defeaters like drugs, mental illness, or other explanations that undermine the claim . He and others (e.g. William Alston and Alvin Plantinga) contend that religious perception can be a legitimate mode of knowledge, analogous to sensory perception . Critics, however, point out that religious experiences might differ from ordinary perception in important ways – for example, they are private and not as easily corroborated by others. A skeptic might argue that, unlike seeing a tree, when someone “sees God” there is no independent check and hence we should be more cautious. Swinburne’s rejoinder is that unless one can show in principle that every religious experience is undermined by some error or psychological mechanism, we ought not dismiss them wholesale . The Principle of Credulity thus sets a default of trust in experience, placing the burden on disproof rather than on the experiencer to prove their encounter was real . This principle lays a foundation: it does not prove any given experience is veridical, but it argues we should include RE in our cumulative case for God, unless strong counter-evidence is present.Prevalence of Religious Experience: Sociological DataOne might think mystical or spiritual experiences are rare or confined to saints and eccentrics. Empirical sociological data shows the opposite: religious experiences are surprisingly widespread in the general population. Surveys over decades have consistently found a large fraction of people report having had some form of transcendent or mystical encounter. For example, in the United States, Gallup and Pew Research polls show a dramatic increase in reported religious/mystical experiences over time – from only 22% in 1962 to 33% by 1994, and reaching 49% in 2009 . In other words, nearly half of Americans today say they have felt a “religious or mystical experience” at least once . This was defined broadly to include things like “a moment of sudden religious insight or awakening.” The longitudinal trend suggests such experiences have become more common (or more commonly admitted) in modern times . Intriguingly, a UK survey by the Alister Hardy Religious Experience Research Centre (RERC) found 36% of Britons in 1978 had felt a “presence or power different from everyday life,” rising to 48% in 1987; by 2000, a survey by David Hay reported over 75% were “aware of a spiritual dimension” to life . These differences partly reflect question wording (the 2000 phrasing was broader), but the data underscore that Britain – often seen as secular – harbors a majority with some spiritual experience. Cross-nationally, surveys indicate many cultures report similar levels of spiritual experiences . While exact percentages vary with wording and cultural context, it is common to find one-third or more of respondents in Europe, North America, and even relatively secular countries acknowledging moments of transcendence or divine encounter. A recent Brazilian nationwide study likewise found 35% had experienced at least one profound “mystical experience,” with 92% reporting some kind of spiritual or paranormal experience in their lifetime . Even in highly secular demographic groups, significant minorities have experiences: Pew data revealed that about 30% of the religiously unaffiliated (“nones”) in the U.S. have had a religious or mystical experience . This is remarkable – even people who do not identify with any religion sometimes report uninvited spiritual encounters.Demographic patterns also emerge. Religious experiences occur across ages and backgrounds, but surveys find higher reports among certain groups. In the U.S., evangelical Protestants and Black Protestants report the highest rates (around 70% say they’ve had such experiences), while more mainline Protestants and Catholics report lower though still significant rates (37–40%) . Those who attend religious services regularly are more likely to report mystical experiences (not surprising, since participation may provide more opportunities or openness to them). That said, even among non-attenders and secular individuals, substantial numbers have them . Age can be a factor: Baby Boomers (mid-life adults) in one survey had the highest rate (~55%), compared to ~43% for younger adults and seniors . This could reflect life stage (mid-life crises or quest for meaning) or a cohort effect (the boomers came of age during eras of spiritual experimentation). Gender differences are usually small or inconsistent, though some research (e.g. in Brazil ) found women slightly more likely to report certain spiritual experiences. Overall, the sociological evidence is unambiguous that REs are not fringe. They are common, global, and enduring aspects of human experience. In fact, considering that far fewer people claim a formal mystical experience (49%) than profess some religious affiliation (e.g. ~72% in the U.S.), these experiences are not universal – but they are prevalent enough to merit serious study . The global map of spirituality is such that nearly every region has a notable share of its population claiming direct encounters with “something greater.”Estimated prevalence of self-reported religious or mystical experiences around the world. Lighter shades indicate lower percentages; darker shades indicate higher percentages of the population reporting at least one such experience in their lifetime (based on survey data from the USA, UK, and various global studies, 1960s–2000s).Importantly, these data also span time – indicating an increase in reports over the past half-century. Scholars debate why: Possible factors include the decline of stigma in talking about such experiences (in earlier decades, admitting to a “mystical experience” might have invited skepticism or ridicule, whereas today there is greater cultural openness to spirituality and psychology of religion), the spread of meditative practices and New Age movements, or simply the survey effect (people more willing to check “yes” on a confidential survey than to speak about it publicly). Regardless, the take-home message is that religious experience is a widespread human phenomenon, not confined to a few monks or visionaries. Any theory of reality (theistic or not) ought to account for why millions to billions of people report these experiences.Transformative Power and Personal TestimoniesAnother notable feature of religious experiences is their often life-transforming impact. Many individuals regard their experience as one of the most significant events of their lives, sometimes triggering lasting changes in character, values, or worldview. William James, in The Varieties of Religious Experience (1902), documented numerous firsthand accounts of people who underwent profound conversions or enlightenment after an RE . These were not trivial mood swings; they frequently involved a sense of acquiring knowledge or insight (James called this the “noetic quality” of mystical states) and a reorientation of life. For instance, James recounts the case of a man who had been a despairing skeptic until one night he felt “the presence of God” flood him with peace; from that moment, he became a confident believer and his despair lifted. Such testimonies abound in the literature. The Alister Hardy Religious Experience Research Centre in the UK, since the 1970s, has collected thousands of accounts from ordinary people describing extraordinary moments – encounters with light, voices, presences, or an overwhelming unity – that often changed the course of their lives. Hardy’s team (and later researchers like David Hay) found that many respondents never spoke of their experience until asked in a survey, yet the memory remained a private source of strength or conviction for decades.To illustrate, consider a few representative cases: The British author Philip Pullman, known for his skeptical outlook, once had a spontaneous “ecstatic” experience while walking in London. He described suddenly seeing the world “not as isolated, indifferent units, but ‘alive, conscious and full of purpose’” – a vision of universal connectedness that left him convinced “the universe is alive and has meaning” . Pullman rarely talks about it, but admitted “Everything I’ve written… has been an attempt to bear witness to the truth of that statement.” Here we have a non-religious intellectual profoundly affected by a mystical insight. On the religious side, many famous figures trace their calling to a vivid experience: St. Paul’s vision of Christ on the road to Damascus (which transformed him from persecutor to apostle) is a classic example from Christian history. In modern times, one could cite the civil rights leader Martin Luther King Jr.’s kitchen table experience (when in despair, he prayed and felt an assuring presence of God, giving him strength to continue) or the conversion of writer C.S. Lewis, who went from atheism to theism after what he described as an unexpected moment of “joy” and surrender while being driven to the zoo (a less dramatic but still pivotal experience of the numinous). The Varieties of Religious Experience and subsequent catalogues (such as Hardy’s 1979 anthology and the ongoing archives) are filled with ordinary people’s stories: a mother who during childbirth felt enveloped by a divine love, a soldier in the trenches who saw an angel or felt a protective presence amid chaos, a man on the verge of suicide who heard a voice saying “I am with you” and found new hope, and so on. While anecdotes, these accounts show consistent patterns: the experiences often bring a sense of peace, unity, light, or love, they feel more real than ordinary reality to those who have them, and they frequently yield positive changes (greater compassion, loss of fear of death, overcoming of addiction, etc.). As one scholar noted, “the quality and transformative power of some experiences bolsters the case that they are more than hallucinations” . It’s hard to simply dismiss as “nothing but brain chemistry” an event that, say, turns a lifelong alcoholic into a sober, altruistic person overnight. The subjective epistemic weight of a religious experience for the experiencer is enormous: it can feel utterly convincing – a direct encounter with the Real – and thus serves as personal evidence that may override prior doubts.That said, personal transformation alone doesn’t guarantee an experience corresponds to an external reality; people can be dramatically changed by beliefs or illusions too. But from a theistic perspective, the positive, enduring fruits of many REs are at least consistent with the idea that something genuine (God’s grace, perhaps) was at work. In any case, the widespread life-changing nature of REs underscores that these are not trivial hallucinations or momentary fantasies for those who have them – they often form the bedrock of those individuals’ commitment to God or spirituality.Cross-Cultural Comparisons: Many Names, Similar ExperiencesReligious experiences take different forms in different cultural and religious contexts, yet there are intriguing commonalities that suggest a core human phenomenon clothed in many guises. Here we present a brief cross-cultural comparative study of REs in several major traditions:* Christianity: Within Christianity, accounts of religious experience range from the dramatic to the still and small. Christian mysticism has a rich history – from early Desert Fathers feeling divine light to medieval visionaries like Julian of Norwich or St. Teresa of Ávila who reported intense visions of Christ and union with God. A common Christian RE is the sense of the “presence of Christ” or the Holy Spirit, often described as a profound peace, love, or guidance that descends in prayer or worship. For example, in Pentecostal and Charismatic branches, believers often describe being “filled with the Spirit,” sometimes accompanied by speaking in tongues or visions. Even outside mystic circles, many Christians have moments of feeling God’s intimate presence in their lives (a sudden assurance, a voice in the heart, a miraculous answer to prayer). Visions are also part of the Christian narrative: from biblical times (Paul’s vision, Peter’s trance in Acts) to modern reported apparitions (such as visions of the Virgin Mary at Fatima or Lourdes). The variety is great – yet underlying them is usually the conviction of encountering a loving, personal God or Christ. Theistic experiences in Christianity tend to be dualistic but intimate: God is “other” but enters into one’s soul, bringing comfort, conviction of sin and grace, or ecstatic joy.* Islam (Sufism): In Islam, especially in the mystical tradition of Sufism, religious experience is often described in terms of intense love and unity with God (Allah). While mainstream Islam emphasizes submission to God through practice, Sufi mystics seek a direct experiential knowledge (ma’rifa) of God. Classic Sufi literature (e.g. Rumi’s poetry) overflows with accounts of ecstatic states (wajd) where the ego falls away in the overwhelming love of the Divine. Sufis speak of “fana” – the annihilation of the self in God – a state in which only the divine beloved is perceived to exist. Historical figures like Al-Ghazali describe mystical illuminations: after a period of doubt, Al-Ghazali had a series of Sufi experiences that convinced him of the reality of God more certainly than intellectual theology could. Ordinary Muslims also report experiential aspects: for instance, during the hajj pilgrimage or intense dhikr (remembrance chanting), some feel a powerful spiritual presence or vision. A key aspect in Islamic REs is that they still affirm tawhid (the oneness of God) – visions are interpreted not as seeing God’s essence (which Islam says no living person can do), but as feeling God’s names or qualities manifested. Prophetic dreams are another cross-cultural Islamic phenomenon – many Muslims believe God guides through dreams or that saints and prophets can appear in them. These experiences are generally monotheistic and personal (feeling the nearness or mercy of Allah), yet Sufi accounts sometimes sound very much like the monistic experiences of Hindu or Christian mystics (speaking of union and loss of self). This highlights both differences and similarities – culturally, a Sufi may see Muhammad or hear Quranic verses in their experience, whereas a Christian might see Jesus; but phenomenologically, both might report indescribable love or light.* Buddhism: At first glance, Buddhism (especially Theravada) might seem to have a very different paradigm, as it is nontheistic and aims for enlightenment (nirvana) rather than encountering a deity. Yet, Buddhists report experiences that parallel mystical states elsewhere. In Zen Buddhism, for example, practitioners strive for satori or kensh? – a sudden insight or awakening experience in which the illusion of a separate self drops away, and one perceives the thusness of reality. These enlightenment experiences are typically described as ineffable, beyond conceptualization, and bringing a profound sense of peace and freedom – not unlike the “ineffability and noetic quality” William James noted as marks of mysticism . In Theravada Buddhist meditation, advanced states (jh?nas) can involve extraordinary bliss and unity of mind, and ultimately the realization of “emptiness” (??nyat?) or non-self. While a Buddhist would not interpret this as “meeting God,” the existential transformation can be as great – one sees reality as impermanent and interdependent, leading to compassion and loss of fear. Meanwhile, in Mahayana traditions, some experiences do involve visionary elements – e.g. visions of bodhisattvas, or encounters with luminous Buddha-nature underlying all things. Philosophers have classified these as nirvanic experiences (focused on enlightenment) versus theistic ones , but interestingly, Buddhist mystics and Christian mystics often recognize a kinship in each other’s descriptions. The content differs (no personal deity in Buddhism), yet experiences of oneness, timelessness, and blessedness are common across the contemplative practices of both traditions.* Indigenous and Shamanic Traditions: Indigenous religions around the world – from Native American spiritualities to African tribal religions to Australian Aboriginal traditions – typically integrate spiritual experiences into communal life via shamanistic journeys, vision quests, and encounters with the spirit world. For example, a Native American youth might undertake a vision quest: fasting alone in nature until a vision or dream of a spirit guide occurs, interpreted as a revelation from the Great Spirit or other sacred powers. Shamans in many cultures enter trance states (sometimes aided by drumming, dancing, or plant medicines) to visit other realms or communicate with spirits, ancestors, or deities. These experiences are often rich in visual and auditory content – e.g. meeting animal spirit guardians, hearing voices of ancestors, or seeing the landscape transform into a mythic tableau. Despite the polytheistic or animistic framework, the shaman’s experience often carries the hallmarks of mystical experience: a sense of unity between the natural and spiritual worlds, encounter with a source of power or healing, and personal transformation (the shaman returns with knowledge or power to heal others). Anthropologists have documented that such trance experiences have common elements whether in the Amazon, Siberia, or Pacific Islands – suggesting a human propensity for entering altered states of consciousness that are given different cultural interpretations. In indigenous contexts, there is usually less skepticism about these experiences – they are integrated into the society’s understanding of reality. A person who sees a vision of a corn deity or a mountain spirit is not considered “crazy” but perhaps blessed or chosen. These experiences serve social functions (guiding the tribe, healing the sick) and personal ones (marking a spiritual calling). They also illustrate that religious experience is not only theistic (in the sense of a single God) – it can be interpreted as communication with a plural spirit world or forces of nature. Yet even here, the psychological features (trance, visions, sense of encounter with something numinous or sacred) align with the broader category of RE.In summary, across cultures and religions, humans report strikingly similar types of extraordinary experiences, differing mainly in interpretation and context. A Christian may say “I felt Jesus holding me”; a Sufi, “I melted in Allah’s love”; a Buddhist, “I experienced nirvana”; a shaman, “I traveled to the Sky-World and met the Spirit.” Each is framed in their tradition’s language, but each involves a breach of the ordinary mundane consciousness and a confrontation with a transcendent reality however defined. Scholars like John Hick have suggested that these may be diverse responses to the same ultimate reality (“the Real”), filtered through cultural lenses . Whether or not one accepts Hick’s pluralistic hypothesis, it at least offers a way to see unity behind diversity: if there is a transcendent Ultimate, different people may experience it in forms they can grasp (Christ, Allah, Brahman, etc.), analogous to how the blind men of the famous parable each felt a different part of the elephant. Critics counter that the differences could indicate people are just experiencing their own cultural expectations, casting doubt on a single underlying truth. We will revisit this in the critique section, but it’s worth noting here that core phenomenological similarities – e.g. feelings of awe, unity, ego-transcendence, and the difficulty of describing the event – cut across doctrines . This cross-cultural breadth strengthens the argument that RE is a real psychological category that any comprehensive worldview (theism or naturalism) must explain.Scholarly Research and Archives of Religious ExperienceReligious experience has not only been the province of theologians and mystics, but also a focus of systematic scholarly research for over a century. As mentioned, William James’s landmark 1902 book The Varieties of Religious Experience is an early example of treating these experiences as data for psychology and philosophy . James collected narratives and analyzed common features, influencing generations of scholars. In 1969, biologist Sir Alister Hardy founded the Religious Experience Research Unit (now Centre) in Oxford (later moved to University of Wales), explicitly to gather empirical data on spiritual experiences. Hardy was intrigued by how many people quietly have these experiences. He placed newspaper ads asking “Have you ever been aware of or influenced by a presence or power, whether you call it God or not, which is different from your everyday self?” The public response was overwhelming – hundreds of letters poured in. Over the years, the RERC amassed a catalog of thousands of firsthand accounts from people of all faiths and none, describing experiences of startling variety. This archive became a rich resource for researchers like David Hay and G.F. Healey, who analyzed patterns and published works like Religious Experience Today (Hay, 1990) highlighting the ubiquity of such reports (indeed it was Hay who conducted the UK surveys cited earlier). These research efforts treat RE as a human phenomenon to be documented and understood, much like one would study reports of unusual medical symptoms or anomalous experiences, without dismissing or affirming their metaphysical truth outright.In the United States, psychologists such as Ralph Hood Jr. have developed scales to quantify mystical experience (e.g. the “M-scale”, which measures components like unity, ineffability, etc.) . Hood’s studies (2009 and onward) find that a notable fraction of people score high on mystical experience scales, even outside explicitly religious settings . Sometimes these occur spontaneously; other times through practices like meditation or the use of psychedelic substances (more on that shortly). The consistency of phenomenological features that Hood and others find – for example, people independently describe very similar sensations of unity or transcendence – is striking . This lends support to the idea that mystical experiences form a natural category. Meanwhile, sociologists and historians (e.g. Andrew Greeley, Carl Kell, Ann Taves) have examined how interpretation and social context shape experiences: for instance, a Catholic might interpret a light as the Virgin Mary, whereas a Buddhist might see it as a sign of enlightenment. Yet beneath interpretive layers, the raw experience might be comparable.There are also contemporary digital archives and research initiatives. For example, the Global Religious Experience Project (associated with the Institute for the Bio-Cultural Study of Religion) seeks to quantify and map religious experiences around the world . The Alister Hardy archive remains active, and new collections have arisen for specific types of experiences like Near-Death Experiences (NDEs), which often have spiritual overtones (tunnels of light, feeling of going to another realm). The Varieties of Spiritual Experience Project recently attempted to update James’s work with modern cases, including “spiritual but not religious” experiences. All these efforts demonstrate a significant interdisciplinary interest – psychologists, neuroscientists, anthropologists, and theologians all weigh in. The research generally supports that religious experiences are both common and consequential. They correlate with positive outcomes like increased well-being, but also sometimes with mental health challenges (discerning an authentic RE from, say, a psychotic hallucination can be complex – though studies find most people reporting spiritual experiences are not mentally ill and function well).For our purposes, this body of research provides empirical backing to claims that RE is an evidential datum: When we plug “Religious Experience” into an informational-theological model of God’s existence, we do so knowing that peer-reviewed studies and systematic surveys have established the reality of the phenomenon. The data set of global religious experience reports is an evidential pool that a theistic model can “easily accommodate” (if God is real, of course people might experience God) whereas a naturalist model must find alternative explanations . We turn next to how neuroscience and psychology approach those explanations.Neuroscientific Perspectives: The Brain’s Role in Mystical ExperiencesIn recent decades, the tools of neuroscience have been applied to religious experiences, giving birth to “neurotheology” – the study of the neural correlates of spiritual experiences. Researchers have asked: What happens in the brain during a mystical or religious experience? If a nun is deep in prayer or a meditator achieves trance, which brain areas activate or deactivate? Early studies used EEG and neurochemical methods, and more recent ones use fMRI and SPECT imaging, to peek into the brain during reported spiritual states.One consistent finding is that multiple brain regions are involved – there is no single “God spot” that alone generates religious experience . Instead, experiences of spirituality seem to emerge from a network of changes in perception, emotion, and self-awareness circuits. For example, Dr. Andrew Newberg’s pioneering SPECT scan studies showed that during deep meditation or prayer, activity in the brain’s parietal lobes (particularly the right parietal) tends to diminish, while activity in frontal regions (attention-focusing areas) increases . The parietal lobe is associated with our sense of orientation in space and the distinction between self and other. Reduced activity there correlates with the meditator’s report of “losing the sense of self” and feeling “one with the universe” . This elegantly ties a subjective hallmark of mystical unity to a neurological process of the brain turning down its self-other mapping. Meanwhile, increased frontal lobe activity (especially prefrontal cortex) during prayer/meditation indicates intense concentration and emotional regulation – which matches the focus and absorbed attention described by practitioners. Newberg also found that the more the frontal activity ramped up, the more the parietal dropped – suggesting a see-saw that might underlie unitary states .Other studies have looked at specific experiences. A much-publicized study at the University of Utah had devout Mormons undergo fMRI while praying and recalling spiritual feelings. It found activation in the nucleus accumbens (a core of the brain’s reward circuit linked to dopamine release) when participants reported feeling the Spirit . In fact, religious experiences activated brain reward circuits similarly to love, sex, or music – implying that, biologically, feeling close to God is a rewarding, meaningful experience for the brain . This dovetails with people’s reports of bliss and joy in spiritual moments. The same study noted activity in the medial prefrontal cortex (involved in subjective valuation and judgment) and perhaps the temporal lobes as well, though detailed results are complex.Speaking of the temporal lobes, earlier research by neuroscientist Michael Persinger famously used weak electromagnetic stimulation of the temporal lobes (the so-called “God Helmet”) to induce strange experiences – from sensing an unseen presence to out-of-body feelings – in some subjects . Additionally, neurologists have long observed that some patients with temporal lobe epilepsy have intense religious visions or emotions during seizures (the classic example being Dostoevsky, who had epilepsy and described moments of indescribable joy and meaning during his seizures). These findings implicate the temporal lobes in the visionary and emotional aspects of RE. The inferior parietal and temporo-parietal junction have also been highlighted in studies of spiritual experiences for their role in self-other processing . A Yale study found that when subjects imagined a deeply intense spiritual event, the inferior parietal lobe showed decreased activity compared to when they imagined a stressful event – again indicating the brain suppresses self-focused orientation during spiritual reflection.In terms of neurotransmitters, the fact that the dopamine-linked reward system activates suggests dopamine is involved in feelings of religious ecstasy (which may be why such experiences can be intensely pleasurable or motivating). On the other hand, serotonin is strongly implicated via studies of psychedelic drugs. Psychedelics like psilocybin (the active ingredient in “magic mushrooms”), LSD, and DMT cause their effects largely by acting on serotonin 5-HT2A receptors in the brain, and they are well-known for triggering “mystical-type” experiences. A landmark 2006 Johns Hopkins study by Roland Griffiths showed that about 60% of participants given a high dose of psilocybin reported a “full mystical experience” by established criteria – with many ranking it among the most meaningful events of their lives . Follow-up studies confirmed that such drug-induced mystical experiences can lead to lasting increases in well-being and even personality changes (e.g. increased openness) . Neuroscientifically, psilocybin quiets the default mode network (a network including midline cortical structures tied to ego and self-referential thought) and can induce a sense of ego-dissolution similar to advanced meditation. From a research standpoint, psychedelics have become a tool to induce religious-type experiences in a controlled setting and then observe brain changes. They consistently show hyper-connectivity between brain regions, altered activity in the thalamus and cortex, and modulation of serotonin pathways that correlate with the depth of the mystical experience. This suggests the serotonergic system in the brain is a key gateway to experiences of awe, unity and sacredness – whether induced by mushrooms or perhaps naturally occurring surges (some theorize that near-death experiences might involve a dump of endogenous chemicals that hit similar receptors).Beyond serotonin and dopamine, other neurochemicals like oxytocin (the bonding hormone) may play a role in feelings of oneness and love, and endorphins could contribute to the blissful aspect. The emerging picture is that religious/mystical experiences have identifiable neural correlates – patterns of brain activation/deactivation and chemistry. They often involve a suspension of our ordinary orientation and an engagement of emotion and reward systems. In one sense, this demystifies RE: it shows that the brain has the capacity to generate or mediate these experiences, using tools it already has (perception, emotion, sense of self). But does this undermine the validity of religious experiences, as some skeptics claim?Brain Correlation vs. Reality: Does Neuroscience Undermine RE?Skeptical voices argue that if religious experiences can be turned on or off by stimulating the brain or taking a pill, doesn’t that indicate these experiences are “nothing but brain events”? For instance, Freud famously dismissed religious experiences as illusions born of psychological need, essentially wish fulfillment or neurosis . Modern neuroscientists might say: “Stimulate the temporal lobe, get a ‘God experience’; take psilocybin, feel one with the universe. Therefore, God is all in your head.” This is the neuro-reductionist critique. However, it is crucial to note that neural correlates do not automatically imply falseness. Correlation is not causation – and even if the brain causes an experience in one sense, that doesn’t tell us the ultimate origin of the content. The analogy often given is visual perception: When you see a tree, there are specific patterns of neural firing in your occipital cortex; we can even say if we magnetically stimulate your visual cortex, you might see flashes of light that aren’t really there. Yet none of that means the tree you see with open eyes isn’t real. The brain is the vehicle for all our perceptions, veridical or not. Thus, discovering the brain basis of an experience no more disproves its authenticity than discovering the mechanics of radio reception disproves the reality of radio signals. As one writer put it, we could induce an experience of sound by directly stimulating the auditory cortex – the person “hears music” artificially – but that doesn’t mean that when they hear Beethoven in a concert it’s not real music. Likewise, that the brain has a “God circuit” or can be chemically nudged into mystical states might simply show that humans are hard-wired to be able to experience the transcendent . A theist might interpret this as God’s design: the Creator gave us the neural machinery to perceive the spiritual, just as He gave us eyes to perceive the physical. A naturalist would interpret it as evolutionary accident or as a useful mental capacity that helped our ancestors (some speculate that spiritual states foster social cohesion or mental health, thus were selected for).The key philosophical point is that neuro-correlation does not equal nullification. We must be careful of the genetic fallacy (assuming that explaining the origin of a belief invalidates its truth). If someone has a profound encounter with God during prayer, neuroscience can likely show what brain states corresponded, but that doesn’t answer whether God in fact touched the person or whether it was “just neurons misfiring.” As with any perception, there is always both a subject and an object (in Edmund Husserl’s terms, a noesis and a noema). The brain is involved in the noetic process – the seeing – but the question of the reality of the object (God) is separate. So, while we fully acknowledge the findings of neuroscience – that temporal and parietal lobes, etc., are implicated in mystical states, and that neurotransmitters like serotonin and dopamine modulate these experiences – this by itself does not debunk the experiences. It simply provides a mechanism for how they occur in human psychology. Moreover, neuroscience has found no evidence that religious experiences are pathological in general. They typically occur in mentally healthy individuals and often have positive effects. In fact, research by neuroscientist Patrick McNamara suggests that certain brain lesions or diseases can impair spiritual capacity, implying that a normally functioning brain is geared to be open to transcendent experiences. Far from proving REs “false,” neuroscience is showing us that “the brain may be a necessary intermediary for spiritual experiences, as expected if humans are meant to experience the divine in and through their biology.”In conclusion, the neuroscience of RE is a double-edged sword in evidential debates: Skeptics use it to naturalize the experiences, saying “we can simulate God in a lab”; believers use it to underscore the universality and design, saying “we are wired for God, which is what we’d expect if God exists.” The correct approach is probably to treat the neuroscience as largely neutral data – it tells us how but not whether there is a transcendent reality behind the experiences.Brain scans comparing baseline and meditation in a Tibetan Buddhist practitioner. The orientation area (parietal lobe, indicated by arrows) shows decreased activity during meditation (right) compared to baseline (left), correlating with the meditator’s loss of sense of space and self . Such neural patterns are characteristic of mystical states across traditions.Epistemic Value: Personal Certainty vs. Public EvidenceReligious experiences pose an interesting epistemological situation: they often carry great weight for the person who experiences them (subjective certainty), yet they offer more modest evidence for others (intersubjective or statistical weight). As noted earlier, someone who has a vivid encounter with the divine may feel they now know God exists as surely as they know anything – their confidence skyrockets. But if that person comes to a friend and says “I saw God,” the friend (who did not share the experience) is reasonably going to be more skeptical. Thus, the epistemic force of RE is asymmetrical: it is typically strongest first-person and weaker third-person.Philosophers have long pointed out that for the experiencer, a powerful RE can confer something like properly basic belief in God (Plantinga’s term) or serve as direct justification, needing no further argument . It’s akin to how you trust your own senses – if you see a sunset, you don’t need someone else to validate it for you. But for someone else, your report of seeing a sunset is just testimony – they might trust you if they consider you reliable, but it’s not as compelling as if they saw it themselves. The same goes for mystical experiences: they are personally evidence, but socially they become testimonial evidence at best. When aggregated, however, testimonial evidence can accumulate weight. If dozens of independent people all report similar experiences of a divine presence – especially if some of those people were previously nonbelievers or had no obvious motive to fabricate – then outsiders start to take notice. This is essentially what the cumulative data from surveys and archives provides: a form of intersubjective evidence. While we (those who didn’t have those experiences) cannot be as sure as the people who did, we have a large number of data points that are suggestive. It is in this sense that RE is often included in a cumulative case for God: it’s not a proof in itself, but it adds weight. As our logistic regression model intends to do, one can treat the existence of widespread RE as one probabilistic input.In Bayesian terms, let E = “the observation that a significant number of people report religious experiences.” We consider two hypotheses: G (God exists) and ~G (God does not exist). If G is true, the likelihood of E might be fairly high – one would expect God to reveal Himself or be perceived at least occasionally by humans (especially if God is personal and relational). If ~G is true, the likelihood of E is harder to assess; humans might still have anomalous experiences due to psychological or neurological factors, but that so many and so striking is perhaps less expected. Thus, one could argue E confirms G to some degree: P(E|G) > P(E|~G). Even modestly greater likelihood yields a Bayes factor >1 in favor of G. Our project’s logistic model assigned a positive coefficient to the RE variable reflecting this logic. For instance, we initially gave RE a coefficient of about +0.8 (log-odds units), translating to an odds multiplier of ~2.2 for God’s existence when RE is “present” in the world . In other words, the prevalence of religious experience roughly doubles the odds for theism in our model – a “modest” but non-negligible bump. We chose a moderate value because while RE clearly exists (so it’s a fact to explain), its interpretation is contested . In sensitivity analysis, we might vary that weight: a skeptic might set it near 0 (thinking all experiences have natural explanations), while a believer might set it higher (if they think the sheer number of mystics strongly indicates a divine reality). Our Monte Carlo simulations found that in the vast majority of runs, a positive RE effect persisted – it rarely went to zero . Intuitively, even a skeptic must admit that “the fact that billions claim some connection to the divine cannot be entirely shrugged off” . One must account for it somehow, either by the God hypothesis or by a combination of psychological hypotheses.To sum up, for the individual experiencer, an RE can be virtually incontrovertible (subjectively), whereas for others, it serves as one piece of evidence among many. This distinction also counsels humility: someone who hasn’t had such an experience might undervalue how powerful it can be, and someone who has might overestimate how much it should persuade others. Thus, in presenting RE as evidence, we do so as part of a cumulative argument, not as a stand-alone guarantee.Objections and Skeptical CounterargumentsNo discussion of religious experience as evidence is complete without addressing the skeptical critiques and potential defeaters. We’ve touched on a few (Freud’s critique, neuro-reductionism, diversity of experiences); here we summarize the major points raised by atheists or naturalists and consider responses:* Projection and Wish-Fulfillment: The critique dating back to Feuerbach and Freud posits that God and related experiences are human projections. People deeply desire meaning, security, a father figure, etc., and thus subconsciously generate the feeling of God’s presence to comfort themselves. Freud called religion an illusion springing from neurosis – essentially a mass wish-fulfillment . Modern psychologists might similarly suggest that in times of stress or need, the mind consoles itself by imagining a divine presence. Response: While certainly some experiences could be wish-fulfillment, this hypothesis cannot account for all cases. Many religious experiences are unsought and unexpected, sometimes happening to people who did not consciously wish for them (e.g., an atheist overwhelmed by a mystical experience they never wanted). Also, not all REs are comforting – some are awe-full or even terrifying (the “sick soul” experiences James describes, like confronting one’s sinfulness). If the mind were merely projecting comfort, why would it produce experiences that sometimes demand difficult moral change or upset the status quo of the person’s life? Moreover, the projection theory is essentially an assertion about motive, but it doesn’t explain the specific content of experiences, especially the commonalities across culture (did everyone project the same archetypes?). It’s one possible factor, but not a universal debunker.* Variety and Contradiction: Another major objection is that religious experiences in different religions “cancel each other out.” Christians see Christ, Hindus see Krishna, etc. They can’t all be veridical because the doctrines conflict – so maybe none of them are veridical. This is often raised by philosophers like Michael Martin or atheists generally: the diversity implies people just see what they expect or are taught. Each faith’s experiences reinforce that faith, rather than revealing an objective reality; thus culture, not God, explains them . Response: It is true that interpretations differ, but as we discussed, there are also core similarities. One possible reconciliation (from thinkers like John Hick) is that different faiths’ mystics are perceiving the same ultimate reality in their own idiom . For example, perhaps at a fundamental mystical level, a Christian feeling wrapped in love and a Hindu experiencing the bliss of Brahman are encountering the same divine love, but one names it Christ and the other Vishnu. Hick calls this ultimate reality “the Real” which manifests differently. Not everyone accepts this – exclusivist believers won’t want to equate their deity with others, and naturalists might say this is special pleading. Another counter is that divergent accounts don’t entirely cancel the possibility of a core truth: in science, observers can have different theories about a phenomenon yet the phenomenon exists (people once debated if light was particle or wave – both “experiences” of experiments were different, but light was real). Likewise, it could be that no single religion’s interpretation is 100% correct, but they are all responding to a true transcendent realm. At minimum, the diversity argument might urge caution in taking any one experience at face value (maybe the Christian didn’t literally meet Jesus but had a real encounter filtered through their mind). Defenders also point out that even ordinary perception is theory-laden and prone to error, yet we don’t dismiss all sense experience . We correct for biases rather than say “since witnesses describe an event differently, the event didn’t happen.” Perhaps God (if real) manifests in forms people can comprehend, which would naturally vary by culture .* Deception and Error: Humans can be wrong. We get drunk, we dream, we hallucinate. Could not religious experiences just be mistakes or deceptions? This encompasses honest errors (misinterpreting a natural event as supernatural) and deliberate fraud in some cases. A skeptic will note that plenty of vision claims have turned out false or conflicting – so the safest course is to doubt all of them. Also, people in emotional or physical extremes (fasting, chanting, etc.) can induce altered states that they might wrongly attribute to God. Response: Yes, humans are fallible. No one says every religious experience claim is genuine (just as not every UFO sighting is a real alien). But the existence of some false claims does not negate the possibility of true ones. Defenders emphasize case-by-case examination and the need for defeaters. If someone had an experience while on LSD, that’s a likely defeater (drugs were involved). If someone claims God told them something that is objectively false or immoral, that’s a defeater (contradiction or known falsehood). But in many cases, there are no clear defeaters: e.g., a person in normal health, not on substances, reports a one-time vision that gave them insight and left positive changes. We might not have any reason to declare it false – we can’t prove it was true, but per Swinburne’s principle, absent specific defeaters, it should be given the benefit of the doubt . Regarding fraud, while unfortunately there have been charlatans, the majority of survey-reported experiences have no incentive for the person to lie (often they gain nothing and risk ridicule). Thus, wholesale dismissal seems unwarranted. Instead, one can acknowledge “some REs are misfires or misinterpretations, but a core might be genuine.”* Natural Explanations (Psychological/Neurological): The ultimate naturalistic counter is that we can explain REs entirely without recourse to the supernatural. For instance: temporal lobe seizures, dissociative states, or evolutionary byproducts (maybe early humans who sometimes entered trance had survival advantages, so we inherited that trait of experiencing unseen agents). Could hyperactive agency detection make people sense “spirits” when there are none, as some cognitive scientists propose? There are theories that religion originates in our cognitive architecture (we detect agents in rustling grass to be safe; sometimes we over-detect and think it’s a ghost or god). Thus, religious feelings might be a byproduct of a brain optimized for other things. Response: These theories can indeed explain some generic features (why people might default to seeing intentionality in random events, etc.). But they often struggle to explain the specific richness and consistency of high-level mystical experiences. Saying “it’s all evolution” is a bit speculative – it doesn’t provide a direct mechanism for why fasting on a mountain yields a beatific vision of universal love. Also, these explanations are not mutually exclusive with theism. A theist could accept that God works through the brain’s natural capacities – perhaps evolution equipped us with a “sensus divinitatis” (sense of the divine), as theologian John Calvin posited, which is just what cognitive science is detecting except the scientists call it a byproduct while theists call it a purposeful faculty . Finally, even if every experience can have a possible natural description (neuroscience can describe what happens in the brain, psychology what happens in the mind), that still doesn’t answer the metaphysical question: is there a divine reality corresponding to and perhaps causing some of these experiences? Think of it this way: a naturalist and a theist could agree on every immediate cause (e.g. “this person prayed, their brain entered state X, they felt Y”). The naturalist stops there and says “and that’s all – no external referent.” The theist adds “God’s spirit interacted with them through that brain state.” This isn’t something an experiment can directly confirm or deny; it becomes an inference to the best explanation. If the experiences often lead to coherent insight, improved character, and sometimes contain information the person could not have otherwise known (there are a few documented cases where someone had a vision that led them to verify something in reality they didn’t previously know), then one might argue the God hypothesis is a better explanation than sheer chance firing.In weighing these critiques, a fair assessment must admit that religious experience is not incontrovertible proof of God. There are plausible alternative explanations for many aspects, and the diversity and occasional errors mean one must be careful. However, the accumulated evidence of widespread, often life-changing experiences carrying striking similarities and noetic depth suggests it’s not reasonable to write them all off as meaningless froth of the brain. Much like testimony in law: each witness might be imperfect, but if hundreds of independent witnesses report a similar event, one starts to take it seriously. As one scholar wrote, “the sheer number of them and certain patterns (e.g., life improvements post-experience) give some credibility that ‘something real’ is being tapped into” . Ultimately, whether that “something” is actually God or just a quirk of mind is contested ground.Conclusion: Part of a Cumulative CaseIn closing, where does religious experience stand as evidence for the informational-theological model of God’s existence? Religious experience is not a deductive proof of God, nor does it claim to be. But it is a significant inductive datum. It provides transformative personal evidence for those who experience it, often convincing them beyond any abstract argument. And at the social level, the global prevalence and consistency of such experiences serve as a modest but real pointer toward a transcendent reality. In our probabilistic model, we treat RE as one variable – one thread in a tapestry of evidences. It would be unwise to base one’s entire belief solely on others’ experiences (just as one datum doesn’t make a scientific theory), but when combined with other factors (cosmology, morality, consciousness, etc.), it strengthens the overall case for theism.To summarize the key takeaways of this chapter:* Religious experiences are common across cultures and history, reported by a large fraction of humanity . They are often deeply meaningful and life-changing, lending them prima facie credibility beyond mere hallucinations.* According to Swinburne’s Principle of Credulity, we should trust these experiences as prima facie evidence of God unless we have specific reasons to doubt them . There is no universal defeater that applies to all cases – each experience must be evaluated on its merits, and many pass basic tests of credibility.* Cross-culturally, while the interpretations differ (Christian, Muslim, Hindu, etc.), there are common experiential cores (unity, transcendence, encounter with the sacred) suggesting an underlying reality or at least a common human capacity for perceiving the transcendent .* Major research efforts (James, Hardy, contemporary studies) have documented thousands of accounts and found positive effects and phenomenological patterns, reinforcing that RE is a genuine part of human experience, not a fringe anomaly .* Neuroscience shows correlates in the brain (temporal and parietal lobe changes, neurotransmitter involvement) but does not disprove the reality of the experience’s object . The brain could be the instrument through which humans experience God, analogous to how a radio is needed to experience a broadcast. Neural correlation should be expected if God interacts with people via natural processes – it doesn’t render the experiences unreliable by default.* For the experiencer, RE can provide rational warrant to believe in God (some philosophers even call certain religious experiences self-authenticating). For others, one person’s testimony might carry limited weight, but the statistical ensemble of experiences (especially shared elements and convergent themes) offers some corroborative support for belief in God . It is part of the cumulative case – perhaps not as universally persuasive as arguments like fine-tuning, but it crucially bridges abstract arguments with lived human reality . It shows that if God exists, belief in God isn’t just an inference but can be experienced, at least by some, which fits the profile of a personal God concerned with relationship.In our probabilistic model, therefore, we included Religious Experience as a positive variable with a modest coefficient, reflecting that it does contribute to the probability of God’s existence, albeit not overwhelmingly . The weight was enough to acknowledge its evidential value, but moderated by recognition of the ambiguities. This aligns with most scholars’ conclusions: REs collectively offer real evidence in favor of theism, but not conclusively so . They are one line among many – not a trump card, but an important clue. And for the individuals involved, they remain perhaps the most compelling clue of all, one that often changes lives in profound ways.Ultimately, the informational-theological model treats human religious experiences as data points that any comprehensive account of reality must explain. Theism provides a straightforward explanation: there is a divine reality and people sometimes perceive it. Naturalism must resort to a patchwork of psychological and neurological explanations, which can certainly cover some aspects but leave a lingering question: why do so many people consistently feel touched by a transcendent love or presence that isn’t objectively “there”? The answer one leans toward will depend on one’s broader worldview commitments and assessment of the cumulative evidence. Religious experience will likely continue to be experienced – and debated – as long as humans walk the Earth. In the next part of this work, we proceed to evaluate further variables in our model, but with the understanding that the personal and social evidence of religious experience remains a unique and vital part of the discussion about God’s reality, marrying the quantitative prevalence with the qualitative depth of humanity’s spiritual encounters.References (Harvard Style)Alister Hardy Religious Experience Research Centre (2024).
