We now synthesize these insights into a single probabilistic model. The core idea is that each evidential factor can be treated as a binary variable indicating whether that evidence is present in our world (1) or not (0). We combine eight such variables in a logistic regression model estimating the probability that God exists given all the evidence. The eight variables (introduced and justified in previous chapters) are:* FT – Fine-Tuning of the Universe: 1 if the fundamental constants/initial conditions of the universe are “fine-tuned” for life.* MO – Moral Objectivity: 1 if objective moral truths exist.* CG – Consciousness Gap: 1 if consciousness has features that physical science cannot fully explain (“the hard problem”).* RE – Religious Experience: 1 if there is reliable testimony of experiences apparently of the divine or transcendent.* **EV – Severity of Moral Evil: Here set to 0 (neutral) by reinterpretation – representing that the observed evils in the world are not being counted as net evidence against God (Chapter 6’s theodicy analysis).* BC – Biological Complexity: 1 if the complexity of life (especially the origin of life and biological information) is extremely high and not easily explained by mindless processes.* QM – Quantum “Mind” Indicators: 1 if quantum physics provides hints of mind-like or information-centric foundations to reality.* MN – Metaphysical Necessity (Contingency): 1 if the universe is contingent (not self-explanatory) and thus demands a necessary being or cause.In reality, all eight factors are “on”: we do observe a life-friendly finely tuned cosmos, objective moral values, conscious minds, widespread religious experiences, significant evil and suffering, intricate life complexity, puzzling quantum phenomena, and a contingent universe. The probabilistic model will quantify how strongly each of these (individually and together) points toward the existence of God.The logistic regression model expresses the log-odds of God’s existence as a linear sum of these evidences . Formally, we write:\text{logit}[P(\text{God exists})] \;=\; \beta_0 \;+\; \beta_{FT}\cdot FT + \beta_{MO}\cdot MO + \beta_{CG}\cdot CG + \beta_{RE}\cdot RE + \beta_{EV}\cdot EV + \beta_{BC}\cdot BC + \beta_{QM}\cdot QM + \beta_{MN}\cdot MN,where $\text{logit}[p] = \ln!\frac{p}{1-p}$ is the log-odds. The coefficient $\beta_0$ is an intercept representing the “prior” log-odds (the baseline before considering these eight evidences). We will take $\beta_0 = 0$ for a neutral 50/50 prior , meaning we start agnostic and let the evidence move the probability. Each $\beta_{\text{X}}$ (for evidence X) is positive if that evidence tends to make God’s existence more likely, negative if it counts against God (we will see $\beta_{EV}$ is ~0 after reinterpreting evil). Chapters 5–8 provided rough estimates for these coefficients based on the latest literature and arguments. In essence, $\exp(\beta_{\text{X}})$ is the odds multiplier for God’s existence if evidence X is present. For example, if $\beta_{\text{FT}}\approx +2.5$, then a life-permitting finely tuned universe multiplies the odds of God by $e^{2.5}\approx 12$, all else equal.Table 9.1 below summarizes the chosen coefficient values. These are point estimates informed by prior chapters, with citations to support each:Table 9.1 – Logistic Model Coefficients (Evidence Weights)Variable (Evidence)DescriptionCoefficient ? (log-odds)FT – Fine-TuningPhysical constants “just right” for life+2.5MO – Moral ObjectivityObjective moral truths exist+1.0CG – Consciousness GapConsciousness not reducible to matter+1.5RE – Religious ExperienceCredible experiential evidence of divine+0.8EV – Severity of Evil (neutralized)Profound evil/suffering in world (treated neutral)0.0BC – Biological ComplexityHigh complexity/information in life+1.5QM – Quantum “Mind” hintsMind-like aspects in quantum physics?+0.3MN – Metaphysical NecessityContingent reality needs necessary cause+1.5?? – Intercept (prior)Baseline log-odds (50% prior)0.0Figure 9.1 below visualizes these weights and their plausible ranges from the literature (as discussed in the Monte Carlo analysis ahead). Each horizontal line spans a range of plausible $\beta$ values, and the black dot marks our chosen baseline. All coefficients are positive, meaning none of these evidences, as we interpret them, counts against the existence of God. Notably, EV (evil) has ? set to ~0 – a dramatic change from traditional arguments where evil is strong negative evidence. Chapter 6’s analysis of soul-making and free-will theodicies justified treating evil as epistemically neutral . This means that, in our model, observing horrific evil doesn’t significantly lower the probability of God, because we assume (consistent with those theodicies) that such evil could exist even if God exists for greater purposes or as a consequence of free creaturely action.Figure 9.1: Estimated evidential weights (log-odds coefficients) for each variable in the logistic model. Higher values indicate stronger evidence for God’s existence. Fine-Tuning (FT) has the largest weight, reflecting the consensus that a life-permitting cosmos is extremely unlikely under atheism but expected under theism . Consciousness (CG) and Biological Complexity (BC) are also assigned substantial positive weights, indicating these phenomena are more expected if a divine Mind created the universe . Moral Objectivity (MO) contributes a moderate positive weight, consistent with arguments that if moral truths are real, a transcendent ground (God) makes them more intelligible . Religious Experience (RE) has a smaller but non-negligible weight, given the widespread reports of encounters with the divine . Quantum indicators (QM) have the smallest weight – they are speculative “wild cards” with only tentative support . The Evil variable (EV) is centered at 0, signifying our revised stance that the existence of significant evil, by itself, does not tilt the odds against God once plausible theistic explanations are accounted for (Hick 1966; Plantinga 1974). Each line shows the range of $\beta$ explored in sensitivity simulations (see §9.3), capturing scholarly uncertainty. All intervals lie to the right of 0 except EV, indicating that none of these evidences, in our judgment, favors atheism. The Logistic Equation in ActionWith all coefficients set, the logistic equation can be used to compute the probability of God’s existence. Let us first plug in all eight evidences as present (1), corresponding to our actual world:* FT = 1 (our universe is fine-tuned for life, by observation);* MO = 1 (we assume objective moral values exist, as argued in Chapter 6);* CG = 1 (conscious, introspective minds clearly exist, with an explanatory gap in physical science);* RE = 1 (there are abundant religious experiences reported historically and today);* EV = 1 (there is certainly tremendous moral evil/suffering in the world – but recall ?_EV=0 by our paradigm);* BC = 1 (life exhibits high complexity and “specified information,” especially in DNA and cellular machinery);* QM = 1 (quantum physics shows phenomena like wavefunction collapse and non-locality that some interpret as hinting at mind or information underpinning reality);* MN = 1 (the universe is not self-explanatory; something outside it must exist necessarily, if one accepts the Principle of Sufficient Reason).Summing the log-odds contributions:$\text{logit}(P) = \beta_0 + \beta_{FT}+\beta_{MO}+\cdots+\beta_{MN} = 0.0 + 2.5 + 1.0 + 1.5 + 0.8 + 0.0 + 1.5 + 0.3 + 1.5 = 9.1$ .This total log-odds corresponds to a probabilityP(\text{God exists} \mid \text{all evidence}) = \frac{1}{1 + e^{-9.1}} \approx 0.99999,essentially 99.99% . In other words, given all the evidences in our actual world and our chosen weights, the model’s posterior probability for God’s existence is ~99.99%. This quantitative result must be interpreted carefully – it does not mean we have “proved” God’s existence with absolute certainty (we will discuss model limitations shortly). Rather, it reflects that if our weighting of each piece of evidence is roughly correct, then the cumulative effect of all eight pieces of evidence is overwhelmingly in favor of theism. The log-odds sum of 9.1 is very high; even if one or two of the ?’s were overestimated, the remainder would still keep the probability near the upper bound. For perspective, if we halved every coefficient (assuming a much more skeptical take on each argument), the log-odds would be $9.1/2 = 4.55$, giving $P \approx 98.95%$ – still extremely high . Even cutting all weights to a quarter (drastically downplaying each evidence) yields log-odds $2.275$ and $P \approx 90.7%$ . Thus, under this model, one would have to dismiss or nullify almost all evidences simultaneously to bring the probability down to modest levels. This highlights the robustness of a cumulative-case approach: multiple independent clues, each modestly favoring theism, together can make atheism exceedingly implausible unless one is prepared to deny nearly every clue.It is illuminating to consider some specific scenarios and see how the model’s output shifts:* Scenario 1: All Evidence Present (the actual world). We already computed this: logit = 9.1, $P \approx 0.9999$ (odds ~$10^4$:1 in favor of God). We should not pretend this number is precise – but it illustrates that given our evidential assessment, the case for God is, in our model, overwhelming. Atheism’s posterior (?0.01%) is vanishingly small under the full weight of evidence. In colloquial terms, the model would say it is thousands of times more likely that God exists given our world’s data than that He does not.* Scenario 2: Counterfactual “More Evil, Less Good” World. To see the impact of our reinterpretation of evil, suppose (counter to our paradigm) that we did treat observed evil as strong negative evidence (as the classical argument from evil would). For example, let $\beta_{EV}$ be -2.0 (instead of 0) – a hefty negative weight corresponding to the idea that the suffering in the world is very improbable if God exists . Then, even keeping the other ?’s the same, the total log-odds would drop to $9.1 + (-2.0) = 7.1$, giving $P \approx 99.92%$. That is still extremely high (99.9%). In other words, even a strong evidential problem of evil could only dent the cumulative case modestly – our other evidences are so weighty that theism would remain the clear winner. (In the original version of the model in Chapter 5, other coefficients were a bit lower, so a negative EV had a somewhat larger effect, but the main point stands: including evil as negative might have brought the probability down to, say, 95% or 90% in some cases – still more likely than not). By neutralizing EV via soul-making and related theodicies, we have removed one of the only significant negative contributors, thereby raising the overall probability a few percentage points and eliminating low-probability outcomes in the simulations (see §9.3). The philosophical takeaway is that how one interprets the problem of evil can materially change the overall credence: in our assessment, plausible theistic explanations for evil mean this factor no longer “penalizes” the God hypothesis .* Scenario 3: Fine-Tuning Absent. What if the physical universe showed no signs of fine-tuning? This is a hypothetical world where the constants of nature fell in a generic life-prohibiting range (so FT = 0). Our model would then rely on the other evidences. Setting FT = 0 (and keeping all others at 1) gives log-odds $= 9.1 - 2.5 = 6.6$, so $P \approx 99.6%$. Still extremely high. In fact, even if we simultaneously set FT = 0, and remove two other major evidences – say CG = 0 (imagine consciousness was easily reducible to brain matter) and RE = 0 (imagine no one ever had any kind of religious or mystical experience) – the model yields log-odds $\approx 2.8$ and $P \approx 94%$ . Thus, even a world with no fine-tuning, no mind-body gap, and no religious experiences, but with moral realism, life’s complexity, quantum oddities, and contingency, would still come out >90% in favor of God. This underscores that theism is not solely resting on cosmology; other domains (philosophical and biological) carry the argument even if physics had turned out differently.* Scenario 4: “Moral Anti-Realist Atheist” World. Consider a more extreme naturalistic world: no fine-tuning (FT=0), no religious experiences (RE=0), consciousness fully explained (CG=0), and suppose moral truths do not exist (MO=0) – i.e. an amoral cosmos. Even there, if life exists at all, we likely still have BC = 1 and perhaps the universe’s contingency (MN=1). Plugging these in: log-odds $\approx 1.8$, giving $P \approx 86%$ . So even in a rather bleak, materialistic world lacking many positive evidences, our model might say theism is ~5–6 times more likely than not, thanks chiefly to the contingency of existence (and to a lesser degree any remaining hints like quantum or the sheer existence of conscious life). If we also remove contingency (MN = 0, effectively saying one is content with brute fact universe), the probability would drop further – indeed if all evidences were turned off (FT, MO, CG, RE, BC, QM, MN all 0), we’d have log-odds = 0 (just the intercept), $P = 50%$. In other words, a person who denied every pro-theistic evidence and insisted nothing about reality needs explanation beyond brute fact would end up truly agnostic per our model (50/50). It’s telling that to get to pure agnosticism or atheism in this framework, one must zero-out all the clues – essentially assume a world with no sign of God in any domain. Such a world is not the one we inhabit. In summary, using the point estimates from Chapters 5–8, the combined evidences yield a credence for God’s existence very close to 100%. The model suggests that a rational agent updating on all these independent lines of evidence should assign an overwhelmingly high probability to the existence of God. While one should not take the exact percentage too literally, the order of magnitude (upper 90s) indicates a strong convergence of evidence. This addresses the question posed at the start of Part II: can disparate arguments for God be combined in a rigorous way, and if so, what do they indicate? According to our logistic model, they indicate that belief in God can be defended as overwhelmingly likely given the total evidence.Before moving on, we emphasize two points: (1) This result hinges on our assessments of each piece of evidence. Later in this chapter we will examine how uncertainties in the ?’s affect the outcome (through Monte Carlo analysis). (2) A high modeled probability does not mean absolute logical proof. It means that within this probabilistic framework – which inevitably involves subjective judgment calls on weighting – the evidence strongly favors theism. We will discuss remaining limitations and why one must avoid overclaiming. First, though, let us explore the model’s outcomes in a more nuanced way via simulation and decision tree analysis.Monte Carlo Simulation of UncertaintyGiven the subjective elements in choosing the $\beta$ values, it is critical to test how robust the conclusion is under plausible variation. In Chapter 8 we identified reasonable ranges for each coefficient based on scholarly debate. Now we perform a Monte Carlo simulation: we will randomly sample each ? from a broad distribution within those ranges, recompute the probability of God for each random draw, and aggregate the results. This simulates the “spread” of outcomes for many combinations of assumptions – essentially asking: if different philosophers or scientists had somewhat different but reasonable weightings, how often would the conclusion still be that God’s existence is likely?.Simulation setup: We drew $N = 100,000$ random coefficient sets. For each run, every ? was sampled from a uniform distribution over a plausible interval (reflecting uncertainty in that evidence’s strength) . For example, $\beta_{FT}$ was sampled uniformly between 0 and +4.0. The low end (0) would correspond to an extreme skeptical view that fine-tuning has no explanatory force (perhaps a multiverse guarantees life-friendly universes), and the high end (+4) corresponds to a view that fine-tuning alone gives an odds ratio of $e^4 \approx 54$ in favor of God – still far below some apologists’ estimates (which can exceed +10 in log-odds), but a strong effect. We similarly sampled $\beta_{MO}$ from 0 to +2 (ranging from moral evidence being irrelevant to fairly strong), $\beta_{CG}$ from +0.5 to +2.0 (most agree consciousness offers some clue, but how much is debated) , $\beta_{RE}$ from 0 to +1.5 (religious experience could be negligible or moderately supportive) , $\beta_{EV}$ from -0.5 to +0.5 (allowing a small negative or positive in case one thinks evil still challenges or perhaps even confirms theism a bit) , $\beta_{BC}$ from 0 to +2.5 (opinions vary if biology is fully explained or still indicative of design) , $\beta_{QM}$ from 0 to +1.0 (quantum clues might do nothing or give a slight hint) , and $\beta_{MN}$ from 0 to +3.0 (some may not feel contingency requires God at all, others see it as very compelling) . The intercept ?? was sampled from -0.5 to 0 (allowing up to a modestly skeptical prior 40% if one prefers) . For each random draw of the 9 coefficients, we computed the implied $P(\text{God})$ (with all variables set to 1, since our world exhibits all evidences).The distribution of outcomes was strikingly skewed towards high probabilities. Figure 9.2 shows a histogram of the resulting $P(\text{God})$ across 100,000 simulations. (Note: a log scale is used on the frequency axis to make the lower-probability tail visible.) The vast majority of simulation runs yielded $P$ extremely close to 1 (the rightmost bin). In fact, about 85% of all runs gave $P>90%$, and ~99% gave $P>50%$ – meaning in almost all random weight combinations, the evidence still favored God over not . The median of the distribution was $P \approx 98.6%$, and the mean was $P \approx 94.6%$ . A small minority of trials (on the far left of the histogram) yielded more mixed results: for example, in the most extremely “atheism-favoring” draw, only about $P\approx 3.7%$ resulted . That pathological case required simultaneously sampling every evidence at its minimum influence (practically assuming all our evidences turned out to be flukes or had naturalistic explanations) – a combination essentially corresponding to an all-out skeptical stance. Such draws were exceedingly rare (<<0.1%). The interquartile range was approximately [97%, >99.9%] , indicating that even the “25th percentile skeptic” still got a ~97% chance of God in this model.Figure 9.2: Probability distribution of $P(\text{God exists})$ resulting from 100,000 Monte Carlo simulation runs of the logistic model. The histogram’s horizontal axis is the output probability, and the vertical axis (log scale) shows how many simulations yielded that probability. Most runs are at the far right (near $P=1$) – the tallest bar represents probabilities in the 0.98–1.00 range, which occurred in the great majority of trials. A much smaller portion of runs (the smaller bars to the left) yielded probabilities in the 50–90% range, and an extremely tiny fraction fell below 50%. In only ~1.21% of simulations did $P(\text{God})$ come out $\le 50%$ (essentially coin-flip or worse for theism) . Conversely, in about 85% of simulations, $P>90%$ . The median outcome was ~98–99%, and the mean ~95%. This demonstrates that even allowing substantial uncertainty in each evidence’s strength, the cumulative case remains robustly in favor of theism. Only by assuming across-the-board minimal impacts (far left tail) does the probability drop to modest levels. The distribution is highly skewed (note the log scale on frequency), reflecting that most plausible weightings of evidence lead to near-certain belief in God, with a long tail of very skeptical scenarios dragging the average down slightly. In short, given the range of credible scholarly opinion on each factor, the model very often yields a conclusion of >90% confidence in God’s existence. This reinforces that our qualitative claim – that the evidences taken together strongly support theism – is not an artifact of one peculiar set of weights, but holds under a wide range of assumptions. To further visualize the cumulative impact, Figure 9.3 plots the cumulative distribution function (CDF) of the simulation outcomes. This curve shows, for any probability threshold on the x-axis, the proportion of simulation runs that fell below that probability on the y-axis. We have marked key reference lines at 50%, 90%, and 99%. You can see that the curve stays near 0 (meaning almost no runs had low probabilities) until very high probabilities – it only begins to rise noticeably after ~90%. For instance, the curve crosses 0.5 (half the runs) at around $P\approx0.986$, confirming the median ~98.6%. It reaches 0.9 (90% of runs) at ~$P \approx 0.999$ (indicating 90% of simulations gave >99.9% credence). The take-away is that under almost any reasonable weighting, the model yields a very high belief in God. Only when one assumes simultaneously that fine-tuning has no import, morality has no import, consciousness offers no clue, etc. (pushing all evidences to their skeptic extremes) does the cumulative probability dip to more agnostic values (seen in the sudden uptick of the CDF at the extreme right). The mean of 94–95% is lower than the median because of those rare extreme-skeptic cases that create a long left tail. But even 94% is an extraordinarily high degree of belief by ordinary standards – for comparison, that’s a stronger probability than many scientific conclusions or everyday factual beliefs carry.Figure 9.3: Cumulative distribution of the probability of God’s existence based on Monte Carlo simulations. The purple curve plots $F(p) = P(P(\text{God}) \le p)$ for the simulation outcomes. It stays near 0 for most of the range, then rapidly climbs to 1 at the upper end, reflecting the highly skewed results. Dotted lines are shown at $p=0.5$, $0.9$, and $0.99$ and the corresponding cumulative fractions. We see that $P(\text{God})>0.5$ in about 99% of runs (the curve is nearly at 1.0 by the time $p=0.5$) ; $P>0.9$ in ~85% of runs ; and $P>0.99$ in well over half the runs. Only a very small fraction of trials (the last few percent) yielded moderate probabilities in the 20–80% range, causing the curve’s jump near $p=1.0$. This means that unless multiple evidences are far weaker than our base estimates simultaneously, the model will output $P(\text{God})$ very close to 1. The probability that all evidences are on the low end is itself very low (assuming independent uncertainties), hence the model’s confidence is robust. In practical terms, one could say: given our current scientific and philosophical evidence, even allowing for reasonable doubt on each piece, one can be >95% confident in God’s existence. This aligns with philosopher Richard Swinburne’s observation that a cumulative case can yield high credence – he famously argued for something like “(roughly) 97%” after combining independent arguments (Swinburne 2004, ch.13), a number our simulation interestingly echoes . We also ran a comparative Monte Carlo where we restored EV as a negative factor (to mimic a worldview that sees evil as evidence against God). In that experiment, we sampled $\beta_{EV}$ from a negative range (e.g. -2 to -1) instead of around 0 , while keeping the other distributions the same. The result was that the mean $P$ dropped to ~87% (from 95%), and far fewer runs gave >99% (the upper tail was pulled down) . In about 65% of runs $P>90%$ in that scenario (compared to ~85% before). In a few percent of runs, $P$ fell below 50%. In other words, treating evil as a strong anti-theistic evidence makes the outcome less uniformly pro-God – some combinations of weights then lead to more equivocal results. This confirms quantitatively that the problem of evil, if not “neutralized,” can meaningfully lower one’s confidence in God (though in our simulation it still usually didn’t tip the scales to atheism; it mostly took one from “extremely certain” down to “fairly certain”) . By contrast, our updated model (with EV ~ 0) yields a more consistently high confidence. This exercise shows how philosophical assumptions (here, the interpretation of evil) impact the numerical output – a reminder that the model is only as good as its inputs. We believe we had good reason to set EV’s weight near zero (as defended in Chapter 6), but one could certainly question that, and Appendix C provides a detailed analysis of the model under a traditional EV negative assumption (showing still a strong but somewhat reduced case for theism) .In sum, the Monte Carlo analysis strengthens our conclusion: the model’s verdict of a very high probability of God’s existence is robust. Even accounting for uncertainties and allowing the evidential weights to vary, in the vast majority of plausible cases the cumulative evidence still points strongly toward theism. This addresses potential concerns that our result might be an artifact of cherry-picked assumptions – instead, it appears to be a stable outcome under a wide range of assumptions. One can reasonably tweak the inputs and still end up, more often than not, with $P(\text{God})$ in the 90+% range. Only a concerted minimalization of every piece of evidence (an overall skeptical stance on all fronts) yields an agnostic result, which is itself an extreme position. Thus, a person who accepts even a few of these arguments to a modest degree should, according to the model, have a strong cumulative reason to believe in God.Decision Tree Analysis: Which Evidence Matters Most?While the logistic regression gives us a quantitative measure and the simulation validates its robustness, it is also useful to have a more qualitative, visual summary of how the evidences combine. For this purpose, we constructed a decision tree based on the model . A decision tree is essentially a flowchart that asks yes/no questions (in our case, about whether an evidence is present) and splits cases accordingly, aiming to classify whether “God exists” or “No God” at the end. By examining the tree, we can see which evidences are most pivotal to the final outcome.Using all $2^8 = 256$ possible combinations of the eight variables and labeling each combination as yielding “God exists” if $P>0.5$ (more than 50% probability) or “No God” if $P<0.5$, we trained a decision tree algorithm to separate the combinations . We truncated the tree to four levels for simplicity (beyond that, splits would just reflect finer distinctions with minor variables). Figure 9.4 shows the resulting decision tree (with color-coded nodes: blue = majority of those cases lead to “God exists” classification, orange = majority “No God”). Each node is labeled with the evidence it splits on and the percentage of scenarios reaching that node.The first split (at the root) is on Fine-Tuning (FT) – indicating FT was the single most informative variable . This makes sense: in our model, a universe without fine-tuning (FT=0) starts off with a significant deficit for the God hypothesis, whereas if FT=1 (as in reality), the odds are immediately strongly tilted toward God. The tree’s right branch (FT present) is entirely blue, meaning if the universe is fine-tuned, the model almost always ends up classifying “God exists” regardless of the status of other variables . In fact, at the FT=1 node, the model’s median probability was already ~$P\approx0.91$ (as noted in the node) – so just from knowing we have a life-permitting cosmos, we are ~91% toward believing in God, before even considering other evidence . This underscores how decisive fine-tuning is (we saw ?_FT was the largest). On the other hand, the left branch (FT absent) is colored orange at the top, indicating that if the universe had not been fine-tuned for life, the initial lean would be “No God” (the model would start skeptical if the cosmos looked random and hostile to life). However, that branch does not remain orange all the way – it has further splits.On the FT=1 side (right side of the tree), the next most important splits turned out to be Consciousness (CG) and Biological Complexity (BC) . Essentially, if fine-tuning is present, the tree then asks: is consciousness of a fundamentally non-material character (CG=1)? If yes, that provides an additional push – indeed in the tree, one branch is FT=1 and CG=1, which ends up blue with virtually 100% God classification. If instead CG were 0 (suppose minds were easily explained by matter), the tree might then consider BC: is life’s complexity high and improbable (BC=1)? In the tree, one path is FT=1, CG=0, BC=1 – which still ends up blue (God exists). In fact, under FT=1, any combination of CG or BC being 1 is sufficient to keep the outcome “God”. Only if both CG and BC were (hypothetically) 0 would it check a lower factor (in some runs MO showed up at level 3) . But in our actual world, CG=1 and BC=1, so we go down the rightmost branch: FT=1, CG=1, BC=1, and whatever the remaining inputs (MO, RE, etc.), the leaf is deep blue (“God exists” with >99.9% probability).On the FT=0 side (left side), the tree shows a different sequence: if fine-tuning were absent (node initially orange), the next question that significantly affects the outcome is Metaphysical Necessity (MN) . This aligns with intuition: if the physical universe didn’t offer evidence, the model leans no-God unless one still has a strong philosophical reason, namely the contingency of the universe requiring a necessary being. Indeed, the tree’s left branch says: FT=0 and then ask MN – if MN=1 (i.e. one affirms that the universe needs an ultimate cause), that can turn the tide to blue . In the tree, FT=0 and MN=1 ends up with many blue leaves (especially if other things like MO or CG are also 1) . For instance, one path in the tree is: FT=0, MN=1, MO=1, CG=1 leads to a “God exists” classification (the model says even without fine-tuning, the presence of moral objectivity, consciousness, and a contingent universe still make God likely) . Conversely, if FT=0 and MN=0 (universe not fine-tuned and one sees no need for a necessary being), the model is likely to conclude “No God” (that scenario basically means a self-sufficient, lifeless universe – very atheism-friendly). The Moral Objectivity (MO) variable appears at Level 3 on one branch of the tree – indicating that, especially in the FT-absent case, having MO=1 can tip some outcomes. For example, FT=0, MN=1, MO=1 gave a higher probability than FT=0, MN=1, MO=0 in our calculations; thus MO helped swing some of the FT=0 cases into the “God” category (albeit at lower levels). Meanwhile, Religious Experience (RE) and Quantum (QM) did not appear in the first four levels of the tree . This means they had relatively little incremental classification power – likely because by the time the tree accounted for FT, CG, BC, MN, etc., most cases were already decided, and adding RE or QM didn’t change the class. In effect, RE and QM are “along for the ride”: they do contribute to the overall probability in the logistic model, but they didn’t need to split any branch in the decision tree. We shouldn’t interpret that too strongly – if we allowed a deeper tree, eventually RE or QM might split some very specific leaf nodes. But their omission in the top tiers confirms that they are the weaker evidences of the eight (as we expected from their smaller ?’s). Finally, as expected, EV (evil) is entirely absent from the tree . Since EV is always 1 (evil exists in all scenarios) and we set ?_EV ~ 0, it provides no information gain – the tree rightfully ignores it. If EV had a negative weight, we would have seen a split like “EV <= 0.5” (i.e. is there low evil?) at some point distinguishing better outcomes. Its exclusion here visually confirms that we have effectively pruned the problem of evil from influencing the decision .Overall, the decision tree provides a clear hierarchy of evidences in this model: Cosmological fine-tuning sits at the top; consciousness and life’s complexity come next (if FT is present), and metaphysical contingency comes next (if FT were absent). Moral values play a supportive role down the line. Religious experiences and quantum phenomena are largely overshadowed by the bigger factors. And evil – by design – plays no role in distinguishing the truth-value of “God exists” in the updated model. This matches our intuitive discussion in earlier chapters. It also matches other philosophers’ assessments of a cumulative case: for instance, Richard Swinburne (2004) argued that the fine-tuning of the universe and the existence of consciousness are among the most powerful evidences for theism, while things like minor miracles or personal experiences add less weight. Our tree and logistic model echo that prioritization.Figure: Color-coded Decision Tree visualizing the logistic regression model for God’s existence. Each node displays the posterior probability $P(\text{God exists})$ given the evidence combination up to that point. Binary splits use 1 (evidence present) and 0 (absent) on the arrows. Nodes are shaded by probability: deep blue for >99%, light blue for 85–99%, grey for ~50%, and orange for <50%. The tree flows from the most influential evidence at the root (FT) through secondary factors (e.g. MN, CG, BC) to less influential ones (RE, QM, EV). All eight variables are included (FT, MO, CG, RE, EV, BC, QM, MN), with brief definitions provided in the legend. Fine-Tuning (FT) at the root splits the scenarios: if FT=1 (right branch), the probability of God’s existence is high (node colored light blue, $P\approx92%$ at that stage); if FT=0 (left branch), the model leans lower (grey node, $P\approx50%$) unless other evidence intervenes. Subsequent splits show MN and CG on the FT-absent side, and CG and BC under FT-present, reflecting their relative importance. Moral Objectivity (MO) appears at Level 3 on one branch (FT=0, MN=0) when other major evidences are missing. Religious Experience (RE) and Quantum Mind (QM) factors come into play at deeper levels, indicating their more minor impact. The Problem of Evil (EV) is neutralized (coefficient ~0) and thus does not affect probabilities – it appears only at the bottom, yielding identical outcomes (both leaves 50%). This tree illustrates how combinations of evidence drive the posterior probability: for instance, FT=1, CG=1, BC=1 leads to a deep blue node (nearly certain $P\approx99.6%$), whereas with no positive evidence the outcome is grey (~50% agnostic). The color legend (bottom-left) and variable glossary (bottom-right) ensure the diagram is self-contained and interpretable for inclusion in a scholarly document.Figure 9.4: *Decision tree illustrating how the model classifies combinations of evidence as “God exists” (blue) or “No God” (orange). (Each split condition is of the form “Variable <= 0.5”, which essentially asks whether that binary variable is 0 or 1; e.g. “FT <= 0.5” branches left if FT=0, right if FT=1). Top node: Fine-Tuning (FT) – the tree first asks if the universe is fine-tuned. If yes, we go right, and the node is blue since virtually all such cases lead to God’s existence in the model. If no, we go left, an orange node (no fine-tuning initially tilts toward atheism). Right side: Given FT, the next split is on Consciousness (CG). If consciousness has a non-physical aspect (CG=1, go left at that node), the subsequent node is blue with God as the class; if CG=0 (go right), the tree then asks about Biological Complexity (BC). Either presence of high complexity (BC=1) or earlier presence of CG=1 suffices to turn the branch blue – meaning with a fine-tuned universe, either consciousness or complex life alone makes God’s existence the majority outcome. Left side: If FT is absent, the next question is Metaphysical Necessity (MN) – essentially, do we have a contingency argument for God? If yes (MN=1, go left), that can salvage a blue outcome down the line (especially if other factors like MO or CG are also present). If no (MN=0, go right), the branch stays orange – in a universe with no fine-tuning and no need for a necessary being, the model leans No God. Lower in the left branch, Moral Objectivity (MO) appears (one branch shows MO splitting), indicating that if FT=0 and MN=1, having MO=1 can help push the outcome to God (blue leaf) whereas MO=0 might leave it orange. Variables not shown in the first 4 levels: Religious Experience (RE) and Quantum (QM) did not appear, meaning their absence or presence didn’t change the classification in most cases once other variables were considered. And Evil (EV) is notably absent, reflecting its neutral role. This tree aligns with our coefficient magnitudes: Fine-tuning is most crucial; consciousness and life’s complexity are next; contingency (MN) is a key fallback in the absence of physical evidence; moral facts contribute in some scenarios; religious experience and quantum hints are minor; and evil (with ? ~ 0) is irrelevant. In plainer terms, the decision tree tells a story consistent with classical “cumulative case” apologetics: “If the universe is fine-tuned for life, that is a huge clue (likely God). If it weren’t, but still the universe exists contingently and we find ourselves with moral consciences and consciousness, that combination can still point to God. No single piece of evidence (except a totally non-fine-tuned universe) makes the model say ‘No God’ – it’s only the conjunction of no fine-tuning, no need for a creator, no moral truths, etc., that yields a negative verdict. In our actual world, however, we have fine-tuning, we have consciousness, we have moral values… so by the end of the tree’s path, it’s “God exists” with ~100% probability.” This mirrors the intuitive idea that while each argument has its conditions, theism can be supported by multiple independent “routes.” Even if one route weren’t available (say, physical evidence), another route (metaphysical or moral) could still lead one to God – perhaps less directly, but still sufficiently . The tree also vividly shows that the only way to get a definitive “No God” in the model is to have almost all evidences absent. In fact, no node in the tree ended in an outright certain “No God” except the top-left hypothetical (FT absent and then presumably MN absent, CG absent, etc.) . This visual corroborates our earlier finding: only a near-total lack of pro-theistic evidences would result in a confident atheistic conclusion from this model.Interpreting the Results and LimitationsThe results of our probabilistic model, taken at face value, indicate that the credence for God’s existence can rationally be very high given the total evidence. A probability on the order of 95–99% (with our baseline ~99.99%) suggests that, within this framework, it is rational to be extremely confident in God. This provides a quantitative backing to the common claim by theists that “the evidence cumulatively strongly supports belief in God.” It also undercuts the oft-heard claim that theistic belief is a mere leap of faith against the evidence – on the contrary, when weighing a broad array of evidences systematically, we find the scales tipped heavily toward theism.However, it is crucial to not overstate what this model accomplishes. A few important caveats and reflections:* Model Dependence: The probability figures (e.g. 99%) are conditional on the model’s structure and inputs. They are not direct empirical measurements; they are the result of a chain of reasoning with many assumptions. Change the assumptions (e.g., assign different priors, different ? values outside our considered ranges, or include different evidences) and the numbers would change. We explored a wide range, but not an infinite range. Thus, one should not go around claiming “science proves God with 99% certainty” – that would misrepresent these results. Rather, the fair statement is: under a reasonable modeling of current evidence, theism emerges as highly probable. That leaves room for reasonable disagreement about the model itself.* Correlation and Overlap: The logistic model treats these evidences as if they were independent contributors. In reality, some evidences might overlap or derive from a common cause. We tried to minimize overlap (e.g. treating FT and BC as distinct – cosmic design vs. biological design – which is defensible since even a fine-tuned universe might need further help to produce life) . But one could argue that consciousness (CG) and moral truths (MO) are somewhat connected (both might be seen as aspects of a non-material reality or soul). Or that fine-tuning and contingency are connected (FT is one specific way the universe points beyond itself). If evidences overlap, then adding their log-odds might double-count some support. We have been mindful of this (noting, for example, that we chose not to double-count the existence of moral evil as a separate “evidence” since the moral dimension of evil is already captured under MO). The decision tree helps show that while evidences are correlated (e.g. in all high-probability worlds, multiple evidences co-occur), each provides a somewhat independent reason to believe. Still, this is a limitation: the model assumes independence for simplicity . A more complex Bayesian network could model dependencies but that’s beyond our scope. We believe the independence assumption, while not strictly true, does not wildly skew the result – it likely slightly overestimates the confidence by treating overlaps as separate. Perhaps the “true” combined probability would be a bit lower if one fully accounted for overlap (say 97% instead of 99.99%). But qualitatively, it would remain very high.* Subjectivity and Confirmation Bias: We have tried to fairly represent mainstream views in physics, philosophy, etc., but inevitably our selection of evidences and weightings has an element of subjectivity. A skeptic might object that we picked evidences congenial to theism. In response, we note that we also initially included what many consider the best evidence against God – the problem of evil – and found a principled reason (endorsed by many philosophers) to set its weight to zero. This could look like we “jettisoned” the inconvenient data. We acknowledge this concern. Our justification is that treating evil as neutral is not ad hoc but grounded in serious philosophical work (Hick, Plantinga, etc.). Nonetheless, a different modeler might insist on counting evil as a genuine negative – their model would still favor God, but less strongly (perhaps ~90% instead of ~99%, as our comparative simulation showed) . Likewise, one might critique inclusion of the QM variable as “grasping at straws.” We included it mainly for completeness and found it has negligible impact (removing it entirely would change nothing important). The danger of confirmation bias is real – one might be tempted to include every possible pro-God evidence and explain away all negative evidence. We have aimed to be balanced by limiting ourselves to evidences that enjoy some peer-reviewed support or broad philosophical acceptance (hence we did not include, say, young-earth creationist claims or numerological Bible codes or other fringe items; we focused on well-known arguments). We also demonstrated what happens if a traditionally negative evidence (evil) is included negatively. Theism still came out on top, just slightly less so. These steps give us confidence that the model is not merely an exercise in preaching to the choir but a genuine, if approximate, synthesis of widely discussed evidences.* Interpretation of Probability: Another limitation is the meaning of saying “99% probability that God exists.” Probability in this context is not a frequency of repeatable experiments (God’s existence is not a repeatable trial), but a degree of belief given evidence – a Bayesian epistemic probability. Some may object that such numbers are too precise for an existential question. We agree that one shouldn’t take, say, “99.99%” at face value; the point is to convey confidence. Perhaps it’s better to say: the evidence makes God’s existence overwhelmingly more plausible than not, rather than focus on a specific percentage. The percentage is a tool to aggregate evidences, not an end in itself. Furthermore, one might argue that human beliefs don’t actually conform to such precise updating – but the normative claim of Bayesian reasoning is that they should, if one is being fully rational with given evidence. So we view the model as a normative guide: if you trust these evidences and weight them roughly as we did, high confidence in God is justified. If one finds those evidences unconvincing or weights them differently, the conclusion would differ – but then the disagreement is about the evidence, not the method.* Does this “prove” the existence of God? No – proof is a strong word usually reserved for logical/mathematical certainty. What we have is a strong probabilistic argument. It falls short of proof in that it is always possible to reject one of the premises or provide an alternative explanation for each fact. For instance, one could say “maybe there is a multiverse to explain fine-tuning, maybe morality is an evolved illusion, maybe consciousness is just brain chemistry, etc.” Our model took those “maybe naturalism can explain this” into account by allowing low-end ? values (even zero) for each piece. The result was that unless naturalism explains all of them away simultaneously, theism wins out. Thus, in a sense, the model strengthens the challenge to atheism: it’s not enough to refute one argument for God, one must address all eight (or provide a comparably strong counter-case). But it does not provide a 100% deductive demonstration. It rather shows that given our world’s features, the God hypothesis is a far more coherent and predictive explanation than atheistic alternatives, to a very high degree of confidence.* What kind of “God” is indicated? Importantly, the model as constructed points to a very general theism – essentially an intelligent, powerful, purposeful Creator of the universe. We have not specified anything about this God’s moral character, relationship to humanity, immanence or transcendence, singularity or plurality, etc. The evidences used are drawn from natural theology (science, philosophy) rather than any specific revealed theology. Thus, a deist who believes in a cosmic architect but not necessarily a personal God could find these probabilities persuasive too (except perhaps the moral and religious experience arguments, which imply something about God’s nature). In the final section of this chapter, we foreshadow Part III of the book, where we will ask: assuming this very high probability that God (in some form) exists, what further inferences can we make about the nature of God or ultimate reality? The evidences might already hint at some qualities: e.g., the fine-tuner and designer seems extremely intelligent and powerful; the moral dimension hints that God is morally good or is the Good itself; religious experiences hint at a personal being who communicates or is felt; the allowance of evil (per soul-making) hints at a deity who values certain virtues, freedom, or growth. These hints will be explored in Part III. But strictly speaking, our probabilistic model here does not differentiate between, say, the God of classical theism, an advanced alien simulator, or Aristotle’s Prime Mover, etc. Those distinctions require additional arguments beyond the scope of this Part.In conclusion, the synthesis of evidence in our probabilistic model strongly supports the rationality of belief in God’s existence. One might quibble with a coefficient here or there, but the overarching picture is that multiple independent lines of evidence – from the cosmic to the personal – converge on the hypothesis of a transcendent, purposeful Mind behind the universe. Even allowing for uncertainties, the cumulative force is robust. We have thereby achieved the goal set at the start of Part II: demonstrating that belief in God can be grounded in a comprehensive, interdisciplinary evidential case, quantified through a rigorous (if simplified) statistical model.Transition to Part IIIHaving established that it is rational – indeed overwhelmingly so – to believe that God exists given the evidence, we now turn to the question of what kind of God this evidence indicates and how this aligns with various conceptions of the divine. Part III will delve into the theological implications: given the existence of God, what can we infer about God’s attributes or intentions from these evidences? For example, fine-tuning and biological complexity suggest God as a purposeful Creator of life; moral objectivity suggests God is good or is the source of moral law; consciousness and mind-like aspects of reality suggest God is Mind or spirit; the fact of evil (even if not counting against God) raises questions about God’s goodness or limitations and will lead us into discussions of theodicy in a theological context. We will also consider how the probabilistic model might be expanded or modified when incorporating specific revelations or religious claims – does identifying God with, say, the God of Christianity, add explanatory power or raise new questions? In essence, having argued that God (in general) exists with high probability, we proceed to ask: “Who is God? What is God like?” – exploring how the evidence might narrow down the identity of this ultimate reality that we find ourselves compelled to acknowledge.References (Harvard Style) – Chapter 9* Barrow, J.D.
