Heraclitus’s surviving fragments describe the Logos as a unifying logic or message inherent in the world, intelligible yet often overlooked by people. The Stoic philosophers later adopted this idea, developing Logos into the immanent rational law pervading the cosmos. For the Stoics, the Logos was the divine fire or reason structuring nature – an omnipresent principle of order identified with God . This Stoic Logos was both the Grund (ground) of being and the natural law organizing all matter and life.As Greek philosophy intertwined with Judeo-Christian thought, the concept of Logos took on new dimensions. Philo of Alexandria (1st c. BCE), a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher, fused Stoic and Platonic ideas with Hebrew scripture . Philo presented the Logos as an intermediary between the transcendent God and the material world – at once God’s creative blueprint and the “messenger” through which divine power orders creation. He called the Logos a “second God” in a derivative sense, the instrument by which the unknowable ultimate God interacts with the finite realm . This Philonic Logos was the rational template in the mind of God, akin to Plato’s forms, now actively structuring the cosmos.The notion of Logos reached a theological apex in the Christian tradition. The Gospel of John opens with a direct identification: “In the beginning was the Word (Logos), and the Word was with God, and the Word was God… All things were made through Him” (John 1:1-3). Here the Logos is not an abstract principle but a person – Christ – through whom creation came into being. Early Christian thinkers eagerly connected this revelation to Greek philosophy. For example, Justin Martyr saw hints of the Christian Logos in Heraclitus and the Stoics, calling these philosophers “Christians before Christ” insofar as they grasped the spermatic Logos (logos spermatikos), the seed of truth sown in all humanity . In Christian theology, Logos came to signify God’s eternal Reason or Wisdom, incarnate as Jesus. Augustine of Hippo linked the Logos to the divine ideas: the archetypal forms or rationes by which God fashioned the world. Augustine taught that the Word contains the eternal reasons (rationes aeternae) for every creature, reflecting the rationality of the Creator in creation . Later, Maximus the Confessor (7th c.) developed a rich Logos theology, affirming that the one divine Logos has planted “logoi” (rational principles) in all created things. Each creature, in Maximus’s view, embodies a unique expression of the Logos, and the cosmic role of Christ is to unify and reconcile all these logoi back to the divine Source. Thus, from antiquity through late antiquity, Logos consistently denoted the rational, intelligible principle behind the cosmos – whether conceived impersonally (as in the Stoic fire of reason) or personally (as in the Word made flesh).Logos as the Rational Structure of RealityAcross these developments, a core idea emerged: Logos as the structuring rationality of the cosmos. Metaphysically, the Logos is the principle that gives the universe its coherent form and lawful order. Epistemologically, the Logos is what makes the universe intelligible to rational minds. Heraclitus hinted that because all follows the Logos, humans share in that common reason – yet we often fail to recognize it . The Stoics explicitly taught that human reason is a spark off the divine Logos, implying that our minds can grasp the world precisely because both world and mind partake in the same rational order. This unity of cosmic and cognitive order would later be echoed by the Scholastics: Thomas Aquinas, for instance, held that the eternal Logos (divine Wisdom) is imprinted on creation (making it orderly) and on our minds (giving us intellect), explaining why truth is accessible to us.In Christian thought, the Logos as Christ is both Creator and Illuminator – “the true light which enlightens every man” (John 1:9). The rational structure of reality was thus personified: “through Him all things were made” (John 1:3) indicates that every law of nature, every pattern in creation, is an expression of the divine Logos . At the same time, “in Thy light shall we see light” (Psalm 36:9) and similar verses were taken to mean that our very capacity for knowledge is a participation in God’s Logos. Augustine argued that when we grasp eternal truths (like mathematics or principles of justice), we are in effect “reading” the divine Mind – a process of illumination by the Logos within us. Maximus the Confessor described all the distinct logoi (rational principles or “inner words”) of created things as anchored in the one Logos, so that the world is like a harmonized discourse spoken by God. In sum, Logos came to signify rational structure writ large: it is that by which the cosmos exists in an ordered, law-like way, and that by which conscious beings can comprehend that order . The cosmos was understood as rational and linguistic at heart – a kind of cosmic discourse or “book of nature” authored by divine Reason.It is notable that even outside explicitly religious contexts, philosophers leaned on Logos to explain reality’s coherence. The Stoics’ Logos was essentially a metaphysical principle of cosmic coherence, ensuring that the world is a kosmos (ordered whole) rather than chaos . Likewise, their epistemology trusted that by living according to Logos (kata logon), humans align with nature’s rational order. This twofold aspect – Logos as ontological blueprint and as epistemic light – set the stage for a unified worldview: reality is fundamentally intelligible because it is grounded in intelligence. The Divine Logos concept thus bridges being and knowing, creation and cognition.From Classical Logos to Information: A Modern BridgeFast-forward to the modern age: the metaphor of the universe as rational order has been recast in the language of information. The advent of information theory, computing, and modern physics has led thinkers to compare Logos to the informational content and structure of the cosmos . The ancient intuition was that the world is permeated by meaning or intelligibility (the “Word”). Today, scientists and philosophers observe that the world is permeated by information – from the genetic code in DNA to the mathematical regularities of physical law. This is more than a superficial analogy. In many respects, information plays the role in contemporary thought that Logos did in ancient philosophy: it is seen as the substrate of reality, the generative principle behind physical phenomena, and the key to understanding why the universe is orderly.One of the pioneers of this bridge was the physicist John Archibald Wheeler. He coined the famous aphorism “It from Bit,” capturing the idea that every physical “it” (object, particle, field) ultimately derives from immaterial “bits” of information . In Wheeler’s words, “all things physical are information-theoretic in origin” . This bold proposal – that information is more fundamental than matter and energy – closely echoes the notion of a primal Logos giving rise to the material world. Wheeler suggested that at the deepest level, the universe might be a kind of information processing system, with physical reality emerging from binary choices (yes/no, bit) . Notably, Wheeler also spoke of the universe as a “participatory universe”, implying that observers (by posing yes/no questions to nature) help “bring forth” the physical reality – a modern twist on the idea that mind and world are intertwined through information.Other scientists extended this line of thought. The cosmologist Paul Davies has argued that information may be as fundamental to the cosmos as matter or energy. Building on Wheeler, Davies (2010) posited that “bits” could be considered prior to “its,” meaning that informational relationships underlie physical manifestations . In a 2014 essay, he phrased it succinctly: “Ignorance is the flip side of information,” noting that in physics, entropy is a measure of missing information . Davies even hypothesizes a hierarchy: information ? laws of physics ? matter, suggesting that the structure of physical law itself might originate from deeper informational principles . Such views resonate strongly with the Logos concept: just as the Logos was seen as the rational origin of natural law and matter, information is now being considered the informing principle that shapes physical law and the material world.Philosophers have also contributed to this bridge. Luciano Floridi, a leading philosopher of information, has developed the idea of Informational Realism. He proposes that the universe can be conceptualized as made of informational structures – what he calls “It-ones” (instances of information) rather than particles. Floridi famously defines semantic information as “well-formed, meaningful and truthful data” . While Floridi’s concern is often epistemic and logical (how information relates to meaning and truth), he also explores ontological implications: if we take information seriously, we arrive at an “informational structural realism” where reality is fundamentally composed of information rather than matter. This mirrors the ancient idea of Logos as the real essence (the realest thing is not atoms, but the rational form/word that gives atoms their pattern). Floridi and others have effectively updated the Logos concept into what we might call an Informational Logos: the idea that to be is to be an informational object in a cosmic infosphere.Theologians, too, have been intrigued by this convergence of ancient and modern ideas. John F. Haught, for example, has engaged with information theory in the context of theology. Haught suggests that if nature is undergirded by information, this provides a new avenue for understanding divine action – not as intermittent supernatural interventions, but as the continuous informational presence of God within cosmic processes . He notes that many scientists now see information as fundamental and asks whether theology can interpret this information in terms of the Logos, the divine Word that “sustains all things.” Similarly, the theologian Niels Henrik Gregersen speaks of an “informational universe” that is open to a theological interpretation . Gregersen has even advocated a “Stoicizing Logos Christology”, explicitly drawing on the Stoic idea of Logos as cosmic reason and linking it with Christ as the informational pattern incarnate in the world . Such work suggests that the classical Logos theology and the scientific information paradigm are not at odds but deeply resonant. The Logos that was “in the beginning” (John 1:1) can be meaningfully correlated with the informational “source code” of the universe posited by contemporary science .In summary, there is a growing synthesis: the classical notion of Logos as rational principle is being reframed in terms of information as the fundamental reality. This bridge invites a unified perspective in which God’s Logos is the ultimate information, the divine Mind imparting bits, patterns, and intelligible order to the cosmos . In the next sections, we will explore this informational paradigm in detail and see how it provides fresh insights into age-old questions.The Informational Worldview in Contemporary ThoughtSeveral prominent modern thinkers have championed an informational view of the universe, effectively reviving Logos-like concepts in new terminology. We have already noted John Archibald Wheeler and Paul Davies, who frame physics in terms of information. Let us consider a few key voices and how each contributes to this worldview:* John Archibald Wheeler (“It from Bit”) – Wheeler’s idea that physical entities arise from binary information marks a paradigm shift in physics . He envisioned the universe as a giant information system, where what we call particles or fields are manifestations of yes/no questions being answered. Importantly, Wheeler’s “participatory universe” suggests the role of observers and measurements (information gathering) is central to reality’s existence . His approach blurs the line between physics and epistemology: the structure of the world is shaped by the information that can be obtained about it. Wheeler’s legacy is evident in quantum information science and digital physics, where researchers seriously entertain that information has ontological primacy.* Paul Davies – A physicist and astrobiologist, Davies has explored the implications of information for both cosmology and biology. In The Fifth Miracle (1998) and other works, he discusses how life’s origin might be understood as an information phenomenon (e.g. the emergence of DNA, a digital information molecule). In the volume Information and the Nature of Reality (2010), which he co-edited, Davies argues that information may be a third fundamental (alongside matter and energy) and even speculates about top-down informational causation in the universe . He notes that entropy (disorder) is essentially missing information about a system’s microstate, so when entropy is low (as in the early universe), information content is high . Davies echoes Wheeler in suggesting that the laws of physics themselves might reflect deep informational constraints or logic. His phrase “the universe from bit” captures the notion that bits could be the bedrock from which space, time, and matter emerge (Davies 2010). By highlighting the information-theoretic aspects of phenomena like quantum mechanics, complexity, and even consciousness, Davies provides a scientific narrative that parallels the theological narrative of a rational Logos upholding all things .* Luciano Floridi – Floridi approaches information from a philosophical and ethical angle. In his Philosophy of Information (2011), he delineates different kinds of information (analogous to different meanings of Logos historically). He distinguishes: (1) information as reality – the ontological information that constitutes the fabric of the world, (2) mathematical/physical information – as in Shannon’s bits, signals, and entropy, and (3) semantic information – meaningful data as understood by minds. Floridi’s definition of semantic information as “well-formed, meaningful and truthful data” has become influential. This definition insists that meaning and truth are crucial – a reminder that information isn’t just a raw byte count but can embody Logos-like meaning. Floridi’s work essentially updates metaphysics: instead of substances and essences, he speaks of information objects and their attributes. He even entertains the idea of God as an infinite informational entity, though he remains more agnostic/philosophical on theology. For our purposes, Floridi provides a rigorous framework for talking about different levels of information, which can enrich the Logos discourse. Where ancient thinkers spoke of divine Ideas, forms, or wisdom, we can now speak of information in an analogous (though not identical) way, with Floridi’s categories helping to avoid confusion.* John F. Haught – A theologian known for work on science and religion, Haught has engaged the concept of information particularly in relation to evolution and cosmology. In his essay “Information, Theology, and the Universe” , Haught suggests that if the universe is informatically structured, theology can view information as a creative Word. He proposes that God’s providence might operate through the dramatic unfolding of information over cosmic history – e.g. the information encoded in the laws of physics enabling complexity, the information in DNA guiding life’s development, etc. Haught also emphasizes the narrative aspect of information: just as a story conveys meaning, the universe as a whole can be seen as a “story” full of information that points to a divine Author. He thus connects the quantitative idea of information to the qualitative idea of meaning and purpose, aligning with the view that the Logos is not just structure but also significance. In Haught’s vision, the informational character of the universe is an expected feature if a rational God created it – modern science is essentially reading the “Book of Nature” in a new script (binary code and algorithms rather than geometric proportions), but it remains a book authored by the Logos .* Niels Henrik Gregersen – Gregersen has pioneered the concept of “Deep Incarnation”, which posits that the embodiment of God in Christ (the Logos incarnate) extends to the whole material order, down to its basic constituents. In recent work, he explores the idea of an informational cosmos in dialogue with theology . Gregersen notes that one can have an “informational universe” without any religious commitments, but intriguingly this concept is consonant with theological notions of Logos . He points out that early Christian Logos doctrine already implied a connection between divine Reason and the form and information of the world . In an essay tellingly titled “God, Matter, and Information: Towards a Stoicizing Logos Christology,” Gregersen argues that we can retrieve the Stoic insight of Logos as seminal reason (logos spermatikos) shaping matter, and integrate it with the Chalcedonian Christ (the Logos incarnate) . This yields a theology in which matter is not just dead stuff but is suffused with information-bearing divinity. Put differently, the informational properties of the universe (its lawfulness, complexity, self-organizing capability) can be seen as reflections of the indwelling Logos. Gregersen’s perspective helps theologians embrace the findings of science (like evolution and quantum theory) by viewing information as the medium of divine action. Rather than seeing God as occasionally suspending laws, this view sees God as the author of the informational matrix of law and chance within which the universe unfolds.Each of these thinkers – Wheeler, Davies, Floridi, Haught, Gregersen, and others (we might also mention Seth Lloyd, Jürgen Schmidhuber, Keith Ward, etc.) – converges on a vision of the universe as fundamentally informational. Whether coming from physics, philosophy, or theology, they all challenge the older materialist notion that mass-energy alone is primary. Instead, information (often tied to mind or reason) is given ontological weight. This represents a kind of modern Logos doctrine: the world’s essence lies not in mindless matter but in the patterns, codes, and meanings that matter embodies .One might say that the ancient intuition of a cosmic Logos has re-emerged in our time as the idea that “the universe is built on information.” As a 2021 physics study remarked, “physical reality can be expressed in terms of data” . The stage is now set to clarify what we mean by “information,” and how exactly this parallels the Logos.Defining Information: From Shannon to OntologyThe word “information” can carry many meanings, so it is crucial to define it clearly in our context. In this chapter, information will be used in several distinct but related senses, corresponding to technical and philosophical concepts:* Shannon Information (Quantitative Information): In 1948 Claude Shannon founded information theory by defining information as the reduction of uncertainty in a message. Shannon’s key insight was that information can be quantified in bits – a bit representing a choice between two equally likely alternatives (yes/no, 0/1). The Shannon entropy $H$ measures the amount of uncertainty (or information content) in a probability distribution of messages. If an event is very unlikely, the news of its occurrence carries a lot of information (many bits); if it’s very likely, learning it adds little information. An often-cited summary is: “information is the reduction of uncertainty” . Mathematically, $H = -\sum p_i \log_2 p_i$, which is high when outcomes are unpredictable (thus information gain on finding the result is high). Shannon information is syntactic; it cares not about the meaning of a message, only the statistical structure. It treats information as an objective commodity that can be transmitted over a channel. Importantly, Shannon showed that information is related to entropy in thermodynamics – in fact, one can say information = negative entropy . When entropy increases, information about the precise state is lost; conversely, creating order (low entropy) can be seen as injecting information. This led physicist Leon Brillouin to coin the term “negentropy” for information. Shannon information is the backbone of digital technology and communications, but it’s just one layer of what we mean by information in a broader, cosmic sense.* Semantic Information: This refers to meaningful information, the kind that carries semantic content or knowledge. While a random string of bits can have a high Shannon entropy, it might convey no meaning. Semantic information involves symbols that refer to something and often implies correctness or truthfulness. Philosopher Luciano Floridi defines semantic information in a “strong” sense as “well-formed, meaningful and truthful data” . For example, a sentence reporting a true fact would count as semantic information, whereas a jumble of letters or a false statement would not (on Floridi’s definition). Semantic information is crucial in fields like biology (e.g. DNA sequences encode functional instructions), cognitive science, and everyday communication. It connects to the concept of Logos because Logos historically implied rational discourse or meaning (the “expressive meaning” of Logos was speech conveying thought ). In an informational universe, we must ask: where does meaning reside? Some argue that meaning emerges only with minds (subjective interpreters), while others like biosemioticians suggest that even life processes involve semantic-like information (signals that about something and have significance for an organism). We will see that a theology of Logos-as-information likely entails that meaning is woven into the cosmos (if the Logos is the source of order, then the universe has semantic depth, not just random bits).* Algorithmic Information (Kolmogorov Complexity): This is a more algorithmic notion of information content, developed by Andrey Kolmogorov, Gregory Chaitin, and others. The Kolmogorov complexity of a string is basically the length of the shortest computer program that can produce that string . In other words, it measures how compressible the information is. A highly patterned string like “AAAAAAAAAA” has low algorithmic complexity (it can be described briefly: “10 A’s”), whereas a truly random string of the same length has high complexity (no shorter description exists than writing it out fully). Algorithmic information theory ties in with ideas of randomness, complexity, and even the definition of life (e.g., life’s DNA may be seen as a highly specific program). It provides a bridge between information and computation. In a sense, Shannon’s theory measures how many bits are in a message, while Kolmogorov complexity asks what those bits really capture (pattern or randomness). If we apply this to the universe, one might ask: Is the cosmos algorithmically simple or complex? Some have tried to estimate the algorithmic complexity of physical laws or the universe’s state. Theologically, one could speculate that God, as ultimate Logos, compresses an infinite amount of complexity into elegant laws (a short “program” like the equations of physics that generates immense variety). This recalls the old idea of logoi spermatikoi (seminal reasons) – compact principles that unfold into the rich tapestry of the world.* Ontological Information: This is information considered as a fundamental constituent of reality, not just a description of it. When Wheeler says “it from bit,” he is talking about ontological information – bits that aren’t merely our knowledge of the system, but are the building blocks of the system itself . Ontological information means that properties of physical things are essentially information. For instance, the spin of an electron (up or down) can be viewed as a bit of information; the configuration of quantum fields in the vacuum can be viewed as information encoded in the universe. Some theorists go so far as to suggest that matter is a form of congealed information. In digital physics, propositions like “the universe is a computer” or “reality is a simulation” are ways of saying that physical processes are in essence information processes . If this is true, then one might consider information as a substance-like or field-like quantity in its own right (just as energy is a substance-like quantity in physics). Physicist Melvin Vopson recently attempted to calculate how many bits of information are in the observable universe (≈ $6\times10^{80}$ bits) , treating each particle’s state as carrying a few bits. Ontological information is essentially the Logos in a scientific guise: it says that what is really real about an electron is not a tiny billiard ball, but the information that defines its existence. If one were to take a leap: God as Logos could be thought of as the source of all ontological information – the reason why any bit exists at all, and why the cosmic computer isn’t running random noise but a meaningful program.When discussing the Divine Logos as the source of information, we potentially mean all the above: God as the source of the rational bits that form physical reality (Shannon/ontological information), the source of meaningful order and signals in creation (semantic information), and the author of the cosmic “algorithms” (imposing compressible lawful structure on chaos). The distinctions help avoid equivocation. For example, saying “God is information” could mislead if one only thinks of information = data. More precisely, we would say: God is the source and ground of the informational structure of reality, analogous to saying God is the source of the Logos that upholds creation . In the next section, we will see how these information concepts play out in physics and cosmology – which will further solidify why information is a powerful unifying concept.Information in Physics and CosmologyModern physics provides striking examples of the centrality of information, often aligning with the idea of an ordered Logos. Let us survey a few key areas:1. Entropy and Missing Information: In thermodynamics, entropy $S$ measures the disorder of a system, but since Ludwig Boltzmann it has been understood in terms of probability and information. Boltzmann’s formula $S = k_B \ln W$ relates entropy to the number of microstates $W$ consistent with a given macrostate. In information terms, a higher entropy means less information about the exact state (more uncertainty which microstate the system is in). Shannon’s entropy formula was deliberately analogous to Boltzmann’s. Thus, one can say entropy is missing information about the system’s detailed configuration . When a system becomes more ordered (entropy decreases), information about its state increases. This insight has even penetrated black hole physics – Black Hole Entropy (from Bekenstein and Hawking) is proportional to the horizon area, suggesting black holes have enormous entropy (disorder) and thus could hide vast information within. In everyday terms, when you clean your room (decrease entropy), you are effectively imparting information (imposing a specific arrangement out of many possible jumbled ones). Some physicists have mused that the low entropy at the Big Bang – the extremely ordered initial state of the universe – corresponds to a huge infusion of information at the moment of creation, akin to a finely tuned setup . This resonates with the theological idea of Logos imparting order at creation. As Paul Davies put it, “ignorance is the flip side of information” – and the early universe had very little “ignorance” (i.e., was in a very specific, information-rich state), a fact that science still struggles to explain naturalistically. In a Logos framework, one might say the initial low entropy (and associated fine-tuning) was a result of the input of informational organization by the Creator.2. The Black Hole Information Paradox: Perhaps the most famous information conundrum in physics is Hawking’s black hole paradox. In the 1970s, Stephen Hawking discovered that black holes radiate (Hawking radiation) and can eventually evaporate completely. His calculations implied that this radiation is thermal (random) and carries no detailed imprint of whatever fell into the black hole . If a black hole fully evaporates, it seemed the information about everything it swallowed is irretrievably lost from the universe. However, this violates a core principle of quantum theory: unitarity, which says information is never destroyed, only transformed. This conflict between relativity (which allowed info loss in a black hole) and quantum mechanics (which forbids it) is the information paradox . Decades of debate ensued, engaging the brightest minds in physics. Most physicists now suspect that the information is not actually lost – somehow, the quantum gravity effects must encode the information either in the Hawking radiation in subtle correlations or perhaps in a remnant. Recent developments (holographic principle, AdS/CFT correspondence, quantum entanglement entropy calculations) suggest that information does escape, encoded via quantum entanglement, resolving the paradox in favor of information conservation . The key takeaway is how sacrosanct information has become in fundamental physics: physicists are willing to revise their understanding of spacetime (introducing holographic dualities where the world is like a projection of information on a boundary) to preserve the idea that Logos-like information cannot be destroyed. This is strongly reminiscent of theological language: the Logos “lights every man” and “the light shines in the darkness and is not overcome” – metaphorically, meaning truth/information ultimately prevails over chaos/ignorance. The black hole paradox tells us that even in the most extreme scenario (a star collapsing into a hole), nature perhaps does not allow meaningful information about the world to vanish without trace. Some authors have even likened black holes to “cosmic conscious” entities that process information in mysterious ways, which is speculative but underscores how information-centric our view of physics has become.3. Quantum Information and Entanglement: Quantum physics, which at first seems bizarre, can be understood deeply as a theory about information. Quantum information theory is a flourishing field that reinterprets quantum states as information resources. A qubit (quantum bit) can exist in superposition of 0 and 1, and multiple qubits can exist in entangled states where their information is holistically shared. Entanglement means two particles are described by one joint state – you cannot fully describe one without the other . This is information nonlocality: measuring one immediately affects what can be known about the other, no matter the distance, as if they share an invisible informational link. Entanglement has been called the “fabric” that holds space together in some theoretical speculations (e.g., ER = EPR, the idea that spacetime connectivity (ER for Einstein-Rosen bridges) is equivalent to entanglement (EPR paradox) correlations). For practical purposes, entanglement is the engine behind quantum computing, which uses the interference of probability amplitudes to perform computations that would be infeasible classically. The fact that quantum computation works suggests that at a fundamental level, the universe stores and manipulates information in quantum states in a way analogous to a computer . Seth Lloyd famously argued “the universe is a quantum computer” that has been computing its own next state from the beginning of time . He estimated the total number of operations the universe has performed and the amount of information processed since the Big Bang. Lloyd’s point is not merely metaphor; it’s an attempt to quantify the informational capacity of physical processes. One of his insights, quoted by Gregersen, is: “Energy makes physical systems do things. Information tells them what to do”, thus “the primary actor in the physical history of the universe is information” . This is a direct modern echo of Logos: energy corresponds to existence (actus essendi) and information corresponds to essence or form (giving shape to that existence). In classical terms, Logos “tells” the universe what to do – like an invisible instruction set.4. Information in Quantum Cosmology: At the cosmological scale, theories like inflation and the multiverse raise questions of initial information. If our universe is one of many, each might be seen as a different output of some underlying “code” (the laws of physics could be thought of as an algorithm with random inputs). The extreme improbability of a low-entropy, life-permitting cosmos has prompted some to consider whether information was input deliberately. The fine-tuning of constants can be framed as certain informational bits (the constants’ values) being set to precise figures that encode a habitable universe . This is akin to how a very large software program can have a few crucial configuration parameters: if those bits are off, the program crashes; if correctly set, the program runs and produces a complex output. Cosmologist Paul Davies has noted that the laws of nature themselves are like “software” that guides matter-energy (the “hardware”) . If one asks, where did this software come from?, one is essentially asking a Logos question in information terms. Some have posited a “cosmic DNA” metaphor – a set of primordial information that the universe expands and develops from, analogous to a seed or an egg containing the instructions for a full organism.5. Emergent Complexity and Information: Another aspect of information in nature is how it grows and transforms. Over 13.8 billion years, the universe has seen an increase in organized complexity (galaxies, stars, planets, life, mind), even as the second law of thermodynamics demands overall entropy increase. The resolution is that entropy can increase while locally information-rich structures form (e.g., life on Earth thrives by exporting entropy to space). Complexity scientists often use information metrics to quantify organization. For instance, algorithmic complexity can measure the information in biological genomes or in neural networks. The emergence of life is essentially the emergence of information-processing systems (cells process genetic and environmental information). Notably, the DNA in a single cell contains about $10^9$ bits – comparable to a computer’s disk – and it functions as a code. This has led people like Hubert Yockey to claim “the genome is an information system and should be treated as such.” Moreover, evolutionary processes can be seen as information algorithms (random mutation generates new strings, natural selection “chooses” those that encode favorable outcomes – it’s like a search algorithm in the space of genetic programs). All this suggests that information begets further information: once informational structures exist, they can create higher-level informational phenomena (like brains creating language and culture). This layered information view aligns beautifully with a Logos theology: the Logos not only sets up initial order, but continually “sustains all things by his word of power” – we might say sustains the flow of information and allows new informational layers to emerge (physical ? chemical ? biological ? mental ? spiritual). Each layer doesn’t override the previous but builds upon it, analogous to how higher-level code calls lower-level routines in a complex software stack.In summary, information has become a unifying theme in physics. From thermodynamics to quantum mechanics to cosmology, the language of bits, entropy, and algorithms is now fundamental. This is not an artificial imposition by philosophers; it stems from practical scientific needs (e.g., understanding black holes or building quantum computers). We see a universe where information is conserved, processed, transformed, and possibly originated. To a person steeped in theology and philosophy, these developments ring a bell – the bell of the Logos. The next step is to make the connection explicit: how might a computational/informational metaphysics be integrated with the idea of God as the Logos?Toward an Informational Metaphysics of CreationIf we take seriously the idea that the universe is fundamentally informational, it opens up a new way to conceive the relationship between God and the world. Traditional metaphysics spoke of God as Being itself (ipsum esse), or as the Designer, or as First Cause. Here we explore God as the source of information, the divine Informer or cosmic programmer . This doesn’t negate the classical ideas, but reframes them in contemporary terms that resonate with scientific understanding.Let us outline how a computational/informational metaphysics might look, and how it connects to theology:* The Universe as an Information-Processing System: This concept, popular in digital physics, imagines the universe as akin to a giant computer. Every physical interaction is like a computation updating the state of memory (the state of fields/particles). Instead of thinking of matter as inert substance moved by forces, we think of bits transformed by algorithms. This metaphor can be made quite rigorous in some models (e.g., cellular automaton models of the universe, or the holographic principle where the boundary of the universe encodes information like a computer memory). Now, if the universe is a computer, the question arises: Who or what programmed it? In one narrative, the answer is “no one, it’s a self-existing computational rule.” But another narrative, consistent with theism, is that God is the programmer who designed the hardware and software of reality. The Logos in John 1 is called the divine Word through whom “all things were made” – we might analogously say all bits were set in place through this Word. Genesis famously depicts God creating by speaking (“Let there be light…” etc.) – speech is an act of encoding information (commands) that bring about effects. Thus, creation can be viewed as informational execution: God’s Logos issues the code and the universe runs according to it. This is not a mechanistic deism, because in classical doctrine the Logos is continuously involved (in Him all things hold together). In computer terms, God is not just the programmer who wrote the code and left; God is also the sustaining operating system or the constant power supply that keeps the information flowing and the computation going at every moment .* God as the Source of Information: To phrase it starkly, if bits underlie its, then what underlies bits? A theistic answer: Mind underlies bits – specifically the mind of God. Every bit of information is a distinction, a difference that makes a difference. It takes a mind to choose one possibility out of others (analogous to measurement). One could posit that the entire set of information that constitutes the universe is ideas in the mind of God, somewhat akin to Berkeley’s idealism but cast in information terms. Rather than saying the material world exists in God’s mind (which can be misconstrued), we say the informational content of the world exists because God imparts information – God is the ultimate informant. Just as the Nicene Creed says creation is made through the Logos (Word), we say creation is made through information that originates in God . The metaphor of God as an author is apt: an author generates information (the text of a story) which then takes on a life of its own in the world of the novel. Similarly, God’s act of creation can be seen as an act of writing the “information that is nature.” This positions God not as a coercive force but as a communicator. Indeed, one could say the universe is not only created, but communicated . Every law of physics is like a message conveying rational structure; every new emergent phenomenon is like God “saying” something further as the dynamic unfolds. This approach dovetails with Eastern Orthodox notions of divine logoi present in creation, and also with process theology’s idea of God luring creation toward forms (except here the lure is information).* Matter and Mind as Emergent Informational Structures: In informational metaphysics, matter and energy are seen as states of information. Thus, what we call the material world is a level of description of underlying information. Mind (consciousness), on the other hand, could be seen as an advanced mode of information processing – the universe aware of itself. If God’s Logos is the ultimate information, then finite minds might be participations in the Logos (which is a classical idea: we are in the image of Logos, endowed with rationality). In our context, human minds are themselves information processors (the brain is sometimes modeled as an information system). But humans also generate semantic and meaningful information (art, science, ethics) that far exceeds mere survival data. One theological interpretation is that this creative information-generating capacity in us reflects the divine Logos working through us. Maximus the Confessor would say our logoi (our reason, ideas, creative words) should align with the Logos. In an informational universe, consciousness is not a fluke; it is the cosmos achieving new modes of information integration (as some neuroscientists and philosophers, e.g. Integrated Information Theory, suggest). A bold conjecture is that consciousness adds a self-reflective semantic layer to reality – the universe not only has information, but now understands some of it through conscious agents. Theologically, one could say the Logos “became flesh” to fully unite the divine source of information with the emergent informational being of humans, sanctifying and elevating it. That is Gregersen’s deep incarnation: the Logos enters into the very informational matrix of creation (DNA, cells, embodied life) and infuses it with a healing, unifying purpose.* Top-Down Causation and Information: One challenge in integrating this view is how God’s influence interacts with natural processes. If the universe is a closed information-processing system, where does God fit in without “breaking” the rules? One proposal is top-down causal influence via information. Think of a computer simulation: the programmer can intervene not by brute force, but by introducing new information (tweaking a parameter, injecting a new subroutine). These are subtle interventions that change outcomes by changing information, not by overturning physical laws. Some physicists and philosophers (George Ellis, Paul Davies, etc.) have argued that higher-level informational patterns can have causal power (“downward causation”). In theology, miracles or providence could be reconceived as special inputs of information that alter the course of events without violating natural laws’ formal coherence – analogous to how inserting a new line of code can change a program’s output while the CPU still faithfully executes the code. This remains speculative, but it’s a way to visualize divine action in an informational ontology. At the very least, we can say God continuously holds the information content of the universe in existence. Were God to stop “broadcasting” the Logos, the information that structures reality would dissipate, and with it the universe (just as, in classical doctrine, if God withdrew sustaining power, creation would lapse into nothingness).Summing up, an informational metaphysics sees reality as akin to a divine communication or computation. It preserves a role for God as the ultimate source and interpreter of information. It also harmonizes with scientific insight by suggesting that what science calls “information” is essentially what theology has called Logos, Wisdom, Ratio. The difference is largely in language and emphasis. Theology can thereby claim that it has anticipated this perspective: as the Book of Wisdom  (Wisdom 11:20) says, God has “ordered all things by measure, number, and weight” – an ancient way of saying God built an information-ordered world.The Universe in Mathematical Language: God as AuthorEver since Galileo Galilei, scientists have spoken of the universe as written in the language of mathematics. Galileo wrote that the “book of nature is written in the language of mathematics” – filled with geometric figures and symbols . This metaphor captures the remarkable fact that nature obeys precise mathematical laws, which are a kind of information code. Equations are information; they encode relationships. The law $F=ma$ (force equals mass times acceleration) is a simple bit of information that surprisingly holds universally for classical mechanics. When Einstein discovered $E=mc^2$, it was as if he glimpsed a line in nature’s source code linking mass and energy. The unreasonable effectiveness of mathematics in the natural sciences (to use Eugene Wigner’s phrase) points to an underlying Logos-like rationality. If the universe is written in mathematical code, it begs the question: Who wrote that code? Mathematics itself is an abstract realm of ideas (platonists would say it has eternal truth independent of the physical world). The instantiation of those mathematical structures in the fabric of reality implies a mediating agency. In Christian thought, that agency is the Logos: “through Him all things were made” can be understood as meaning the Logos impressed the rational structure (mathematics, logic, form) onto creation .Thus, if we picture the cosmos as a vast book or a simulation running on a cosmic computer, then God is the author or coder behind it. The information content of the universe – from quantum fields to galaxy clusters – corresponds to divine thoughts or logoi. This is not a new analogy; Christian theologians like Johannes Kepler often described themselves as “thinking God’s thoughts after Him” when discovering scientific laws. What is new is the rigor with which we can flesh out this analogy using information theory and computing concepts.For example, consider the fine-tuning of constants: these are like key variables in the code set to specific values. If one sees those as chosen by an intelligent agent (rather than random), then the inference is that a Programmer set them intentionally to produce a desired outcome (complex life, etc.) . Or consider the presence of objective moral truths or aesthetic values: these could be seen as part of the semantic content that the Author wrote into the story of the universe (beyond the bare physics). In theology, the divine Logos is not only responsible for physical order but also moral and rational order – logos in the sense of divine reason pervades ethics and logic too. This suggests that mathematics is just one “language” God speaks in creation; others might be the language of morality (the natural law in the heart, as Aquinas would say) or the language of beauty. All these are species of information.An illuminating example is the DNA in living cells, often called the language of life. It literally has an alphabet (A, C, G, T nucleotides) and conveys instructions for building proteins. Biologists like Francis Collins have marveled that “DNA is the language of God” (to quote his book title), meaning that the informational properties of life might reflect the work of a mind. If the whole universe is similarly “written” in a language (whether mathematics or genetic code or quantum bits), then by analogy there is an Author of Nature. Just as a literary text has an author’s intent behind it, the cosmic text would have divine intent. This doesn’t imply a naive literal reading of natural events as sentences, but it underscores that the meaning in the universe originates beyond the universe. We as readers (scientists, philosophers) decode bits of this meaning through experiments and equations, but the full meaning (the “why” behind the “how”) comes into view only when we acknowledge the Logos.Galileo’s proclamation that nature is a mathematical book was a harbinger of the information age perspective. Mathematics is highly compressed information (formulae that generate endless true results). So to say the world is mathematical is to say it’s algorithmically compressible – it follows intelligible patterns that we can codify. To a theist, this ability to codify nature is not a happy accident; it reflects the design of a rational God. Albert Einstein famously said “I’m not interested in this phenomenon or that phenomenon, I want to know God’s thoughts – the rest are details.” In context, he meant the fundamental laws (God’s thoughts) versus particular outcomes (details). The laws of physics can be thought of as a subset of God’s thoughts about how the universe should run – in other words, divine information shared with creation.In summary, the idea that “the universe is written in mathematics” reinforces our Logos-as-information theme: God is the ultimate mathematician and coder, and the universe is His code unfolding. Every scientific discovery, then, is like deciphering another fragment of the cosmic code, a code that originates in the Logos . This gives a profound significance to our quest for knowledge: it’s a way of communing with the divine intellect, reading the traces of the Logos in the matrix of reality.A Unified Framework: Logos as Information Tying Together the EvidenceBy reconceiving the Logos in terms of information, we gain a powerful integrative framework that can unify various strands of evidence for purpose and design in the universe. Earlier in this book, we encountered phenomena such as the fine-tuning of physical constants, the complexity of life, the emergence of consciousness, and perhaps the existence of objective moral law or mystical experiences. These were considered as clues or evidences pointing toward a transcendent source (God). Now, in the Logos-as-information perspective, we can see all those clues as different manifestations of one underlying reality: the universe is a meaning-bearing system generated by the divine Logos .Consider the fine-tuning of the cosmos. The precise values of constants (like the electromagnetic coupling or the cosmological constant) and initial conditions (like the low entropy at the Big Bang) are necessary for a complex, life-bearing universe. Under our framework, fine-tuning is essentially an informational calibration of the universe’s basic parameters. It’s as if the Logos encoded just the right “settings” so that the cosmic story could unfold in a rich way . The informational view thus interprets fine-tuning not merely as improbable luck, but as intentional information input. The constants are like carefully chosen bits in a master program. This aligns with how a signal in information theory stands out against noise: fine-tuning might be seen as the “signal” of a calibrating mind amid the “noise” of all possible random universes. It transforms the traditional teleological argument into an information-based argument: the cosmos appears to come pre-loaded with specific information that yields structure rather than chaos .Next, consider the complexity of life (biological complexity). Life is distinguished by its high information content – DNA sequences, cellular signaling networks, metabolic pathways are all information-rich and improbably ordered. In earlier chapters, one might have discussed whether this complexity implies design. Now we can say: biological complexity is the unfolding of the initial cosmic information into the realm of life. If Logos-information underlies physics, it would naturally propagate into higher levels, producing things like the DNA code. In fact, one could regard DNA as an extension of the Logos – a local instance where information takes on the role of forming and sustaining life. The epilogue of this book (if hinted at earlier) suggested viewing life’s complexity as evidence of an “informational source” behind it . Here we make that explicit: DNA, proteins, etc., are encoded with functional information that points back to an intelligent origin, much as a software module points back to a programmer. Theologically, the Gospel of John says “All things were made through the Logos”; we can apply that to the origin of life by saying the Logos imparted the basic information that life later harnessed. This might have occurred through finely tuned chemistry and physics that naturally give rise to biomolecular information (still a mystery, but one potentially guided by a deeper order).Another example: consciousness. The hard problem of consciousness (why subjective experience exists) has stubbornly resisted reduction to physics. If we treat consciousness as an informational phenomenon – e.g. integrated information or quantum information in the brain – we still face the leap to subjective awareness. However, Logos theology offers an interesting lens: consciousness could be the participation of finite minds in the infinite Mind (Logos) . In our terms, perhaps consciousness arises when information systems become complex enough to reflect on themselves – a property imbued by the Logos so that creatures can share in understanding. We might say the universe, through conscious beings, gains the ability to know itself, which was latent in the Logos from the start. This participatory knowing was hinted by Wheeler’s participatory universe and even more by theologians who see humans as co-creators or co-authors under God. So the existence of mind and reason in us is no longer anomalous; it is expected if the cosmos is fundamentally informational and grounded in divine Mind. Our rationality is simply a localized expression of the cosmic rationality. This addresses the old Enlightenment question: How is it that our thoughts can mirror the external world? The answer: both are grounded in the Logos/information – epistemology aligns with ontology because the Logos is both the cause of being and the light of knowing .Even moral values or spiritual experiences could be unified here. If the Logos is the source of meaning and value (not just structure), then the information paradigm can extend to moral law as a sort of moral information embedded in the nature of rational agents (us). Earlier chapters might have dealt with the objectivity of morality or the occurrence of mystical experiences. Those can now be seen as signals or resonances of the Logos in human consciousness . For instance, a profound mystical experience could be interpreted as the human mind tuning into the “broadcast” of the divine Logos – a download of transcendent information producing feelings of unity, love, etc. (This remains interpretative, but it’s a fertile analogy.) Moral truths could be understood as principles inherent in the informational structure of reality, which we discover by reason and conscience (the “voice” of the Logos in the soul, as some Church Fathers thought).Thus, this Logos-as-information framework is not a thin, reductionist view – it is a broad metaphysical umbrella under which physical design arguments (fine-tuning), biological design arguments (complex information in life), and even philosophical arguments (rationality, morality, etc.) all converge. They are different data points pointing to the same hypothesis: that an intelligent informational principle pervades and originates the cosmos . In effect, all these evidences become “signals from the source,” to borrow language from the epilogue :* Fine-tuning is the calibration signal – the Logos encoding physical law.* Biological complexity is the organic signal – the Logos expressing itself in the language of life.* Consciousness is the cognitive signal – the Logos reflected in thinking minds (the Word echoed in rational souls).* Moral and aesthetic values are the ethical/aesthetic signals – the Logos imparting a moral order and beauty (the “splendor of the Logos” that our moral intuition and artistic inspiration tap into).* Religious experiences might be direct signals – moments of synchrony with the Logos, when one “tunes in” to the divine frequency.Even evil and chaos can be reinterpreted in this model. They are like noise in an information system . Moral evil results from the misuse of freedom by creatures (agents introducing destructive noise by acting against the divine information, the moral law). Natural evils (suffering, disasters) can be seen as the inevitable glitches or byproducts in a complex informational system (like turbulence in fluid dynamics – not intended as evil per se, but chaotic outcomes within a rule framework). However, the beauty of an informational view is that error can be corrected: information theory has concepts of error-correcting codes. One could theologize that the Logos, in Christ, introduces an error-correcting code into the fallen world – perhaps analogous to how grace and redemption work to bring the system back in alignment.As we prepare for subsequent chapters, which might delve deeper into theological implications or cumulative arguments, we have now a consolidated vision: Reality is informational at root because it originates from a divine Logos . This vision not only synthesizes the evidence but elevates it – it suggests that science and religion are speaking of the same underlying truth in different tongues. The physicist’s “information” and the theologian’s “Logos” point to a singular metaphysical framework in which everything is understood as part of a coherent communication from God. The fine-tuning, the complexity, the consciousness, all are parts of a message, or perhaps chapters in the story that the Logos is telling – a story in which we ourselves are characters who can begin to read and comprehend the plot.In the chapters to follow, we will explore how this unified framework of Logos-as-information can further illuminate classical doctrines (creation, incarnation) and address modern questions (such as the possibility of a simulated universe or the destiny of information at the end of time). For now, we conclude: The Divine Logos is not a relic of ancient philosophy, but a living concept that powerfully resonates with the cutting edge of scientific thought. In an age of information, to say “In the beginning was the Logos” is to say “In the beginning was the Information” – and this information was with God, and was God’s own rational self-communication . Through that primordial Information, all things came to be, and in that Information we find light, life, and meaning.Ancient and Patristic Sources* Augustine (2002). On the Trinity.
