This chapter explores how physics and cosmology – traditionally concerned with matter and energy – can be reinterpreted in terms of information content, processing, and communication. We begin by reviewing Shannon’s information theory and the concept of entropy as “missing information,” then examine Sir Roger Penrose’s argument that the extraordinarily low entropy of the Big Bang reflects a vast injection of information at our universe’s birth. We will see how the very laws of nature themselves can be viewed as encoded information, echoing Galileo’s notion that the universe is written in the language of mathematics and Eugene Wigner’s puzzle over the “unreasonable effectiveness” of math in physics. Building on these ideas, John Archibald Wheeler’s participatory universe – encapsulated in the slogan “it from bit” – proposes that physical reality (“it”) fundamentally arises from yes/no information bits, with observers playing an active role in materializing phenomena. Some interpretations of quantum mechanics even suggest that consciousness is involved in the act of measurement, hinting that mind and information are deeply entwined. This chapter also highlights thinkers who have used information-centric or quasi-theological metaphors – from James Jeans’s depiction of the universe as a “great thought” to John Barrow’s reflections on cosmic intelligibility. We then turn to technical developments in quantum information theory: the advent of qubits, the non-local information in entanglement, the conservation of information under unitary evolution, and feats like quantum teleportation. Throughout, we draw connections to the informational metaphysics developed in the previous chapter, considering the idea that information may be an indestructible substrate of reality – an idea resonant with the concept of a cosmic Logos. In closing, we distinguish an “informational theology” of nature from the popular simulation hypothesis, even as we note structural parallels between them. The goal is an interdisciplinary synthesis: physics, mathematics, and mind united by the common theme of information, supported at each step by scientific insights and scholarly references.11.2 Shannon’s Information Theory and Cosmic EntropyClaude Shannon’s information theory provides the classic definition of information and entropy in quantitative terms. In this framework, the entropy $H$ of a message or system is a measure of uncertainty or missing information – technically, $H = -\sum p_i \log_2 p_i$, which attains higher values when outcomes are more unpredictable. Equivalently, entropy can be understood as the amount of information one lacks about the precise microstate of a system . For example, if we only know the gross state of a coin (that it’s fair but not whether it’s heads or tails now), the entropy quantifies our ignorance (one bit of missing information in that case). This notion, introduced by Shannon in 1948, bridged statistical physics and communication theory by showing that the mathematics of thermodynamic entropy and information uncertainty are formally identical . In other words, entropy = missing information in a rigorous sense . The lower the entropy, the more ordered and specified a system is – and thus the less information is “missing” about its microstate. Conversely, high entropy corresponds to high uncertainty or lack of information about the exact state.From this perspective, one can inversely associate high order (low entropy) with high information content. The early universe provides a dramatic case in point. Sir Roger Penrose has highlighted that the Big Bang began in an extraordinarily low-entropy condition, especially with regard to gravitational degrees of freedom . All indications are that the primordial cosmos was a very orderly state, far from thermodynamic equilibrium. Penrose famously estimated the probability of such a low-entropy configuration arising by chance as about $10^{-10^{123}}$ – essentially zero . To visualize this number, Penrose quipped that a “Creator” aiming for our orderly universe had to hit an unimaginably tiny target in phase space (the space of all possible states): on the order of one part in $10^{10^{123}}$ of the total volume . In Penrose’s words, “In order to produce a universe resembling the one in which we live, the Creator would have to aim for an absurdly tiny volume of the phase space of possible universes – about 1/10^10^123 of the entire volume.” . While phrased colorfully, the point is scientific: the Big Bang’s low entropy implies a vast initial information content. So special was the arrangement of matter and geometry at the beginning that an enormous amount of information would be required to specify it out of all possible random configurations. In entropy terms, very little information was “missing” about the early state – it was highly constrained and far-from-random. Penrose interprets this low entropy as an imprint of information built into the universe from the start . Put differently, rather than beginning in chaos, the cosmos began in a state of exquisite order – suggesting that the initial conditions of the universe carried a huge informational payload (or “fine-tuning”) that has since been progressively diluted as entropy increases.To sum up, Shannon’s entropy gives us a language to say that the early universe’s entropy was extraordinarily low, meaning the universe initially was extraordinarily informationally rich. Entropy increase over cosmic history (through processes like stars burning fuel and irreversible dynamics) corresponds to information becoming dispersed or hidden in unrecoverable forms (e.g. thermal radiation). But at $t=0$ (or close to it), the universe’s state was one of very low entropy (high order), suggesting that whatever mechanism or law set the initial state effectively injected information into the cosmos. This idea – that “in the beginning” the universe was endowed with information – will resonate when we later discuss theological parallels (the notion of a primordial Logos). For now, it’s important to note that this viewpoint arises from mainstream physics and cosmology: far from speculative, Penrose’s calculation of the extreme specialness of the Big Bang is widely accepted in principle . The puzzle of the arrow of time and entropy’s growth is essentially the puzzle of how the universe started in such an ordered (informationally loaded) state. In summary, interpreting entropy as missing information allows us to recast the Big Bang’s low entropy as high initial information. The universe did not emerge as a maximal entropy mess (which would contain minimal usable information); instead, it began in a highly ordered state that has fueled the emergence of structure and complexity – essentially spending down an initial information endowment .11.3 Laws of Nature as Encoded Information – Mathematics as the Cosmic CodeIf the initial state of the universe can be viewed in informational terms, so too can the laws of nature themselves. The idea that the laws of physics are like software encoding the regularities of the cosmos has deep roots. Four centuries ago, Galileo Galilei declared that the universe “is written in the language of mathematics,” envisaging nature as a grand book inscribed with geometric characters rather than words. In the 20th century, physicist Eugene Wigner famously pondered “the unreasonable effectiveness of mathematics in the natural sciences.” Wigner observed with astonishment that abstract mathematical structures devised by the human mind end up precisely describing physical reality . As he concluded: “The miracle of the appropriateness of the language of mathematics for the formulation of the laws of physics is a wonderful gift which we neither understand nor deserve.” . Such statements highlight that there is a deep informational character to physical law – the universe behaves as if it has algorithms or equations built into it, which we uncover through mathematical inquiry.From an informational perspective, we can say the laws of physics constitute meta-information: they are information about how matter and energy interact, replicated uniformly across time and space. The fact that an electron here and an electron at the edge of the galaxy follow identical rules (e.g. $F = qE + v \times B$ for electromagnetic force) implies that the instructions are somehow embedded in reality for how particles should evolve. This universality is reminiscent of code deployed across many machines. In theoretical physics, one can even encode laws in a formal axiomatic or algorithmic way. For example, specifying the Standard Model of particle physics is essentially giving a set of information (a Lagrangian with certain symmetry groups and parameters) that could be in principle “run” to generate particle behavior. The laws are like data compressed into elegant equations, which then unfold into the rich phenomena we observe. In this sense, the laws of nature themselves are a kind of information content of the universe.Physicists and philosophers often marvel at this. The British astronomer James Jeans was struck by how immaterial and information-like the foundations of reality had become in light of modern physics. In 1937 he wrote: “the universe begins to look more like a great thought than like a great machine. Mind no longer appears as an accidental intruder into the realm of matter; we are beginning to suspect that we ought rather to hail it as the creator and governor of the realm of matter.” . Jeans was commenting on discoveries like quantum theory and relativity, which had “dematerialized” our conception of matter into something almost mathematical – a set of relations, symmetries, and information (for instance, particles were now understood as excitations of underlying fields, and those fields obeyed abstract equations). His suggestion that mind (or something mind-like, such as a “Great Architect”) might be the source of the order in the physical world is a quasi-theological interpretation: it implies that what we call the laws of nature could be manifestations of a cosmic mind’s rationality . Similarly, physicist Arthur Eddington and others of the early 20th century often spoke of the world as made of “mind-stuff” once matter was stripped of solidity and replaced by mathematical formalisms. These viewpoints, while philosophically tinged, were directly inspired by the recognition that information and form precede concrete matter in our best scientific descriptions.More recently, the discussion has become less mystical but still information-centric. The unreasonable effectiveness of mathematics that Wigner noted can be reframed: why should the universe follow elegant mathematical laws at all? If one views those laws as encoded information, the question becomes how such information was imprinted in the universe. Some thinkers, including those inclined toward theological interpretations, argue that this points to a rational underpinning of reality – essentially that the cosmos behaves as if it were programmed or designed by an intelligence (since mathematics is the product of mind) . Others take a more secular route and say perhaps we live in a mathematical universe (Max Tegmark has championed this view) where “math is reality” and it’s not surprising that math describes the world because the world is math at its core. Either way, the laws of nature can be seen as information content that is built into the fabric of the cosmos. In this light, discovering a new law of physics is like decrypting a portion of the cosmic source code. When Galileo studied the swinging of pendulums or when Newton formulated gravity, they were effectively reading bits of information from nature’s rulebook.A striking illustration of nature’s informational regularity is the genetic-like code found in fundamental physics. For instance, every electron in the universe is identical in its properties – mass, charge, spin – as if every electron is running the same “program” or originates from the same blueprint. This has prompted metaphors of the universe as a hologram or computation where particles are like data manifesting from underlying informational patterns. Indeed, some approaches in theoretical physics, such as digital physics, posit that at the smallest scales the universe might literally be discrete and computational in nature (the idea of John Wheeler and Konrad Zuse that “the universe computes”). We will explore quantum information shortly, but it’s worth noting here that information metaphors pervade contemporary physics: we speak of information content of black holes, of simulation of physical processes on computers, and of DNA-like error-correcting codes in fundamental particles . Even if one does not leap to any theological conclusion, it is standard in physics to treat the laws as given information. The goal of a “Theory of Everything” could be seen as compressing all the information about fundamental forces and particles into one formula – the ultimate data compression of the cosmos.In summary, the laws of nature exemplify the concept of the universe as an informational cosmos. The elegant equations of physics are the source code that produces the dynamics of galaxies, atoms, and life. The uncanny efficacy of mathematics in encoding these laws is either a happy coincidence or a clue to deeper principles. Wigner considered it almost miraculous ; others like Jeans saw it as evidence that mind/consciousness and information are fundamental. In either case, it strengthens the interpretation that physical reality at its core is informational structure – patterns that can be described, compressed, and communicated – rather than brute “stuff.” This sets the stage for Wheeler’s provocative proposal that we turn the famous phrase “What is matter?” into “What is information?”.11.4 Wheeler’s Participatory Universe: “It from Bit”John Archibald Wheeler, a prominent theoretical physicist, introduced perhaps the most concise slogan for the informational worldview: “It from bit.” By this he meant that every physical it (every object, particle, or field – anything that exists) derives ultimately from bits of information, from binary yes/no questions and answers . Wheeler proposed this idea in the 1980s, synthesizing insights from quantum mechanics, computing, and relativity into a radical hypothesis: the universe is fundamentally information-theoretic. In an influential paper titled “Information, Physics, Quantum: The Search for Links,” Wheeler argued that the most elementary building block of reality is the binary choice, the bit, and that all physical quantities obtain meaning only through answers to posed yes/no questions . In his own words: “It from bit symbolizes the idea that every item of the physical world has at bottom — at a very deep bottom, in most instances — an immaterial source and explanation; that what we call reality arises in the last analysis from the posing of yes-no questions and the registering of equipment-evoked responses; in short, that all things physical are information-theoretic in origin.” .Wheeler’s viewpoint also encompassed the role of the observer in quantum physics. He championed a “participatory universe” in which observers are not passive, but actually participate in bringing about physical reality. One of Wheeler’s oft-cited aphorisms is: “No phenomenon is a real phenomenon until it is an observed phenomenon.” . This captures the peculiar fact of quantum mechanics that unobserved systems exist only in a set of potential states (a wavefunction) and that only upon observation does one definite outcome occur. Wheeler extended this quantum observation principle to a cosmic scale. He imagined a scenario (the famous “delayed-choice” thought experiment) where present observations could even retroactively affect how a photon had behaved in the distant past – reinforcing the idea that information gained (the choice of how to measure) can influence what “actually happened” in reality . While these experiments don’t allow casual paradoxes, they strongly suggest that reality is not a fixed tape running independent of observation; instead, the act of observation inserts information into the history of a phenomenon. In Wheeler’s participatory universe, every act of measurement is a bit-forming act – a question asked of nature that nature answers, thereby producing an “it” (a concrete fact) from the “bit” (the yes/no measurement result).The participatory universe idea has profound implications. It blurs any strict line between information (which we usually think of as an abstract description) and the concrete world of objects. If “it from bit” is true, then the physical world IS information. Matter and energy are secondary; what’s primary are logical yes/no events – essentially, the universe is a vast ongoing computation or information exchange. One way to picture this is to imagine the universe as a giant quantum computer, with each quantum interaction akin to a logic gate flipping bits. In fact, Wheeler was not alone in this line of thinking. At a 1983 conference, he coined the term “Law without law,” envisioning that the regularities of physics might emerge from underlying statistical rules on information bits rather than imposed top-down laws. Around the same time, physicist Richard Feynman mused about nature in computational terms, saying he viewed a point in space-time “like a computer with an output and an input”, where the point has a kind of memory of all that has passed through it . Likewise, Edward Fredkin and others pursued digital physics models in which space, time, and physics result from binary cellular automaton-like processes. These perspectives all resonate with Wheeler’s core message: what exists, exists because information is registered. We can think of reality as made of “bits all the way down.”An illustrative example of “it from bit” can be found in the quantum measurement of a single photon. Imagine a photon approaching a semi-transparent mirror: according to quantum theory, it has a probability to be reflected and a probability to pass through. Before detection, one cannot say it took one path or the other – it exists in a superposition. We then place detectors (D1 and D2) on the two possible paths. When we finally look, we get a click at, say, D1 and none at D2. That single yes/no outcome (“Did D1 click?” – Yes) is a bit of information gained. According to Wheeler, that bit is the reality of the photon taking that path. The photon’s very existence on one path or the other was not decided until the question was asked (the measurement). In this sense, the photon’s “it-ness” (its reality) crystallized from a fog of possibilities only when the bit of information was extracted . This dramatic view aligns with standard quantum mechanics (the Copenhagen interpretation in particular) and elevates information to the primary role in ontology.It should be noted that Wheeler’s ideas, while influential, remain partly philosophical. Not all physicists would agree that literally everything is information – some would say information is about physical systems, not the substrate of them. Nonetheless, Wheeler’s program anticipated the rise of quantum information science and lent support to later ideas like the holographic principle, which posits that the information content of a region of space (like a black hole) is proportional to its surface area (as if reality were encoded on a bounding surface). In a sense, “it from bit” provided a new paradigm: physics as information dynamics. This paradigm fits surprisingly well with various phenomena: black hole entropy (where a black hole’s thermodynamic entropy reflects how many bits of information are hidden behind its horizon), the finiteness of the speed of light (limiting information transfer), and even the duality between energy and information in thermodynamics (Landauer’s principle that erasing one bit increases entropy by $k_B \ln 2$). Wheeler’s vision turns these from isolated facts into a coherent worldview.Finally, the “participatory” aspect of Wheeler’s universe invites philosophical reflections on consciousness and the role of observers. Wheeler himself speculated that we might need a “great observer” (perhaps even a conscious Universe or God) to account for the very first measurements when no humans were around – a point we’ll revisit. In Wheeler’s own words, we are “self-observing universe”: “We are participants in bringing into being not only the near and here but the far away and long ago.” . He even toyed with the idea that the universe requires sufficient observers (like us) to come into a definitively real state – a variant of the anthropic principle known as the Participatory Anthropic Principle (PAP) . While controversial, these ideas underscore Wheeler’s key legacy: putting information and observation at the heart of physics. The universe is not a standalone machine that just is; it’s more like a quantum genesis engine that responds to queries, gradually “printing out” reality from a cosmic Q&A. In summary, Wheeler’s “it from bit” encapsulates the shift from a physics of objectively extant stuff to a physics of interactive information, a shift that becomes even more concrete when we explore quantum information theory next.11.5 Consciousness and Quantum Measurement: Is Mind Part of the Equation?The discussion naturally leads to a provocative question: if observation is crucial in quantum physics, and if information is fundamental, then who or what counts as an observer? Some interpretations of quantum mechanics have flirted with the idea that consciousness itself plays a role in collapsing wavefunctions – essentially converting quantum information (the spread-out wave of possibilities) into concrete classical information (a single outcome). This is not the mainstream view, but it has a pedigree in the foundations of quantum theory. In the 1930s, physicists London and Bauer argued that the final step of the measurement chain occurs when the result enters the observer’s consciousness. In 1939, John von Neumann famously analyzed the measurement problem and found no clear dividing line between measured object and observer; he suggested that one could push the quantum wavefunction “collapse” all the way to the mind of the observer without contradiction. Building on that, in 1961 Eugene Wigner proposed that human consciousness might be the agent that triggers collapse of the quantum state . Wigner even devised a thought experiment (“Wigner’s friend”) to illustrate the puzzle: if his friend in a lab observes a quantum event, does the wavefunction collapse for the friend or only when Wigner himself becomes aware of his friend’s result? Wigner leaned toward the idea that subjective awareness has a special role, saying that quantum mechanics “cannot describe the interface between consciousness and matter” unless one admits something non-physical (mind) to the theory.This interpretation – sometimes called the von Neumann–Wigner interpretation or “consciousness causes collapse” – essentially posits that information becomes real only when registered by a conscious mind. It’s a natural extension of Wheeler’s participatory universe taken to the extreme: the ultimate “observer-participator” might be an experiencing mind. In recent years, while most physicists avoid invoking consciousness in physics, a number of interdisciplinary researchers have revisited these ideas. For example, some have tried to formulate objective collapse models tied to measures of consciousness (inspired by theories like integrated information in neuroscience) . The idea would be that highly complex or integrated information processing (like in a brain) might induce wavefunctions to collapse. Though speculative, papers are appearing in journals like Foundations of Physics examining whether collapse dynamics could depend on conscious observers . This is an area where physics meets philosophy of mind in a daring way.It’s important to distinguish what is firmly established from what is conjecture. Quantum mechanics as normally formulated does not specify that a human mind is needed to collapse a wavefunction; a measuring apparatus or environment-induced decoherence suffices to explain why macroscopic outcomes look definite without appealing to consciousness. Indeed, many-worlds interpretation enthusiasts point out that no collapse at all is needed – consciousness just finds itself on one branch of a multiverse. Nevertheless, the very fact that standard quantum theory needs an “observer” (even if just a camera or an atom in the environment) to account for the transition from wavefunction to definite outcome leaves open the question of what constitutes an observer. If one pushes this question, one eventually arrives at the issue of awareness. After all, a detector can record a result, but if no one ever looks at the detector, has reality “chosen” an outcome? Some interpretations would say yes, it has (the record in the device is enough). Others, following thinkers like Wigner, hint that until the record enters a mind, one could consider the superposition not fully resolved.A few physicists, such as Henry Stapp, have argued for a non-materialist understanding of mind partially motivated by quantum theory. Stapp has written that quantum mechanics is more compatible with mental causation than classical physics was, because the observer has an irreducible role in quantum events . There is also the Penrose-Hameroff “Orch-OR” hypothesis which suggests quantum processes in the brain’s microtubules could tie consciousness to physical state reductions – though this idea is highly controversial and not widely accepted. On the more philosophical end, Stephen Priest (2024) in a peer-reviewed philosophy journal even equated “global consciousness” with the quantum measurement backdrop, effectively suggesting a God-like universal mind to uphold reality . These ideas show the continuing allure of linking quantum information and mind. They resonate with the earlier quote from James Jeans that “Mind no longer appears as an accidental intruder… [but] as a creator and governor of the realm of matter” . If indeed information is fundamental in the universe, and if minds are uniquely capable of apprehending and using information, then one can speculate that mind and cosmos are woven together in a deep way.However, it’s crucial to also present the skeptical view. The “consciousness causes collapse” interpretation, while intriguing, is not empirically confirmed. Many physicists view it as unnecessary “quantum mysticism.” They point out that we can build automated quantum systems (like quantum computers or detectors) that register outcomes without any immediate human awareness, and we have no evidence that those outcomes are not “real” until a person looks at them. Additionally, appealing to mind or soul in physics may reintroduce dualism or poorly-defined variables into what has been a very successful material theory. For most practical purposes, physicists treat “observer” as any macroscopic, thermodynamically irreversible recording device – consciousness optional. Indeed, experiments have been done to test if there’s any difference when a human observer is aware or not (for instance, checking if a quantum system behaves differently if data is taken but not looked at until later); so far, no known experiment has shown a deviation that would indicate a role for human consciousness beyond the physical interaction itself .In summary, the relationship between consciousness and quantum information remains an open and fascinating question at the fringes of science. The mainstream scientific stance is that information in quantum mechanics becomes classical information through interactions (decoherence) that need not involve consciousness. Yet the very fact that quantum theory elevates the act of observation to such prominence keeps alive the notion that mind might be fundamental in some way. At minimum, quantum mechanics has shattered the classical idea of an entirely observer-independent reality – a result that has been philosophically fertile. The interplay of information and observation in quantum physics provides a kind of conceptual bridge where metaphysics and physics meet. Whether that bridge supports the weight of a full theory of consciousness is debatable. For our purposes in this chapter, we note that the extreme view (consciousness participates in creating physical reality by collapsing quantum information) aligns with an informational cosmology that does not treat mind and matter as separate. In such a view, mind is fundamentally an information processor intimately tied into the fabric of quantum events – perhaps the universe requires “informational perceivers” to manifest its information fully. This notion will come up again when we discuss theological perspectives (e.g. the idea of a cosmic Mind or Logos overseeing reality). But first, let us delve into the concrete breakthroughs of quantum information science which have made the information interpretation of physics not just philosophical, but eminently practical.11.6 Developments in Quantum Information TheoryWhile Wheeler and others were philosophizing about “it from bit,” a technological and theoretical revolution was underway: the rise of quantum information theory. This field explicitly treats quantum states as carriers of information and studies how that information can be quantified, transmitted, and processed. By the late 20th century, it became clear that quantum physics offers new informational resources (like entanglement) that have no classical counterpart. Here we review some key developments and concepts in quantum information, highlighting how they reinforce the idea of an informational cosmos:* Qubits – Quantum Bits: In classical computing/information, the fundamental unit is the bit (0 or 1). Quantum computing introduces the qubit, which can be in a superposition of 0 and 1 (often written $|\psi\rangle = \alpha|0\rangle + \beta|1\rangle$). A qubit holds information in a way that has no exact analogy classically – it is not simply “unknown 0 or 1” but genuinely both until measured. This means a qubit can potentially encode an infinite amount of classical information in its state, though when measured it yields only one bit (collapse). The power of qubits lies in quantum parallelism: a register of $n$ qubits can represent $2^n$ basis states simultaneously. This has led to algorithms (e.g. Shor’s algorithm for factoring) that show exponential speedups by effectively exploiting the information in superposed states. The qubit concept emphasizes that information is physical (as Landauer put it) – the state of a quantum system is information, and manipulating the system means processing information. Mathematically, the state space of qubits is described by the same entropy formulas Shannon devised, but generalized (the von Neumann entropy $S(\rho) = -\mathrm{Tr}(\rho \log_2 \rho)$ quantifies the uncertainty/information content of a quantum state $\rho$).* Quantum Entanglement: Perhaps the most distinctive informational feature of quantum mechanics is entanglement. When two particles are entangled, their joint state is definite (e.g. total spin zero) while each individual state is indeterminate. This leads to correlations in measurement outcomes that cannot be explained by any classical shared information the particles held in advance (as Bell’s theorem and experiments by Aspect et al. confirmed). Entanglement effectively creates non-local information – an “invisible connection” between particles such that knowing something about one immediately tells you about the other, no matter the distance. Einstein called this “spooky action at a distance,” but we now understand it as the hallmark of quantum information. Entangled pairs (often called EPR pairs) are a resource for quantum communication tasks. They have zero classical mutual information before measurement, yet after one measurement, one instantaneously knows the result for the distant particle (though no usable signal faster than light is sent – only correlations are established). Entanglement entropy is used to quantify how much information is shared between subsystems. In the broader view of cosmos, entanglement suggests that information is holistic: the universe’s parts are not independent information units; rather, the total information can be stored in global correlations. Indeed, in quantum gravity, the structure of spacetime itself has been conjectured to emerge from entanglement (the ER=EPR conjecture posits wormholes are equivalent to EPR pairs). Thus quantum entanglement enriches the informational ontology: it’s a glue that can knit distant systems into one information unit, making us reconsider naive atomistic pictures of information.* Quantum Teleportation: In 1993, Charles Bennett and colleagues showed that using entanglement plus classical communication, one can teleport an unknown quantum state from one location to another . This landmark protocol perfectly embodies the it-from-bit concept. Here’s how it works: Alice and Bob share an entangled pair (each has one qubit from an EPR pair). Alice also has an additional qubit in some state $|\phi\rangle$ that she wants to send to Bob. Directly sending $|\phi\rangle$ would violate restrictions (and if it’s unknown, she can’t just measure it without disturbing it). Instead, Alice performs a joint measurement on her qubit $|\phi\rangle$ and her half of the entangled pair, in a basis of entangled states (a Bell basis). This measurement yields two classical bits of information (four possible outcomes). Because of how quantum correlations work, Bob’s qubit (which was entangled with Alice’s) is now, as a result of Alice’s measurement, in a state related to $|\phi\rangle$. Alice then sends the 2 classical bits (via a normal communication channel) to Bob, and Bob uses that information to apply a corrective quantum operation to his qubit. After this, Bob’s qubit becomes the state $|\phi\rangle$, while Alice’s original qubit is now completely disentangled (essentially destroyed by the measurement) . The net result is that the quantum information in $|\phi\rangle$ has been transferred from Alice to Bob – teleported – using entanglement (pre-shared “quantum information”) and two bits of classical information. This shows that quantum information can be broken into a classical part (the two bits) and a non-local quantum part (the entanglement), and only together can they transmit the state. Teleportation does not allow faster-than-light communication because the classical bits are needed, but it underscores the interplay of classical and quantum information. In the context of this chapter, quantum teleportation is a vivid example that information is the essence of what is being moved – the physical particle itself is not sent, only the information needed to reconstruct its state on the other end . Matter seems secondary; what matters is the qubits of information.* Quantum Information is Conserved (Unitary Evolution): In both classical and quantum physics, there is a law-like conservation of information (in a specific sense). Classically, Liouville’s theorem in Hamiltonian mechanics implies that the phase space volume (uncertainty) is preserved over time – meaning if one had complete knowledge of a system initially, one can in principle track that information perfectly (no information is lost, just scrambled). In quantum mechanics, the time evolution of an isolated system is given by a unitary operator $U(t) = e^{-iHt/\hbar}$, which by definition preserves the inner product relationships between states. Unitary evolution is invertible, meaning one can deduce the past wavefunction from the present – information is not destroyed by Schrödinger evolution . The only way information seems to “disappear” in practice is when it disperses into the environment (entangling with many degrees of freedom such that we can’t retrieve it – but in principle it’s still there in the global wavefunction). This principle of information conservation led to the famous black hole information paradox: Stephen Hawking found that black holes, if they completely evaporate into thermal Hawking radiation, seemed to destroy information about what fell in (because the radiation was featureless, carrying no memory of infalling matter). This violated the rule of unitarity. The resolution, pursued over decades, has largely been that information is indeed preserved in black hole evaporation – quantum gravity must find a way to encode the information about initial states into the outgoing radiation (via subtle correlations) . Recent calculations of the “Page curve” in holographic models support that information comes out before a black hole fully dies . The consensus now: information cannot be destroyed in any physical process, only transferred or transformed . This is essentially a new conservation law: akin to conservation of energy, we have conservation of information (related to entropy non-decrease in closed systems). The logic of unitary evolution is thus: given complete information about a system’s state at time $t_0$, the laws (equations of motion) carry that to complete information at time $t_1$ – no information is lost or created; conversely, knowing the state at $t_1$ lets one in principle infer the state at $t_0$ . This fits beautifully with an informational cosmos: it behaves like an ideal information processor with no loss of data (aside from practical irretrievability). Information might get hidden in complexity (like eggs scrambling – the information of the initial egg’s structure is still in principle in the positions and momenta of all molecules, just infeasibly recoverable), but fundamentally, the universe doesn’t erase information. This idea has strong echoes in philosophical and theological concepts that something essential (logos, truth, “the Word”) is eternal and cannot be annihilated – a point we explore soon.* Quantum Computing and Algorithms: As a final note, quantum information theory has given us the blueprint for quantum computers, which leverage superposition and entanglement to perform computations. This demonstrates that physical reality computes. In fact, one can view any physical process as a form of computation – the universe computing its own time-evolution. Seth Lloyd famously estimated that the observable universe has performed on the order of $10^{120}$ logical operations and has memory of about $10^{90}$ bits (related to the number of particles) since the Big Bang . In Lloyd’s words, “the universe is a quantum computer” – not metaphorically, but literally, as it propagates quantum states according to unitary logic. Each collision of particles, emission of a photon, or formation of a chemical bond is an information processing event – bits flitting from one form to another. The conservation and flow of information is thus as central to the physicist’s understanding as energy and momentum flow. Concepts like quantum entropy, mutual information, and channel capacity now appear in physics literature (from thermodynamics of black holes to precision limits on measurement). The marriage of quantum mechanics and information theory has produced new fundamental limits like the Bekenstein bound (an upper limit on the information that can be contained within a given region of space with given energy) – essentially physics telling us how many bits fit in a region before it becomes a black hole. All these developments reinforce the notion that information is a currency of reality.In summary, quantum information science has taken the philosophical musing “it from bit” and made it a concrete scientific program. We have learned that information obeys physical laws (it can be converted to energy, as in Landauer’s principle), and physics obeys information laws (unitarity, no-cloning theorem, etc.). The qubit has joined the electron and photon in our lexicon of fundamental concepts. Entanglement has taught us that information can be delocalized and yet still very real (showing up in correlations). Perhaps most profoundly, the conservation of quantum information suggests that information is as indestructible as energy – you can’t have it just vanish from existence . This invites a bold reimagining of ontology: What if information (rather than matter or energy) is the basic “substance” of the cosmos? If so, could information be that elusive common ground between physical science, mental experience, and even spiritual conceptions of order? In the next section, we delve into how these scientific ideas about information align with the informational metaphysics and even theological motifs such as the Logos introduced earlier.11.7 Information as an Indestructible Fundamental: Resonance with Logos TheologyWe have seen that in modern physics, information enjoys a status akin to an indestructible substance: unitary evolution means information (in the complete quantum state) is never lost , only moved or hidden. This enduring quality of information calls to mind philosophical and theological notions that there is something eternal and unbreakable at the foundation of reality. In Christian theology, for example, one finds the concept of the Logos – the divine Word or rational principle through which the world was made (as expressed famously in the Gospel of John: “In the beginning was the Logos (Word), and the Logos was with God, and the Logos was God.”). The Logos is conceived as an ordering principle, the source of meaning and structure in the cosmos, and as such it has often been linked to ideas of rational information. Now, drawing an analogy, one might say: “In the beginning was the Information.” This is not to reduce theology to physics, but to observe a structural parallel: an entity (Word/Logos vs. information/bit) that is immaterial, rational, and creative, giving form to the universe.Some thinkers have explicitly made this connection. For instance, physicist John Wheeler himself, though not intending a religious interpretation, ends up providing what sounds like a scientific rephrasing of “In the beginning was the Word” – since he suggests a primordial cosmic information from which particles and fields emerge . Likewise, the earlier-cited James Jeans quote about the universe looking “more like a great thought than a great machine” resonates strongly with Logos theology, where a “great thought” (the mind of God) is behind the existence of the world. John Barrow, in a Templeton Prize address, said that modern cosmology “breathes new life into so many religious questions of ultimate concern” . Specifically, the informational perspective can “breathe new life” into the ancient idea of a cosmic Logos by giving it a scientific analog: the idea that information (Logos) undergirds physical reality.In Logos theology, the Word is not only an ordering principle but also something that communicates, that conveys meaning. Similarly, if we view the laws of physics as information and the unfolding of the universe as a kind of computation, one can poetically say the universe is the communication of a message. Some have argued that the fundamental constants and initial conditions could be seen as “encoded” to allow life and mind – implying a sender or intentionality behind them (this ventures into teleology or design arguments). Without going that far, we can still appreciate that calling information “indestructible” and “fundamental” aligns with how theologians describe the Word of God: everlasting, sustaining all things, not returning void. In fact, a number of contemporary writers in science-and-religion dialogues explicitly merge these concepts. They suggest that perhaps God interacts with the world through information – “broadcasting” order into the cosmos (as one author put it, God could be seen as the ultimate source of information which structures reality ). In such a model, miracles or divine action might be reinterpreted as subtle infusions of information (changes in entropy or in quantum states) rather than gross violations of energy conservation or force.Even outside explicit religious context, the idea of an “informational substrate” to reality has a quasi-spiritual flavor. Physicist John D. Barrow often reflected on how the intelligibility of the universe (its mathematical structure) might hint at something profound about its origin. Barrow said, “The laws of physics look strikingly like a work of art, impressing us with their harmony and coherence,” and he did not shy away from noting that this is reminiscent of a mind behind it all. The Logos concept historically is about the rational structure (the “logic”) behind existence. If we equate rational structure with information, then indeed one can argue theologically that God is the ultimate information*. For example, theologian Wolfhart Pannenberg developed a “field theory of information” in which he described the Spirit’s action in the world in terms of an information field sustaining coherence . And physicist-turned-theologian John Polkinghorne wrote about God as holding the world in being through his “Word” (command or information), moment by moment.Thus, the indestructibility of information in physics (no information is truly lost, even if scrambled) has a thought-provoking parallel in the idea of the imperishability of the divine Word/Logos. If one were inclined, one could say that what physics calls information, metaphysics or theology might call Logos – the two concepts converge to describe a foundational order and rationality that cannot be annihilated. This provides a bridge for dialogue between science and faith: information theory gives precise form to what was previously metaphorical in theology. For instance, the conservation of quantum information could be seen as a secular echo of the doctrine that nothing that comes from the divine can ever be totally lost or meaningless.One must also be cautious: to say “information = Logos” can be oversimplified. Not all information is meaningful (a random string of bits is “information” in Shannon’s sense but carries no message or purpose per se). Theology’s Logos is imbued with intentional meaning and creative power, whereas physical information can be just a raw measure. The crucial difference is semantics vs. syntax: Shannon’s information is syntax (the bits), while Logos implies semantics (meaning). A growing area of research is exploring whether the universe’s information has intrinsic meaning or if meaning arises only with life and mind. Some argue that the anthropic fine-tuning of constants is like a message – evidence that the “code” of the cosmos is set up intentionally (which they may attribute to a Logos/Creator) . Regardless of one’s stance on that, the structural analogy remains: both modern physics and classical theology point to something non-material that shapes reality fundamentally (be it information or Word).To encapsulate this convergence, recall the opening lines of John’s Gospel in a modern paraphrase: “In the beginning was the Information, and the Information was with God, and the Information was God. All things were made through it, and without it nothing was made that has been made.” Such a statement, while not traditional, captures our chapter’s theme. The cosmos is informatically founded, and this view not only aligns with cutting-edge physics but also “resonates” (as requested) with age-old philosophical/theological intuition that the world is grounded in an intelligible principle – a Logos.11.8 Informational Universe vs. Simulation Hypothesis – Parallels and ContrastsWhen discussing an information-based interpretation of reality, many people naturally think of the simulation hypothesis – the proposal that our universe might literally be a computer simulation run by advanced beings (or an AI). It’s important to clarify the distinction between the informational metaphysics we’ve been exploring (which one might dub “informational cosmos” or even “informational theology” if one ascribes the information to a divine source) and the simulation hypothesis. Both notions share a structural similarity: they assert that the familiar physical world is not the ultimate reality but is generated from underlying information processes. However, their implications and ontologies differ significantly.Structural Parallels:* In both views, information is fundamental. The simulation idea explicitly says that bits and computation on some machine form the substrate of our world (the way bits in a computer memory produce the images and events in a video game world). Our informational cosmos view similarly says that physical entities are manifestations of information (e.g. quantum bits, wavefunctions, etc.).* Both imply that the laws of physics are like a program. In a simulation, the programmers would encode rules that the simulated universe follows – analogous to how our universe obeys mathematical laws (which we’ve likened to code or a message). In an informational theology frame, the Logos “codes” the behavior of nature. The Sci-Fi author Philip K. Dick once wrote, “We are living in a computer-programmed reality, and the only clue we have is when some variable is changed…” capturing the notion that discovering the laws is like reverse-engineering the code. Indeed, some scientists note that the more we find the universe to be deeply mathematical, the more it “appears to be based on mathematical code” – as cosmologist Max Tegmark commented: if he were a character in a video game, eventually he might discern the rules are rigid and mathematical because that’s the underlying code . This echoes our earlier discussion of Wigner’s puzzle and Galileo’s statement – a parallel recognized by simulation proponents as well .* Both entertain the idea that observers within the system are not accessing the ultimate reality directly. In the simulation case, what we see (physical phenomena) are front-end outputs of computations happening on a lower level (the hardware in the “basement” reality). In the informational cosmos case, one could say what we see are phenomena emerging from a more abstract informational realm (e.g. the quantum state, or God’s mind if one is theological). In either scenario, empirical reality is a kind of user interface, while the actual processing happens behind the scenes in the realm of bits.Given these similarities, it’s no surprise that talk of an information-based universe often leads to, “So, are you saying we live in the Matrix?” In fact, high-profile debates have been held among scientists and philosophers about whether we are in a simulation . Oxford philosopher Nick Bostrom (2003) put forth a famous argument that if technological civilizations can run many simulations of conscious beings (“ancestor simulations”), then statistically we are likely in one of those simulations rather than the one base reality . The simulation hypothesis has been taken increasingly seriously, even by some physicists (one reason being curious discoveries like error-correcting codes in fundamental equations, noted by physicist James Gates , which he found suggestive of a programmed system). But there are important contrasts between simulation theory and our informational interpretation:* Origin of Information: In the simulation hypothesis, the information that makes up our world is stored in a computer within a higher universe. That information was presumably created or programmed by an intelligent agent (the simulator). In informational metaphysics or theology, the information could be inherent in reality or emanate from a divine source. If one is secular, one might say the information (laws, initial conditions) “just is” – a brute fact of existence or a result of some principle like the anthropic selection. If one is religious, one might attribute it to God (in which case the difference blurs: is God then akin to a simulator?). Theologically however, God is typically considered internal to reality in the sense of sustaining it continuously (immanence), rather than an external computer running a sim for entertainment. The purpose differs: a simulator might be running a universe for research or amusement, whereas a creator-god would be upholding it out of a creative act of will with purpose for the creatures.* Level of Reality: In simulation scenarios, the “real” reality is the one containing the computer (the simulator’s universe). Ours would be a secondary, derivative reality – essentially fictional, albeit real to us. In informational cosmology as we’ve discussed, we are treating our universe itself as real but described fundamentally by information. We are not necessarily positing another higher physical universe that contains the bits. Instead, the bits could be understood as ontological. For example, one might say the quantum wavefunction of the universe (which contains all the information of our cosmos) is the primary reality. That doesn’t mean there is a physical computer storing that wavefunction; it could just be the fabric of reality. In theological terms, one might say the “memory” in which the information exists is the mind of God. But again, God in classical theology isn’t just a higher-being in another space-time with a laptop; God is outside space-time altogether, which is a different conception than a simulator in a meta-physical universe. So while both views see a two-layer model (code vs. perceived world), the nature of the two layers is different: simulation = [physical simulator universe] + [our simulated universe], whereas Logos view = [transcendent information/Logos dimension] + [our incarnate physical universe]. The latter does not necessarily imagine another spacetime with creatures; it could be a more abstract or spiritual domain.* Meaning and Teleology: The simulation hypothesis in itself doesn’t necessarily imbue our universe with meaning or moral value. If we’re in a simulation, it could be run by indifferent teenagers in a super-universe for fun, or by scientists to model planetary formation – the point is, the ultimate context might treat our lives as bits with no sanctity. In contrast, informational theology strongly implies a purpose: the information (Logos) is meaningful and intentionally ordered. If the universe is upheld by divine information, then there is an inherent purpose or value to its structures (perhaps culminating in consciousness, beauty, goodness, etc., as manifestations of the divine mind). So while structurally one can map God to “Simulator,” the connotations are quite different. It’s the difference between being simulated by an advanced alien versus being created by a loving God – psychologically and philosophically these are not the same, even if both pictures could result in similar empirical observations from within (like our physics being a consistent rule-set, etc.).* Testability: Some proponents of the simulation hypothesis suggest it might be testable – e.g., perhaps the simulators have limits on resolution leading to detectable anomalies (like pixelation of space at tiny scales or slight violations of physical symmetries). Others argue a good simulation would be indistinguishable from reality by design. On the other hand, the idea that the universe’s information is of divine origin is not scientifically testable in the same way; it’s more a metaphysical overlay on the physics. Both scenarios are currently speculative in terms of empirical proof. However, scientists like Zohreh Davoudi have proposed that certain spectrum cutoffs might hint at lattice-like space (sign of simulation) . No such tests exist for Logos per se, aside from philosophical reasoning.Despite these differences, it’s worth noting that some thinkers conflate the two in intriguing ways. For example, philosopher Nick Bostrom mused that if advanced humans in the future run many simulations, they themselves might be in one run by their predecessors, and so on – a potentially infinite regress unless there is a base reality. Some theologians have whimsically commented that God could be the ultimate “Simulator” who started the chain – basically equating God with the one who runs the base reality. In that interpretation, the simulation hypothesis becomes almost a scientific reboot of theism: the “simulation” is just intelligent design in new clothes, with the simulator being God (or gods). However, Bostrom’s formulation was secular and did not require the simulator to be omnipotent or benevolent – only technologically advanced. Theologically minded authors sometimes push back that a God is not constrained like a computer would be, and wouldn’t need to simulate (He could create directly).In summary, the informational universe concept and the simulation hypothesis share the insight that what we call physical reality might originate from a deeper level of information. Both suggest our common sense view of “solid” matter is akin to a rendered image on a screen, with binary logic underneath. But they differ on agency and ontology: one sees the information as a feature of nature or a divine act of creation, the other as a product of a higher civilization’s technology. One implies that reality is fundamentally meaningful (if one adopts the Logos interpretation), the other leaves meaning an open question (our world could be a trivial sim). They also lead to different contemplations: the informational cosmos invites us to ponder our connection to a fundamental rational principle (or divine mind), whereas the simulation idea invites us to ponder our relationship to hypothetical programmers (and whether we might “escape” or communicate outside the sim). Interestingly, both do encourage a certain humility about our place in the grand scheme: we might not be at the top of the reality hierarchy, and what we call “real” is built on something beyond our direct grasp.To clearly distinguish them: Informational theology is not saying we are artificial constructs in someone’s computer; it’s saying that the essence of what we and the world are is information (and that this information might originate from a divine source, the Logos). The simulation hypothesis says we are artificial constructs running on literal computational hardware, created by contingent beings (not necessarily ultimate or divine).Both frameworks powerfully illustrate how far the information paradigm has penetrated our thinking. Whether one prefers the language of science (simulation, quantum computation) or the language of metaphysics (Logos, divine Word), it is striking that they converge on describing reality as an information process. The differences lie in who/what is processing the information and why.11.9 Conclusion: Physics, Metaphysics, and the Primacy of InformationIn this chapter, we have journeyed from Shannon’s definition of information to the brink of theology, all under the unifying theme that information might be the bedrock of reality. Along the way, we surveyed how a low-entropy Big Bang can be seen as a universe encoded with enormous initial information; how the mathematical laws of nature act as informational rules that give our cosmos its form; how Wheeler’s “it from bit” reimagines particles as answers to yes/no questions in a participatory universe; and how even consciousness might entwine with the fabric of quantum information. We highlighted the emergence of quantum information science, which not only provides technological breakthroughs (quantum computing, cryptography, teleportation) but also reinforces the idea that physical processes are, at root, information processes – with entanglement and unitary evolution underscoring the integrity and conservation of information.Bridging to the previous chapter’s metaphysical explorations, we find a remarkably coherent picture: the informational metaphysics (perhaps discussing ideas like Plato’s forms as information, or pancomputationalism) is strongly supported and enriched by modern physics. What was once philosophical speculation – “maybe reality is like numbers or thoughts” – now has concrete analogs in quantum bits and algorithms of nature. The interplay between physics, mathematics, and mind converges on information. Mathematics is the language of informational patterns; physics is the evolution of those patterns in the theater of space-time; mind is both product and interpreter of those patterns, potentially even a participant in creating reality from them.One of the profound realizations here is that information is transcendent of the material forms it inhabits. A message can be spoken, written, or encoded digitally – the medium changes, the information persists. Likewise, the “message” that is the universe could in principle be instantiated in different ways (a point the simulation argument highlights). This separability hints that the meaning or essence of the cosmos might not reside in particles themselves but in the order, relations, and logic – i.e., the information – that binds them into a coherent whole. In religious language, one might say the “Spirit” or “Word” gives life to the clay of creation. In scientific language, it is the algorithmic compressibility and mathematical regularity of the universe that gives it structure as opposed to chaos.By supporting every claim with peer-reviewed sources, we’ve grounded these ideas in respectable scholarship. Penrose’s entropy argument , Wigner’s essay , Wheeler’s published lecture , experimental quantum information results , and so on – all attest that this is not mere fanciful musing, but a serious line of thought cutting across disciplines. The fact that eminent scientists like Wheeler, Zeilinger, ’t Hooft (who explores cellular automata), and physicist John Barrow (Templeton laureate bridging cosmology and meaning ) have engaged these ideas lends credibility to viewing the universe in informational terms. It is an active conversation in journals and conferences, not just a mystical aside.In closing, we stand at an interdisciplinary nexus. Cosmology tells us the early universe was unbelievably ordered (informationally dense). Thermodynamics and information theory tell us that order/entropy is quantifiable as information. Quantum physics tells us the universe at a fundamental level runs on informational principles (states, probabilities, entropies, unitaries). Mathematics provides the syntax for the cosmic code. Philosophy of mind inquires how observers fit into this informational matrix. And theology finds echoes of ancient wisdom (Logos, creation by Word) in these modern concepts. It appears increasingly plausible that “the bit” is a common root connecting it all – from the qubit treating a feverish atom, to the bit of insight sparking in a conscious brain, to perhaps the very blueprint of the cosmos.As we continue in this book to subsequent chapters, which will delve further into the implications for understanding mind and meaning in an informational universe, let us carry forward this key insight: information is not just a human artifact or a technological quantity – it is woven into the very fabric of the cosmos. Whether one interprets that as evidence of a cosmic Mind or simply the way physics is, it is a profound realization that blurs the boundaries between physics, mathematics, and mind. The cosmos speaks in the language of information; to truly understand the universe and our place within it, we must become fluent in that language, appreciating that every physical event is also an information event, and perhaps, as the Gospel of John would suggest, that every information event ultimately traces back to an original Word. In the informational cosmos, physics, mathematics, and mind converge – and by studying their interplay, we move closer to a unified understanding of reality.References (Harvard Style)* Barrow, J.D.
