This “informational theology” conceives of reality fundamentally in terms of information, with God as the cosmic communicator or Logos (the rational divine Word or code underlying existence) . Such a perspective finds echoes in both ancient thought and cutting-edge science. The Stoics, for instance, saw the logos as the “active reason” pervading and animating the universe, identifying it with God . In modern physics, John Archibald Wheeler’s famous dictum “It from bit” asserts that every physical entity derives from binary choices – information at the most primordial level . As Wheeler put it, “all things physical are information-theoretic in origin” . Philosopher of information Luciano Floridi likewise argues that “information… [can be] a foundation for… a cosmic value system”, with information growth equated to goodness and its loss to evil . Even Fred Dretske quipped in the preface to his seminal work that “In the beginning there was information. The word came later.” – a play on the biblical Logos that underscores the idea of information as a primal reality. Embracing this framework, we will reinterpret each piece of evidence for God’s existence as a manifestation of divine information. Each subsection below will recall how the evidence was originally used in our probabilistic model, then offer a rigorous re-reading through the informational lens, citing relevant scientific, philosophical, and theological sources. Objections from naturalist or atheistic perspectives will be noted and addressed. Throughout, we will use metaphors from information theory (e.g. signal vs. noise, sender/receiver) to illustrate concepts. The chapter culminates in an “Informational Unity of Evidence” section, integrating the insights into a single coherent paradigm.Fine-TuningOriginal perspective: The Cosmological Fine-Tuning argument observes that the fundamental constants and initial conditions of the universe fall within an extraordinarily narrow range that permits the existence of complex life. Even slight deviations in parameters like the strength of gravity or the cosmological constant would render the universe lifeless . Proponents argue this is unlikely to have happened by chance; rather, it looks as if the universe’s “settings” were deliberately calibrated for life – suggestive of an intelligent Designer . For example, the cosmological constant appears fine-tuned to about 1 part in 10^120; such precision “cries out for explanation” if one assumes a random origin . On a theistic interpretation, if God intended to create embodied life, we expect life-permitting conditions, whereas under atheistic chance they are astronomically improbable . In Bayesian terms, observing a life-friendly universe yields a huge likelihood ratio favoring the God hypothesis (assuming God’s aim to create life) . As William Lane Craig emphasizes, the combination of an independent pattern (the specific life-permitting “target”) with exceedingly low probability on naturalism are “earmarks of design,” making God’s existence “clearly more probable given the fine-tuning… than it would have been without it” . Detractors traditionally counter with the multiverse (if countless universes exist, one could randomly have the right constants) or question the premise of how “narrow” the life-permitting range truly is . Nonetheless, fine-tuning is widely regarded in physics and philosophy as real and in need of explanation .Informational reinterpretation: Under informational theology, we reinterpret fine-tuning as evidence of a cosmic information input or “program” underlying physical reality. The precise values of constants can be seen as encoded parameters in the initial conditions of the cosmos – analogous to how specific bits in a computer’s configuration file enable certain functions. That our universe’s “settings” are exactly those needed for life is akin to finding a complex software program that just happens to run meaningful, intricate processes: it points to an intelligent source of that information. In Shannon’s information theory, highly improbable configurations carry high information content . The fine-tuning data indeed represent an improbability so extreme that it conveys an enormous amount of specified information about the universe’s structure. In technical terms, if an outcome has probability on the order of 10^(-120) under a null hypothesis, the information content (surprisal) of that outcome is about  $- \log_2(10^{-120}) \approx 400$ bits – an astronomically large amount of information . The fine-tuned cosmos can thus be viewed as containing a message: a  “message” that life is intended. Physicist Paul Davies notes that the universe “is rigged in favor of life” in a way that suggests an underlying rationality or intent, effectively an embedded code or blueprint . John Wheeler’s it-from-bit thesis pushes this further: physical constants and laws may themselves arise from information-theoretic principles . If God is the ultimate programmer, the fine-tuning is like the well-chosen lines of code that yield a life-permitting simulation. On this view, the universe is not a random assortment of physical stuff but a meaningful information structure. This aligns with the insight of Norbert Wiener (the founder of cybernetics) that “Information is information, not matter or energy. No materialism which does not admit this can survive.” – the informational architecture (e.g. the values of constants, the form of physical laws) is a reality in its own right, and one most naturally explained by a purposive mind. In summary, fine-tuning fits the paradigm of a cosmos where God as Logos imprints informational order onto the fabric of reality.Objections and counterpoints: A naturalistic skeptic might argue that calling physical constants “information” is merely a semantic recasting – the constants could just be brute facts or products of a multiverse “selection effect” with no need for a sender. Indeed, multiverse proponents claim that if there are zillions of universes with random parameters, eventually one will by chance have the requisite information for life (akin to a monkey randomly typing a meaningful sentence). However, even multiverse theories often presuppose higher-level laws or meta-information (the mechanism generating universes must itself be finely tuned to produce life-friendly outcomes) . The informational paradigm highlights this meta-problem: the existence of a multiverse still begs an explanation for the information-rich mechanism that yields life-permitting universes . Some theorists have suggested that information itself might be a fundamental component of reality (e.g. mathematical universe hypotheses or pancomputational models) – but without an intelligent ground, it is hard to explain why the information embedded in nature is meaningful (life-conducive and ordered) rather than random noise. By conceiving God as the source of cosmic information, we can coherently explain why the “cosmic code” appears deliberate. The fine-tuning, then, is not a coincidence but the signature of a cosmic Author encoding a life-hosting world . While this doesn’t prove God’s existence, within an informational framework the fine-tuning gains a unifying explanation that is philosophically rich: the universe’s fundamental constants are the bits of a divine message.Moral ObjectivityOriginal perspective: The Moral Objectivity variable in our model captured the intuition that objective moral truths or values exist (e.g. that torturing an innocent child is wrong, universally and not just by convention), and that such truths are more expected if God exists. The classic moral argument posits that if moral values are real and binding independent of human opinion, they likely originate from a transcendent source (a moral lawgiver) rather than from evolution or social contracts . In popular apologetics, C. S. Lewis famously called the moral law “a clue to the meaning of the universe”, arguing that our innate sense of right and wrong points to a higher moral Mind behind reality . William Lane Craig formulates the argument succinctly: 1. If God does not exist, objective moral values do not exist. 2. Objective moral values do exist. 3. Therefore, God exists. . While philosophers debate each premise, our probabilistic model treated the widespread human perception of objective morality, and the substantial philosophical arguments for its existence, as evidence tilting toward theism (since a theistic God provides a robust ontological foundation for moral facts). Objections include naturalist accounts of morality: perhaps evolution ingrained us with social instincts that we mistake for objective morals, or moral realism might be a fundamental feature of the universe without requiring God (as some atheist philosophers like Erik Wielenberg contend). Still, the “queerness” of objective moral facts in a purely material world – an ought that exists beyond any description of matter – is often seen as supportive of a transcendent ground.Informational reinterpretation: In informational terms, we can think of objective moral truths as part of the divine information structure of reality – effectively, the moral law is like a source code or governing algorithm for how conscious agents ought to behave. If God is the ultimate information and the cosmos is His communication, then moral principles represent a high-level message about how creatures with free will should use that freedom. An analogy can be drawn from data embedded in a complex system: just as DNA carries prescriptive information for living cells, one could say the fabric of reality carries prescriptive moral information for personal agents (us). Augustine’s theological concept of divine ideas is illuminating here – he taught that the archetypal forms or truths (including moral truths) reside in the mind of God and are imparted to creation . All created beings thus reflect, to varying degrees, the rational order and goodness of those divine ideas . Under this view, moral truths are not arbitrary fiats but expressions of God’s own nature (the ultimate “moral information”). Humans, being made imago Dei (in God’s image), have the capacity to receive or discern this moral information – akin to a radio receiver tuning into a broadcast. Our conscience and rational recognition of duty can be seen as the built-in antenna for the moral signal that God’s character emanates. This metaphor maps well onto information theory: God is the Sender of the moral law (the signal), the human mind is the Receiver, and the channel is our practical reason, which can sometimes be noisy or distorted by selfish desires. Yet across cultures we find striking commonality in basic moral principles (fairness, prohibitions on murder, etc.), which C. S. Lewis and others attribute to the constant underlying broadcast of the moral law .Luciano Floridi’s information ethics provides a contemporary philosophical reinforcement. Floridi suggests that ethical value can be understood in terms of information: actions that increase or preserve information (structure, well-being, flourishing) are good, whereas actions that destroy or corrupt information (causing entropy in the “infosphere”) are evil . In this light, an objective moral fact (e.g. “kindness is good”) corresponds to a principle that promoting certain informational patterns (like the flourishing of conscious beings) is intrinsically valuable. If God is the ultimate information, then aligning with objective moral truth means aligning with the divine information flow that upholds goodness. Moral objectivity thus fits the model of a universe designed as a meaningful communication: just as physical laws communicate the rules for matter and energy, moral laws communicate the rules for free agents. This provides a unifying metaphysical explanation for why moral realism “feels” true to us – we are effectively reading the moral code written by God into reality.Objections and counterpoints: A critic might argue that this informational take on morality is metaphorical – can we really talk about moral facts as “bits” of information? It is true that moral truths are not information in the Shannon sense (measurable in bits), but the analogy is fruitful in capturing how morals convey meaning and intent, just as messages do. Another objection is the Euthyphro dilemma: is something good because God “broadcasts” it, or does God broadcast it because it’s good? The informational framework would respond that God’s nature is the fundamental source of all goodness and truth, so God’s “moral information” is not arbitrary data but self-expression of that nature. Without God, it’s hard to see why the universe would contain any binding moral information – it would be akin to a computer with no programmer to write a code of ethics for the avatars inside. Non-theistic moral realists, of course, posit that moral truths just exist necessarily, perhaps as abstract Platonic facts. But then one must explain how these abstract ethical bits of information are integrated into the concrete world and recognized by evolved beings. By contrast, if humans are equipped by God with a moral receiver (conscience) tuned to objective values, our evolution and moral cognition have a coherent explanation: we are meant to apprehend the moral signal. This view doesn’t deny evolutionary or social influences on moral beliefs, but it suggests those are the means by which the basic moral architecture (the divine code) is implemented in us. In summary, conceiving objective morals as part of a divinely authored information-system makes robust sense of their incumbency and our perceptions, whereas a strictly materialist view struggles to account for the normative dimension (the “ought-ness”) that information in the moral realm carries .Consciousness GapOriginal perspective: The Consciousness Gap refers to the explanatory gap in science’s understanding of consciousness – specifically the difficulty of explaining subjective experience (qualia) and self-awareness purely in terms of physical processes. In our model, this variable captured the intuition that consciousness is a fundamental reality not reducible to neurons, and that its existence might suggest a transcendent source (e.g. a soul or divine mind) rather than being an emergent epiphenomenon of matter. Philosophers of mind like David Chalmers famously dubbed consciousness the “hard problem” because no purely physical theory has yet bridged the gap between objective brain states and subjective feeling . Theism offers an attractive explanation: if mind originates from a greater Mind (God), then consciousness in creatures is not surprising – it is a kind of image of the divine mental nature. Our probabilistic model included this variable as evidence for God on the grounds that strict materialism has trouble accounting for first-person awareness, intentionality, and qualia, whereas a dualistic or idealist worldview (often harmonizing with theism) naturally accommodates them. In simpler terms, if the universe were purely impersonal and physical, why would sentience ever arise? Yet it has – which might indicate a fundamental mind-like aspect to reality consistent with the existence of a cosmic consciousness (God).Informational reinterpretation: Within an informational theology framework, we can recast consciousness as the receiver in the cosmic information exchange. If reality is conceived as God communicating information (through physical structure, moral law, etc.), then consciousness is the faculty that receives, interprets, and responds to that information. In technical terms, think of consciousness as a decoder. God, the ultimate sender, has embedded meaning in the universe; conscious minds are the instruments by which meaning is decoded and understood. This perspective bears similarity to John Wheeler’s idea of a “participatory universe,” where observers are not just passive but in some sense necessary participants in bringing about meaningful reality . Notably, Eugene Wigner, a Nobel-winning physicist, argued that “it was not possible to formulate the laws of quantum mechanics in a fully consistent way without reference to consciousness.” In other words, the act of observation (conscious awareness) enters fundamentally into the structure of physical theory. Interpreted theologically, this could hint that consciousness is woven into the informational fabric of the cosmos by design. On a more philosophical note, Thomas Nagel has suggested that the appearance of conscious beings is baked into the universe’s blueprint – implying a kind of teleology or intended informational outcome .In the informational metaphor, each individual mind might be seen as a node in a grand network, linked to the divine mind as the server or cloud. Our conscious experiences (qualia) are like data packets processed locally, yet their very existence relies on the network’s architecture supporting such data. Augustine proposed that true knowledge involves divine illumination – the idea that our minds connect to God’s light to actually recognize truth. This resonates with the notion of the mind as a receiver: to truly know anything (mathematical truth, moral truth, etc.) is to sync up with the information in God’s intellect. In modern terms, philosopher Frederick Dretske’s work naturalizing mental content still treated information as the bridge between mind and world: “objective information… bridges physical and semantic realms, so that information becomes the raw material from which meaning… is manufactured.” . If meaning (semantic content) is ultimately grounded in God (the ultimate semantic encoder), then our consciousness is what allows that meaning to be instantiated in the world – we “download” and act upon God’s information. This casts the mind-body problem in a new light: perhaps the reason consciousness doesn’t fit neatly into a materialist frame is that it’s not just another emergent property, but a fundamental aspect of the informational structure – essentially, every conscious soul is a spark of the divine Logos, a point where the cosmic information becomes self-aware. The gap in explanation that materialists face suggests an ontological mismatch; by including God (a supreme mind) and information as fundamental, that gap may be naturally closed (mind comes from Mind).Objections and counterpoints: Materialists would object that invoking God or a cosmic information sender to explain consciousness is a “God of the gaps” move – just plugging mystery with mystery. They might point instead to developing science: perhaps integrated information theory (IIT) or emergentist models will eventually show how bits and patterns produce awareness. Indeed, IIT equates the degree of consciousness with the amount of integrated information (?) in a system, implying that information structure is deeply linked to subjective experience. Our informational theology can actually embrace such insights: if higher integrated information correlates with consciousness, this is exactly what we’d expect if consciousness just is the perception of information at a certain level of complexity. What IIT calls “integrated information,” a theist could call “the breath of God” organizing matter into a self-aware form – colorful language, but pointing to the idea that the emergence of ? might be the formal way God’s infusion of a soul or mind manifests. A naturalist counter-explanation is that evolution optimized brains for information-processing, and consciousness is an accidental byproduct of that. But this fails to address why subjective experience accompanies the processing at all; one could in principle have complex data-handling with no inner life. The informational perspective suggests instead that consciousness is meant for communication: it allows a two-way exchange with the divine. We not only receive God’s informational input (through perception, moral intuition, etc.), we also can send information back (prayer, creative thoughts, freely willed actions that contribute new information to the world). In short, by framing God as the ultimate information and our minds as derivative information processors, we get a holistic picture where the “hard problem” of consciousness is softened – consciousness is the expected result when divine information synergizes with matter in God’s image. It is less a surprising anomaly and more the very point of the cosmic information enterprise.Religious ExperienceOriginal perspective: Religious Experience encompasses a broad range of phenomena in which individuals report direct awareness of the divine or transcendent reality – from mystical union and prophetic visions to the simple sense of presence during prayer. In our model, this was considered evidence in favor of God’s existence insofar as such experiences are numerous, consistent (in core elements), and often transformative in ways hard to attribute to delusion. Philosophers like Richard Swinburne have argued from the Principle of Credulity that if people seem to perceive X (especially across cultures and ages), we should prima facie trust that X exists unless we have a defeater . The model treated a world with widespread religious experience as more likely given God (who could be the cause of those experiences) than given atheism (under which it’s surprising so many would have such veridical-seeming encounters). Of course, naturalistic explanations abound: psychological wish-fulfillment, neural misfires (e.g. temporal lobe epilepsy producing visions), evolutionary byproducts (hyperactive agency detection making us sense gods), etc. Yet, while any one person’s experience might be explained away, the cumulative weight and commonalities (e.g. feelings of love, unity, encountering an awe-inspiring “Light” or ultimate Reality) point to something real, say many scholars of religion. William James, in The Varieties of Religious Experience, noted the noetic quality (sense of gaining knowledge) and lasting positive fruits of genuine mystical experiences, suggesting they tap into something beyond the mundane.Informational reinterpretation: Religious experiences can be understood as instances of signal reception in the divine communication system. If God is continuously broadcasting His presence and will (the “signal”), then a religious or mystical experience is what happens when a human “receiver” is particularly well-tuned or when the signal is especially strong. In communication terms, these experiences might be considered high-bandwidth downloads of divine information, compared to the ordinary trickle that one might get through conscience or subtle providential hints. Consider the metaphor of Wi-Fi: normally, our spiritual receiver might have one bar of signal – enough to sustain faith or a sense of meaning. But in a religious experience, it’s as if one is right next to the router: full bars, high signal-to-noise ratio, yielding a clear perception of the source. This aligns with how people describe these moments: illumination, revelation, a feeling of divine love flooding in, or hearing a “still small voice.” Such language is inherently informational (light, message, word). Theologically, many traditions affirm this model. The Judeo-Christian scriptures speak of God revealing Himself through visions and words (information content) to prophets. In Christianity, the Holy Spirit is said to “witness” to the human spirit – a direct communication that one is a child of God. In Eastern traditions, meditation and prayer are ways of quieting the mind (reducing internal noise) to receive the signal of enlightenment or divine presence.From a philosophical perspective, religious experiences under informational theology are data points where the divine reality becomes part of a human’s phenomenal data set. They are not random; they often occur in response to practices (prayer, meditation) that can be seen as attempts to connect to the source. This gives a coherent explanation for why, for example, prayer and meditation across cultures tend to yield feelings of peace, unity, or contact with a greater reality – these practices are effectively calibrating the receiver. Moreover, the informational view can account for both the commonalities and differences among religious experiences. The common core (sense of the transcendent, ineffable insights, moral renewal) reflects the one true source (God’s signal is ultimately one). The differences (Christians seeing Jesus, Hindus seeing Krishna, etc.) can be compared to how the same underlying signal might be interpreted differently depending on one’s “interface” or cultural/psychological schema – just as the same data file could display as text or images depending on software. Importantly, the transformative effect of genuine experiences – often converting a person to a life of virtue, compassion, and deep conviction – indicates that real information transfer has occurred. The person has “learned” or internalized something from beyond themselves, consistent with the claim that they encountered God.Objections and counterpoints: The skeptic will say that there’s no need to invoke a real divine signal; the brain is capable of generating these experiences internally. Neurotheology shows that stimulating certain neural pathways (e.g. via psychedelics or electromagnetic impulses) can trigger intense mystical feelings. Doesn’t that suggest it’s all in the brain? The informational paradigm, however, can incorporate this without negating authenticity. Just because we can force the radio to play static or even snippets by tinkering with its circuits doesn’t mean there are no genuine broadcasts. In fact, one might say the brain has specific structures that facilitate religious experience because evolving in a universe with God, such capabilities were advantageous (they allow connection to truth). Another objection is the diversity of religious claims – if God is the source, why the conflicting messages? As noted, the differences can be attributed to noise and filtering. God’s pure message (perhaps simply His being and love) is filtered through human categories, yielding various interpretations. Informational theology would predict that in the limit as noise approaches zero (say, at the highest mystical union), the messages converge. Indeed, mystics from different faiths often describe an ultimate reality that is remarkably similar in essence (an experience of unity, transcendence of time, ultimate love or light) even if theology later frames it in different doctrines. A naturalist might further argue that no information is actually received – that these experiences don’t convey new verifiable facts. In response, while it’s true the content is often ineffable or not empirically verifiable (“God is love” isn’t a lab experiment), the fruits of the experience (changed lives, occasionally veridical insights or healed addictions, etc.) serve as indirect evidence that something real was communicated. Under our model, religious experiences are not proof that every doctrinal detail a mystic reports is true, but they are exactly what we’d expect if God is an interactive informational agent who desires communion with creatures. They are personal, meaning-laden communications that strengthen the signal of God’s reality in the world.EvilOriginal perspective: The presence of Evil – particularly severe moral evil and intense suffering – was initially modeled as a negative evidential variable (counting against God’s existence) in the traditional problem-of-evil sense. After all, if a supremely good and powerful God exists, why do we observe so much wrongdoing, pain, and injustice in the world? However, our revised model neutralized the evidential weight of evil, considering theodicies (reasons God might allow evil) that can reconcile an all-good God with the observed data . Notably, a soul-making theodicy posits that some evils serve as challenges that bring about greater virtues or goods (courage, compassion, growth) that couldn’t exist without hardship . Skeptical theism adds that our knowledge is limited, so we shouldn’t be confident that apparent pointless evils really have no divine justification . In the broader conversation, while evil remains the strongest argument against classical theism, many philosophers find that it doesn’t outright refute God but calls for a deeper understanding of God’s purposes. Our model thus treated the Severity of Moral Evil as largely neutral after adjustments – effectively acknowledging that, yes, evil is a challenge, but perhaps not an insurmountable one for theism.Informational reinterpretation: Reframing evil in informational terms provides a novel lens: think of evil as noise in the system. If God is communicating goodness and order (the signal), evil represents distortions, corruptions, or absences of that information. This analogy builds on a long theological tradition, especially from St. Augustine, who defined evil not as a substantive thing in itself but as a privation of good – like darkness is the absence of light. In our terms, good is the intended data in the cosmos (the positive content from the divine sender), whereas evil is what happens when that data is lost, scrambled, or intentionally overwritten by malicious agents (creatures misusing free will). An example: consider a moral action as a message – a kind deed communicates love (a bit of God’s nature) into the world. A murder, by contrast, is like introducing gibberish or a malicious code that breaks part of the system of relationships. This resonates with Floridi and Sanders’ idea in information ethics that entropy (disorder) in the infosphere can be seen as “evil” . They propose laws such as “entropy ought not to be caused in the infosphere” – essentially paralleling moral prohibitions (don’t cause destruction). By conceptualizing evil as entropy or noise, we can make sense of why a good God might allow some of it: a communication system can still function with a certain noise threshold and often employs error-correcting codes to overcome noise. In the grand story, one could see divine providence and redemption as the error-correcting mechanism for the evils that occur.Consider natural evil (e.g. disasters, disease): these can be seen as the byproduct of a complex physical system running its course (the “code” of nature includes chaotic elements that sometimes yield harmful outcomes – analogous to thermal noise in a circuit). Moral evil (sins and cruelty) are more like deliberate tampering or hacking of the system by free agents. Why not prevent those? In information theory, maximizing signal often involves a trade-off with allowing a range of freedom in the channel. If God made a world with free beings (to enable genuine love and growth), He had to allow the possibility that some would introduce noise. The key is that God, as the master of information, can and does work to filter and compensate for the noise. The history of salvation in religious terms – God teaching, correcting, incarnating, sacrificing – can be seen as a sequence of signal boosts and error corrections to ensure the ultimate message (the triumph of good and the realization of divine love) isn’t lost. This perspective also sheds light on soul-making: encountering evil can be instructive information for us. For instance, suffering can transmit the knowledge of compassion or dependence on a higher strength; battling injustice can convey the values of courage and solidarity. In a way, grappling with noise can enhance our appreciation of the signal. As an old metaphor goes, one cannot appreciate a melody without contrast; in visual terms, the brightest light is seen in darkest night.Objections and counterpoints: Some may worry that calling the Holocaust or childhood cancer “noise” sounds dismissive – these are horrendously real pains, not mere abstract distortions. The analogy is not meant to trivialize suffering, but to classify it in the metaphysical schematic. The informational model must still confront the emotional weight: why would God allow so much noise that at times the signal seems almost drowned out in static? Traditional theodicies still apply. For example, if human moral growth is a key goal, then giving creatures the capacity to err (and even horrifically so) might be a necessary risk; the informational framework simply adds that God will ultimately retransmit and restore the lost data (in religious terms, heal and redeem every injustice – a promise found in eschatological doctrines of many faiths). Another objection: if God is omnipotent, couldn’t He design a noise-free channel? In theory, yes – but a noise-free channel with free participants is paradoxical, as true freedom includes the freedom to deviate (create entropy). Additionally, some noise might be logically inherent in any finite system (analogous to the Heisenberg uncertainty in physics – absolute precision/zero randomness is unattainable in creation). The encouraging aspect of the informational view is that it portrays God not as passively tolerating evil, but as actively communicating through and despite the noise. All major religions hold that good can come from evil – here, we say the meaning of the overall message can survive noise. In fact, error-correcting codes in telecommunication add redundancy and clever structure so that even if parts of a message are lost or corrupted, the original message can be reconstructed . One might analogize divine revelation, conscience, and miracles as redundant or fortified packets of information ensuring that, ultimately, God’s purposes aren’t thwarted by evil. Thus, while evil remains a profound challenge, under informational theology it transforms from an inexplicable black mark into something that can be conceptually managed: the divine signal of goodness is fundamental, evil is a contingent interference, and the system as a whole is engineered (we trust) such that the final output will reflect the input of Love, with noise eliminated or overcome in the end.Biological ComplexityOriginal perspective: The Biological Complexity variable addressed the intricate design-like features of life – from the information-rich sequences in DNA to the molecular machines in cells and the fine-tuned ecosystems in nature. Traditionally, such complexity has been cited as evidence of a designing intelligence (going back to William Paley’s watchmaker analogy). Modern proponents point to things like the digital code in DNA or the irreducible complexity of certain biomolecular systems as improbable to have arisen by unguided processes, thereby hinting at a Creator. In our probabilistic model, biological complexity was considered supportive of theism to the extent that life’s origin and development show signs of requiring input of information beyond what random mutation and natural laws would generate. We incorporated mainstream views – e.g. acknowledging evolution as a process that can produce complexity – but noted that the origin of life (a jump from chemistry to a self-replicating information system) remains a poorly understood leap, which some argue is more plausible with a guiding Mind. Objections, of course, are well-known: evolutionary biology explains apparent design without a designer, via natural selection acting on random variation over eons. DNA’s information content can increase through gene duplication and mutation, with selection filtering results – no supernatural infusion needed. Moreover, many “design” arguments have fallen to scientific explanation (the “God of the gaps” concern). Our model thus treated this evidence modestly, recognizing life’s complexity might be expected under God (who could choose to create via evolutionary or direct means) but is not unexpected under naturalism either, given enough time and the proven creative power of evolution in generating complexity.Informational reinterpretation: Framing biological complexity in informational terms is almost natural, since biology is literally awash in information. The genome is often described as a database or program: a human DNA has over 3 billion “letters” of a quaternary code (A, C, G, T) which, when decoded, produce a functioning human body. The informational theology approach would assert that this is not metaphor – it is reality: life is information at its core, and that information ultimately originates from the divine mind. As biologist Hubert Yockey put it after decades of applying information theory to biology, “Information is the difference between life and matter, between biology and physics. Information is the ultimate explanation of life. Information is the secret of life.” . Under this view, when we see the staggering complexity of a cell – effectively a tiny information-processing factory – we are seeing the imprint of God’s informational organizing principle manifest in the material realm. It is as if God’s Logos (rational word) has condensed into biochemical code. This perspective dovetails with the concept of divine ideas mentioned earlier: all species, all biological forms were ideas in the mind of God, and through creation (whether instant or evolutionary) those informational blueprints became instantiated in matter . So the biosphere is essentially a manifestation of the infosphere (to use Floridi’s term) shaped by God.One can use the analogy of software and hardware: The laws of physics and chemistry (hardware) provide the substrate, and God’s informational input is the software that makes the hardware run life’s program. Evolution by natural selection can be seen as a sort of algorithmic process that explores variations, but informational theology might suggest that even this process is guided or constrained by an underlying informational logic imparted by God. For example, the genetic code (the correspondence between DNA codons and amino acids) is a very specific, highly efficient encoding scheme – one could ask, why that code and not countless other possibilities? Some researchers note it has error-correcting properties and optimality that are surprising for a random development . Such observations can be interpreted as the fingerprints of an intelligent coder. Additionally, the origin of life problem – how the first self-replicating, information-bearing molecules arose – is essentially an information-origin problem. In an informational worldview, it would be natural to say that the entry of biological information into the world was an act of God communicating a new layer of order. Whether this happened miraculously or through laws we don’t yet understand, the key is that life carries specified information (a term often used to denote meaningful, functional information) which comes from the ultimate Specifier.Even staunch evolutionists like Richard Dawkins describe biology as studying things that appear “designed for a purpose” – they attribute it to natural selection, but the informational theist can retort that natural selection itself can be viewed as a tool pre-set by God to channel the flow of information from environment to genome. Thus, God could be the ultimate source of the information, with evolution as the transmission medium. This synthesis avoids a false dichotomy of creation vs. evolution by recognizing evolution as an information-processing algorithm in a system whose very capacity to generate complexity was endowed by an Informer. Importantly, information theory teaches that information cannot arise from nothing; it is transmitted or transformed but not magicked out of thin air. If the universe started with low information (just simple particles), whence the high information content in DNA and the biosphere? A purely closed system would struggle to explain this increase without invoking something like the sun’s energy driving local entropy reduction. But energy alone doesn’t guarantee specified complexity – you also need an algorithm or mechanism to harness that energy for building structures (like how a computer needs software to turn electricity into meaningful output). God’s design could act as that “software.”Objections and counterpoints: Evolutionary biology will counter that natural mechanisms suffice to explain complexity; invoking God’s information is unnecessary. And indeed, remarkable computer simulations (genetic algorithms) and studies in digital evolution show that information can increase in a system without an external programmer, provided the system is set up with an evolutionary rule-set. Our informational view can concede much of this – yes, the “rule-set” (natural selection operating on variation) generates complexity, but who set up the rule-set and the finely tuned conditions for it to work over billions of years? It loops back to fine-tuning and to metaphysical necessity: we still desire an ultimate explanation for why our universe is capable of evolving life at all. Another objection is that if God pre-loaded information into life, the evidence should be clearer (many biologists see no empirically detectable intervention and consider the complexity fully reducible to natural causes). However, the subtlety of the information injection could itself be part of the design – allowing a world that operates by regular laws and evolution (thus respecting the integrity of nature) while still being directed toward certain informational outcomes. Some proponents of theistic evolution articulate that God’s guidance is at the level of quantum indeterminacies or genetic mutations that are statistically undetectable but providentially guided. In our terms, God could be modulating the random mutations – essentially feeding bits of information into the evolutionary process at opportune moments, akin to nudging the algorithm toward certain solutions without breaking its overall random-seeming character. Finally, a secular perspective might argue that calling DNA “code” or “information” is just metaphor – molecules have no meaning by themselves. Yet as a matter of empirical fact, DNA does function exactly like a coded message system, with ribosomes acting like machines reading digital data to synthesize proteins. This is a striking observation that even non-theistic scientists describe in information language. Informational theology simply takes them at their word: it is a code, and codes come from coders. Therefore, the incredibly complex, information-dense phenomenon of life is best understood as part of the divine communication, with God as the author of the genetic text of life . Biology is thus theology in cipher, waiting to be recognized as such.Quantum IndicatorsOriginal perspective: Quantum Metaphysical Indicators encompassed those curious features of quantum physics that hint the universe is not a straightforward clockwork governed by local, materialistic laws. These include the observer-dependent nature of quantum states, the non-local correlations of entanglement, apparent randomness, and the way reality at small scales defies classical intuitions . In our model, this variable was admittedly abstract, but we noted that if the fabric of reality is mind-like or information-like (as some interpretations of quantum mechanics suggest), this could be evidence in favor of theism. For instance, the von Neumann-Wigner interpretation posited that consciousness causes collapse of the wavefunction, implying that mind is fundamental to making the universe’s state real . Moreover, physicists like Sir James Jeans and Arthur Eddington long ago remarked that the universe seems more “like a great thought than a great machine” . In our earlier discussions, we compiled thoughts: maybe a cosmic Consciousness (God) underlies quantum probabilities, or maybe the holistic interconnectedness observed (e.g. in entanglement) aligns with a sustainer deity “in whom all things hold together.” We gave this variable a small positive weight, recognizing these interpretations are speculative but symbolically pointing toward a universe where materialism is incomplete . A fully atheistic materialism might have expected a separable, observer-independent reality; quantum physics delivered the opposite. Objections are straightforward: physics is physics, and none of this requires God per se – one can stick with agnostic interpretations like many-worlds or decoherence that keep consciousness out, or simply say “weirdness isn’t divine, it’s just how nature is.”Informational reinterpretation: Quantum phenomena can be re-read under informational theology as strong hints that information and observation are foundational, even more so than physical objects. Wheeler’s “It from bit” once more becomes relevant: he conjectured that the universe fundamentally is made of information, with quantum events representing bits of yes/no choices . In fact, he suggested a “participatory anthropic principle” – that observership is required to bring the cosmos into its concrete form . If one takes that seriously, it is a small step to say the primordial observer is God – the ultimate knower who collapses the universal wavefunction, so to speak. Thus, quantum indeterminacy might be the stage where God’s knowledge and decisions interface with the world. When a quantum event “randomly” goes one way or another, perhaps it is not truly random but guided by divine providence injecting information at the subtlest level (this idea has been suggested by philosophers like Robert John Russell in theology of science contexts).Entanglement, where particles separated by vast distances appear to instantly affect each other’s states, defies classical locality. How to make sense of it? One way is to think of the universe’s information as non-local – reality is like an integrated network rather than isolated parts. This again resonates with a view of God as the one maintaining a consistent global information state. Some have poetically compared quantum non-locality to God’s omnipresence: everything is interconnected possibly because all things are rooted in the one mind of God that transcends space . Bernard d’Espagnat, a physicist, spoke of a “veiled reality” beyond the quantum phenomena, hinting at something holistic and perhaps mental underlying what we observe . This aligns with the idea that the world we see is a result of an underlying informational matrix.Quantum randomness is another aspect – the unpredictability in outcomes. A naturalist might shrug and accept genuine randomness; a theist might see an opportunity for free will or divine choice to operate. If physical determinism is broken at the quantum level, it opens a door for minds (human or divine) to influence outcomes without violating physical laws – by “choosing” specific branches of possibilities. Henry Stapp and others have proposed models where the mind can exploit quantum indeterminacies in the brain to exert top-down control. While speculative, it again underscores that information (the mind’s will) can affect matter if quantum mechanics permits wiggle room.The overall impression from quantum physics is that reality is less solid and more informational than previously thought. Particles are described by information (the wavefunction) which only yields definite classical properties upon measurement (information exchange). This is fertile ground for theology: “In the beginning was the Word (Logos)” could be analogized to “In the beginning was the Information,” and “the Word became flesh” could parallel the idea that the abstract quantum realm (the information) becomes concrete physical reality upon observation – the ultimate observer being God who made the Word flesh in a literal theological sense in Christianity, but in a metaphysical sense constantly makes the Logos manifest as the physical cosmos.Objections and counterpoints: It must be acknowledged that many physicists prefer interpretations of quantum mechanics that do not require consciousness or anything mystical – e.g. the many-worlds interpretation, where every outcome happens in a branching multiverse, or objective collapse models where some physical threshold triggers collapse. These arguably keep quantum physics within an impersonal frame. The informational theologian might respond that even in these interpretations, the mathematical formalism of quantum theory remains about knowledge and information (the state is a probability amplitude, essentially encoding what can be known about a system). As Anton Zeilinger noted, “whatever we do in science is based on information which we receive… reality is related to information” . Denying a role for consciousness doesn’t remove the whiff of information-centric reality. If many-worlds is true, it itself is a very extravagant information claim: that the universe’s full information content is an ever-splitting tree of possibilities – which one might say is unnecessarily complex unless one presupposes a kind of platonic information space. Ockham’s razor might favor a single world with an active observer influencing it (which could be God). Another objection is that tying God to quantum effects risks a “quantum God of the gaps” – future physics might explain these phenomena without any appeal to mind. Our aim, however, is not to say “quantum mechanics proves God,” but that it surprisingly coheres with the notion that mind and information are fundamental. It demolishes the old Newtonian view of matter-in-motion as all there is, which had been a cornerstone of atheistic materialism. In an age where physics tells us detached observers are fiction and that reality might be deeply unified and information-based, theism finds a friendlier conceptual environment. As Eugene Wigner remarked, “The miracle of the appropriateness of the language of mathematics to describe nature is a wonderful gift” – implying that the universe is unreasonably intelligible (why should human-understandable information – math – so perfectly map onto external reality?). The informational answer: because the same Logos that structured the universe structured our minds, allowing us to resonate with the informational matrix of creation. In sum, quantum indicators strengthen the case that reality is more than brute matter; it is permeated by something akin to mind or at least immaterial information . This is precisely what we would expect if the foundation of reality is the mind of God, the divine informational substrate.Metaphysical NecessityOriginal perspective: The Metaphysical Necessity variable captured arguments that there must exist a necessary being or ultimate ground of reality, which many identify as God. In classical philosophy, this is the cosmological argument from contingency: every contingent thing (which exists but could possibly not have) requires an explanation, and tracing the chain of explanations leads to something that exists necessarily (cannot not exist) . That necessary reality, the argument goes, is the source of everything else – and the qualities it must have (eternal, independent, uncaused, and sufficiently powerful to cause all else) overlap strongly with the God of classical theism. In our model, this was an evidential variable favoring God because the existence of anything at all (rather than nothing) and the contingency of the world point to a necessary foundation. If not God, one must posit the universe itself is necessary or that abstract principles are. But many philosophers (and even some scientists like Leibniz, Spinoza, etc.) have reasoned that an infinite regress of contingent explanations is unsatisfying; something fundamental must “just exist” in its own right, and God is a prime candidate . Objections include: why must the necessary thing be God (could be a quantum vacuum, laws of physics, etc.)? And if God is necessary, is that coherent (could God have not created, etc.)? Moreover, some challenge the principle of sufficient reason underlying this argument. Nonetheless, our model weighted this variable positively: a world with an appropriate necessary being (God) elegantly explains existence, whereas an atheistic worldview often ends up asserting brute facts (the universe “just is”), which some see as less satisfactory.Informational reinterpretation: Through the informational lens, the notion of a necessary being can be framed as the necessary source of all information. In any information system, you ultimately have a source (or multiple sources) of information. We ask: why is there something rather than nothing? Or why is there order and intelligibility rather than chaos? The informational answer is: because there is an eternal Information (capital I) that undergirds reality. This aligns with theological ideas of God as the Logos and the doctrine of divine aseity (God’s self-existence). Earlier we cited Augustine’s view: “All creaturely reality is ontologically grounded in the very mental content of God—nothing exists apart from his divine ideas instantiated by creation.” . That statement effectively claims God’s information is the necessary precondition for anything else. The created world is contingent – it could be otherwise, it could fail to be – which implies the information that defines the world is not self-sustaining. Just like a novel’s story requires the author’s mind to exist, the universe’s “story” (the total information of history and law) requires the divine mind to keep it. In more analytic terms, if we consider the set of all true facts (all information that is the case), that set has a structure and reason only if anchored by a fundamental fact that explains itself. Theism posits that fact is “God exists” – a necessary truth. Informationally, “God exists” can be seen as a self-generated bit that then generates all other bits.One might picture an infinite library of information (all truths). If there were no catalog or author, it would be an incoherent infinite regress of books referring to other books with no origin. By contrast, if there is an Author who necessarily exists, His mind contains the library’s plan and ensures no contradiction. Metaphysical necessity of God means God’s own “information” (nature, attributes) are uncaused and self-consistent. This is reminiscent of Gödel’s ontological argument in logic, where God is defined as a being that possesses all positive properties essentially – being of that sort either is impossible or necessary. In our parlance, God is the necessary information source – if such a source is even possible, it must exist (since a self-sufficient source could not depend on anything else to come into being).Another angle: The principle of sufficient reason (PSR) can be rephrased as “every piece of information has a source or explanation.” It leads to the conclusion of a final source that itself isn’t contingent on further information. That final source, the PSR argues, is God . Science implicitly operates on a form of PSR – we seek explanations (information about why) for phenomena, expecting that the universe is comprehensible. This expectation is borne out; we keep finding deeper layers of order (information). An infinite regress of explanations is basically an infinite regress of deferred information. At some point, the chain terminates in metaphysical information that is fundamental – such as the laws of logic, or the existence of something. Theism identifies that fundamental “law” or “fact” with an intellect: God’s existence is the foundational truth that accounts for itself (God is often described as ase – existing by the necessity of His own nature ). Therefore, God as the necessary being is like the master key of the informational world – the one bit of information that is unconditioned, generating all conditioned bits.Objections and counterpoints: A common atheistic retort is to question why the necessary thing must be personal or conscious – why not say the quantum vacuum or a multiverse exists necessarily? Or even the abstract platonic realm of mathematics exists necessarily and that’s enough? Here, informational unity plays a role. If mathematics (or some formal informational structure) is necessary, one still hasn’t explained why the concrete universe aligns with that structure so precisely (e.g., why does physical reality obey sophisticated mathematical laws that were “out there”?) . It’s more coherent, some argue, that the reason the world matches math is that both originate from a rational source (God’s mind). Purely impersonal necessary entities face the task of producing personal, contingent realities – it’s arguably easier for a necessary Mind to produce matter than for necessary matter to produce mind. Another objection is that perhaps nothing is truly necessary; maybe reality is just a brute fact. But brute factism is the antithesis of explanation – it’s like giving up on the quest for information. It undercuts the very rational enterprise. By contrast, asserting God as necessary transforms “brute fact” into “self-sufficient reason” – it says there is an ultimate reason, it’s just that it lies in God’s nature which by definition cannot not exist . That is not a cop-out; it’s a philosophical end-point that avoids contradiction.Critics might also claim that calling God the source of all information raises the problem of divine simplicity vs. complexity – if God contains the ideas for all creation, isn’t God an incredibly complex being? Wouldn’t that need explanation? Classical theology responds that God is actually metaphysically simple (not composed of parts or disparate data) – God’s knowledge is not bits of separate information like ours, but one single act of knowing that comprehends everything. In a sense, God’s complexity is an illusion from our perspective; in Himself, God is one perfect unity (one could say one “bit” that encodes infinite detail implicitly). This is admittedly a heavy concept, but it aligns with the idea that all multiplicity of information in the universe springs from a single unified source – a source whose essence is existence and intellect itself.In conclusion, reinterpreting metaphysical necessity in informational terms reinforces that something fundamental must exist to generate the world of contingent infos, and that something looks a lot like a mind: the divine mind as the necessary database in which the “data” of all possible and actual things resides eternally . Only such an infinite, self-existent information-source could fulfill the role of explaining everything without needing explanation beyond itself. This dovetails beautifully with millennia of theological reflection on God’s necessary being (as seen in Anselm’s and Aquinas’s reasoning) but now couched in the modern idiom of information.Informational Unity of EvidenceThroughout this chapter, we have reconceptualized eight diverse lines of evidence under a single unifying paradigm: the universe as a grand communication of information, with God as its ultimate source and meaning. This informational theological synthesis offers a coherent lens through which fine-tuning in physics, objective moral values, conscious minds, spiritual experiences, the reality of evil, the complexity of life, quantum phenomena, and the need for a necessary being all converge on a common insight – that reality is fundamentally intelligible and information-rich because it is grounded in a supreme Intelligence. What initially might appear as unrelated clues (cosmic constants, moral laws, etc.) now fit together like pieces of a jigsaw puzzle, revealing a picture of a cosmos suffused with informational order and purpose.Several themes emerged repeatedly, reinforcing the unity:* Logos and intelligibility: The fine-tuning of the universe and the unreasonable effectiveness of mathematics in describing nature both point to an underlying rational structure – the Logos . We saw that this rationality is not passive: it actively “chooses” specific life-permitting values (fine-tuning) and provides a lawlike framework we can discover. Informationally, it’s as if the cosmos was programmed to be understood by minds. This directly links to consciousness and moral truth – our minds can grasp mathematics and morality because they are formatted in the same language that our minds use. This would be an astonishing coincidence under atheism, but naturally follows if one Logos underlies both cosmos and nous (our reason) .* Signal and receiver: We cast humans (with our consciousness and capacity for religious experience) as receivers of divine information. Each category of evidence – moral law, mystical insight, even our perception of logical necessity – can be seen as signals we receive from the transcendent. The success of science is itself a signal reception: we tune into nature’s encoded regularities. The prevalence of religious experiences across history indicates many people, at various times, pick up at least a faint transmission of the divine signal. The concept of conscience as a “voice of God” aligns with this too. The unity here is that human beings are not isolated data-processors in a meaningless void, but rather explicitly designed to interface with the informational content God imbued in reality. This gives an integrated account of why we find meaning, pursue truth, and yearn for the transcendent – our very cognitive faculties are oriented toward receiving the ultimate information, i.e. knowing God .* Noise and free will: The presence of evil and suffering, which at first glance stands apart (even against) the others, found a place in this paradigm as well. By interpreting evil as noise or entropy in an information system, we integrated the problem of evil into the same signal-channel framework. It explains why our reception of the divine can be so imperfect or distorted – the channel (world) has interference, partly due to our own misuse of freedom (introducing noise). But crucially, an information system can be robust to noise. The unifying perspective sees God’s redemptive work (throughout history and in individual lives) as akin to error-correcting codes, ensuring that ultimately the message of love and goodness triumphs. It also ties into free will: the ability to deviate (create noise) is a design feature to allow genuine participation rather than forcing a one-way transmission. Thus even the dark threads of the world’s tapestry are woven into an informationally consistent narrative, one that promises resolution (in religious terms, a final restoration where the signal clarity is 100%). This harmonizes with the idea that all things will ultimately be reconciled or judged by the divine Logos – the corrupted information will be either fixed or quarantined, and the truth will shine forth.* Information as the common denominator: Each evidence variable ultimately boiled down to information. Fine-tuning and biological complexity explicitly involve information content (physical constants as bits, DNA as code). Moral objectivity and consciousness involve semantic and experiential information (meaning, qualia). Religious experiences are direct experiential information (a sense of God). Quantum indicators point to the primacy of information in physics (wavefunctions, observer knowledge). Metaphysical necessity speaks to the need for a self-existent information source to ground all contingent information. This remarkable convergence suggests that information is a fundamental category for understanding reality . As Floridi and others in the philosophy of information argue, the whole universe can be conceptualized as an infosphere . We have simply identified God with the origin and sustainer of that infosphere – effectively saying the “infosphere” is not impersonal, but the intellect of God in action. All evidences being explainable in terms of information is itself a unifying achievement: rather than juggling disparate explanations (a design argument here, a moral argument there, a consciousness dualism elsewhere), we have one explanatory principle (the informational character of divine creation) that covers them all.* Interdisciplinary harmony: An informational paradigm naturally bridges physics, philosophy, theology, and information theory – precisely the disciplines at play in this work. We saw Stoic and Christian theological ideas (Logos, divine ideas, aseity) resonating with cutting-edge physics and computing concepts (quantum information, Shannon entropy, code). This cross-disciplinary harmony lends credence to the approach: truth, if unified, should manifest across domains. By showing, for instance, that Augustine’s doctrine of divine ideas maps onto a modern notion of an informational cosmos , or that Wheeler’s quantum speculations echo ancient Logos theology , we demonstrate a consilience of knowledge. The evidences for God’s existence are no longer isolated arguments, but facets of a single jewel – the idea that God is the informative core of being.In light of this synthesis, one might revisit the logistic model for God’s existence not as a sum of independent probabilities, but as an integrated network of evidence. Each piece reinforces the others in a coherent worldview. Fine-tuning makes more sense if we also accept consciousness as fundamental (since a finely-tuned universe that produces minds fits a purpose); moral values being objective dovetails with a conscious, purposive Creator; quantum indeterminacy allows for conscious free will which in turn is necessary for moral responsibility – and around it goes, a virtuous circle of coherence. This holistic, informationally unified picture arguably raises the cumulative force of the evidence beyond what any one argument could do in isolation.Finally, it’s worth reflecting on an even deeper unification hinted by this approach: the union of being and meaning. In the classical view, physics dealt with being (what exists) and theology dealt with meaning (purpose, value). Information is the bridge between the two: information is at once something that exists (it has physical instantiations, whether in particle configurations or neural states) and something that means (it has content, message, function). By interpreting God as the foundation of information, we unify being and meaning at the ultimate level – the foundation of reality is not a cold atom or equation, but a meaningful Word. This fulfills in a modern key what the Gospel of John proclaimed: “In the beginning was the Logos (Word) … and the Logos was God… All things were made through Him.” The chapter we have written is, in a sense, an extended commentary on that idea: all the evidence we have examined are best seen as manifestations of one single Logos.In conclusion, the informational theological synthesis not only reinterprets each evidential variable in a compelling way, but it also shows them to be one tapestry. The fine-tuned physical constants, the moral law written on our hearts, the spark of mind inside us, the transcendent encounters, the allowance of struggle, the coded elegance of life, the bizarre quantum clues, and the demand for an ultimate ground – all these are threads woven by the same weaver. When viewed through the lens of information, they collectively illustrate a universe that is comprehensible, directed, and sustained by a conscious Source. This unifying paradigm significantly bolsters the rational case for theism: instead of eight separate arguments, we discern eight chapters of one argument – that reality behaves exactly as it would if it were the expression of an ultimate mind, an eternal Logos, who is communicating a meaningful existence to us. And that is perhaps the most profound evidence of all: that the world is not random data, but meaningful information – pointing to an Informer behind the information.References (Harvard Style)* Augustine (2002).
