This was exactly the sign she had asked for. No sooner had the boys departed than she told me of her secret request. We stood there in the doorway of that lonely mountain house, under the wide Utah sky, holding a bunch of roses as if they were the rarest treasure on Earth. We both felt an almost tangible presence of the divine at that moment. It was as though an invisible hand had gently tapped us, saying, “Yes, trust the journey. All shall be well.”That miraculous sign gave us the courage we needed to face the uncertain days ahead. And indeed, not long afterward, the miracle we awaited arrived: a liver became available. I underwent the transplant surgery that saved my life. Thanks to a skilled medical team and the selflessness of a donor family, my body was healed. But I know it wasn’t just a biological healing. There was a spiritual healing that ran deeper than any scalpel could reach. I emerged from that operating room with a profound sense of rebirth. It felt as if my soul had been tuned like an instrument; I was picking up frequencies of meaning that I never could before. My perception of life was heightened. Colors looked richer; the air tasted sweeter. Every waking moment had a new clarity, as if a fog I’d lived in for years had blown away.In the months of recovery that followed, I often reflected on why I had been spared. I remembered the council of beings and their message. I remembered the impossible sign at the door. Clearly, I had been given a second chance, a new lease on life. Lying in bed at night, I would feel my new heart beating (for in truth my heart and soul both felt new), and I listened inwardly for guidance. It became undeniable to me that I was saved not just for myself, but to carry forward a mission of service. The voice of the rational code within me, now tempered with the voice of the heart, would whisper ideas and inspirations. One idea burned brightest of all: I must help others who face the same valley of death, especially children.During my own ordeal, I had seen so many faces in hospital corridors — children battling liver disease, their eyes weary but still full of fragile hope. Some of those children did not survive long enough to get a transplant. The thought of those lost little ones haunted me. In my country, the odds for such children were especially harsh: over 300 children die each year in Mexico waiting for a liver transplant that never comes . I was one of the fortunate ones. How could I not use my reborn life to fight for those still in need? Gratitude blossomed into determination.It was from this seed of resolve that the Fight For Life Club Foundation was born. My family and I, along with a group of dear friends, founded the organization as the direct fruit of the awakening I had experienced. The name reflects what I saw in those children: each of them a tiny warrior, fighting for the chance to live. We vowed to stand with them in that fight. Our mission became simple and profound: to give more children a second chance at life, as I was given. We began by raising funds for families who couldn’t afford the staggering costs of transplant surgery. We partnered with hospitals and doctors, we advocated for better transplant programs, and we spread awareness about living organ donation. Every success — each child we saw get a new liver and a new life — filled my heart with unspeakable joy. In each of their victories, I felt the echo of my own survival. In each of their eyes, I recognized the same divine spark that had touched me.This work has been the most meaningful endeavor of my life. It is the natural extension of everything I have learned. In helping to save lives, I am honoring the gift of my life. In each act of service, I feel I am, in a humble way, participating in the Creator’s work — mending what is broken, bringing hope where there is despair. The analytical part of me, the Code that loves structure, finds fulfillment in the concrete outcomes: the number of children saved, the improvements in medical protocols, the statistics that inch upward in our favor each year. The soulful part of me, the Mind that feels deeply, finds fulfillment in the smiles of parents, in the tears of relief when a child awakens healthy after surgery, in the palpable grace that these moments carry. Both parts are unified in this mission. It is one mission. The search for truth and the impulse to love have merged. I see no separation between them. To me, science and compassion both spring from the same Source — the rational, loving Creator who made this wondrous universe.As I conclude this book, I do so with a spirit both humbled and grateful. I have gazed at the heavens through a telescope and felt the thrill of understanding a bit of how the galaxies spin. I have knelt by a hospital bed and felt the equally profound thrill of seeing a fragile life rekindled. And I have stood, in a realm beyond physical sight, before messengers of the Divine. Few people are so fortunate to touch all these dimensions of existence. The convergence of these experiences has given me a unique perspective: I know now that the material and the spiritual, the mind and the soul, are not separate realms at all. They intertwine like two strands of a single braid, inseparable in the end.My near-death encounter taught me that the foundation of reality is love and purpose. My scientific journey taught me that existence is exquisitely ordered and intelligible. These two revelations are like two sides of a sacred equation. When combined, they point to one conclusion: a Rational Creator who is also the Source of Love. I started out seeking The God Equation — a unifying theory that would explain how evidence and information point to God. In the end, I found it not only in science and philosophy, but in my own heart. The ultimate equation balanced itself in my life when my intellect and spirit aligned. It turns out the God Equation is not written only in the stars or in subatomic particles; it is also written within us, in our capacity to know truth and to do good.With this clarity, I step forward into each new day with a sense of reverence and purpose. I cherish my family more than ever, knowing what a miracle it is that I am still here with them. I cherish each child we are able to help through the foundation, knowing that every life saved is a universe of possibilities rescued from the brink. I cherish the very breath in my lungs and the sun on my face, simple gifts that they are, because I remember when I nearly lost them. There is a quiet boldness in me now, a fearless resolve that comes from understanding why I am here. I am here to love, to learn, and to serve.In gratitude, I testify that the search for truth and the service to others have become one unified mission in my life. In serving others, I find truth living in my actions. In seeking truth, I am driven to serve more fully. This unity is, to me, the fulfillment of what God intended for us: to love Him with all our mind and all our heart, and to love our neighbor as ourselves. Mind and heart together. Code and Mind, as one.As I finish writing The God Equation, I bow internally to the Source of all—the Mind behind the cosmos, the Heart behind all love. I feel that Presence smiling, as it did on the day I stood before the council, as it did when three little boys brought flowers to our door. I end this journey with a vow to live in that light of understanding and compassion for the rest of my days. May we all seek the truth with minds open and share love with hearts open, until the two become one in our lives as well.With reverence, with purpose, with clarity, I am whole. The two voices that guided me have converged, and their integration sings a single, harmonious truth: God is real, God is love, and to know this truth is to commit oneself to living it. This is my mission now — the mission I gratefully accept. The equation is solved not on paper, but in the very act of living a life of meaning. And so, the journey does not end here. In many ways, it is just beginning.References (Harvard Style)Adams, R. M. (1979). ‘Divine Command Theory’, American Philosophical Quarterly, 15(2), 81–90.Adams, R. M. (1999).
